T
he world is full of romantic sounds: rain tapping cobblestone, thunder rolling over mountains, surf crashing against shore, children laughing, steel wheels shrieking against tracks.
Agent 204 opened his eyes. His ears flooded with the rumbled of the train car. His body rested deep in the seat cushion. His senses were sharp.
He sat in the last row of the train car. The surrounding seats were mostly empty. He had no memory of boarding. No memory of where he'd come from.
The Agency had reset him. That meant one thing, a new mission.
Who he would be killing this time?
He studied his hands for clues. No scars. A callus curved around his trigger finger. The phantom recoil of a gunshot flickered in his mind. That was all he could access from before.
A small black backpack rested at his feet.
His phone buzzed.
Thumbprint unlock was pointless. He had no fingerprints. The Agency had stripped his identity, or he'd surrendered it. The truth didn't matter. His purpose was singular.
Kill bad people.
A wicked smile edged across his face. There were monsters in this world. Removing them brought satisfaction. Even without memory, he felt it, the quiet pleasure of extinguishing rot.
Three words appeared on the screen.
"Delta. Puppy. Coda."
He read them out loud and the screen unlocked. A message waited.
Agent 204,
Your mission is simple. Find this man and kill him. He was last seen at Sunset Boulevard and 4th Street near the metro station.
He is dangerous. His mind is fractured. Stop him before he harms anyone else.
Find him. Kill him. Disappear.
Method is irrelevant. Outcome is not.
Time is against you. The longer he remains in the wind, the harder he becomes to track.
This is not punishment.
It is
removal.
The Agency requires his death.
A photo is stored in your gallery. Note the web of scars around his right arm.
Money and equipment are in the backpack. You can reach me on this phone.
Good hunting.
Spade
204 zipped the phone away and opened the backpack.
Inside: a laptop, cables, stacks of cash, and a pistol.
SIG P320.
Compact. Smooth frame. No sharp edges to snag fabric. Perfect for concealment. The barrel was threaded for a suppressor. He found it nestled between the cash.
Three loaded magazines sat in the front pocket.
He closed the bag and opened the photo app.
There was one image. The target was mid-thirties. Brown hair. Clean-shaven. Sharp jawline. The kind of face you pass a thousand times without noticing.
The eyes were different.
Dead. Tired. Killer's eyes.
No name provided.
Los Angeles was an ant farm. Millions moving in slow currents. A hoodie and lowered head would make the target invisible.
The train stopped.
204 slung the backpack over his shoulder and stepped onto the underground platform. The metro station sat directly beneath Sunset and 4th.
He rode the escalator up.
Heat hit him.
His lungs resisted the air. This climate wasn't home. The sun baked the pavement so hard he felt it through his soles.
He scanned the area.
Three security cameras. A metro booth with an employee glued to his phone. Ticket kiosks lined the wall.
204 tapped on the glass.
The employee looked up. Thick glasses magnified his eyes. Name tag said his name was Dale. "Can I help you?"
"I need a favor, Dale." 204 nodded toward the cameras. "Where can I view yesterday's footage?"
"You a cop?"
"Let's say I am."
"I need ID."
204 slid two thousand dollars through the metal slot. "Let's skip the ID."
Dale pocketed the money and went back to his phone.
204 knocked again.
Nothing
.
He stepped back. Unzipped the bag. His fingers found the pistol. Familiar. Intimate.
He engaged the safety, gripped it by the barrel, and smashed the handle into the glass.
The window exploded.
Dale yelped as 204 reached through, grabbed him by the collar, and dragged him out. A shard of glass sliced Dale's leg. Blood pooled.
Dale screamed.
204 pressed the pistol barrel against his ear.
"I won't kill you. But I will hurt you if you don't do exactly what I say. Tell me how to access the footage."
"I'm bleeding!"
"Not arterial." 204 increased pressure. "Where is it?"
"Downtown! Security Operations Center! Central servers!"
204 released him and stood.
"See? That wasn't difficult."
Dale whimpered.
204 ran.
Ten blocks.
Distance first.
He flagged a yellow cab and slid into the back seat.
"Drive."
He pulled up the address for LA Metro's Security Operations Center and gave it to the driver.
As traffic crawled, a patrol car screamed past in the opposite direction. By the time they got his description, 204 would be gone.
He leaned back, listening to the driver's distorted techno thumping through cheap speakers as Hollywood glass and steel blurred by.
The hunt had begun.

A
gent 917 stayed up all night reading the ragged journal. He sat perched beneath the dim lightbulb and turned the pages.
I am more than a number. For years, I have been Agent 048. But I have a name. The name was taken from me, but now it's back. My name is Sofia Vega and I will have my vengeance, in this life or the next.
It is difficult to find a starting point. After my implant malfunctioned, the artificial walls of amnesia crumbled. Sometimes memories from the past trickle into my consciousness like droplets falling from a faucet, and sometimes the past hits like a fire hose, covering me in a deluge of memories that's impossible to sort. Even now, I can't figure out the order of all my memories. The Agency has stolen so much from me. They believe they are righteous. They believe they are carving evil from the Earth. And maybe in the beginning they were. But somewhere along the way, they became evil themselves.
The means no longer justify the ends.
My implant malfunctioned nine months ago. I was on a mission in Chicago. It was the dead of winter. An arms dealer named Brutus Cassius was flooding the streets with uzis and high-powered rifles. Gun violence reached an all-time high. The Agency discovered Brutus was on the FBI's payroll as an informant. He had complete immunity from the law, free to move weapons through the inner city while lining his pockets with more cash than he could spend in a lifetime. While the gangs killed each other and citizens hid from the crossfire, Brutus was living happily ever after in a penthouse, watching the chaos unfold below.
The people I worked for, the people at the Agency, wanted Brutus dead.
The Agency has a poetic way of doing things. They don't want targets to simply die. Shooting them with high-powered rifles from a safe distance or cutting their throats in their sleep is too subtle, too humane. The Agency weighs the magnitude of a target's crimes and then designs a punishment to send them to the afterlife in the most fitting way possible. Every mission is meticulously planned before an agent is deployed.
I don't know the exact count, but I believe I have killed over forty people during my time as an agent. Every day, I remember a new mission, a new life that I took. They were all bad people, but that much killing makes you
question where you are going to wind up in the afterlife. Is there a special place in hell for people like me?
Guess I'll find out when I get there.
Am I a good person? I'm not so sure. I have memories of watching bad people bleed out at my feet. I stand over them, gun in hand, and watch the sheer terror of death dominate their gaze as their lives end. It makes me feel all warm and fuzzy inside.
Chicago started out the same as every other mission.
Eyes opened while lying in a soft bed with no memory of who I was or how I got there. A cellphone on the side table waited for me. I read a string of random words into the speaker and it unlocked. Mission orders came in text messages. All I knew was that I needed to kill someone. After that I could start over, go into hibernation and drift into oblivion. I remember thinking only a terrible person who'd done terrible things would live a life like this.
No fingerprints, no past, no identity. I was gone from the world, a ghost in the machine with one purpose: to execute people.
My hair had been dyed platinum blonde. My roots were invisible, the styling freshly done. According to my mission directive, Brutus Cassius got his rocks off to platinum blondes with big busts and pear-shaped hips. It irked me that I was given this mission because I matched the arms dealer's sexual preferences, but I wouldn't let that take away from the satisfaction of killing him.
The Art Institute of Chicago was perched atop sharply cut steps. The museum was built of limestone. Brutalist brick and sharp columns spiraled around the building, giving it the feel of an ancient Roman structure adorned with modern construction. As I walked up the steps to the glass entry doors, a shiver tickled my spine. At the time, I chalked it up to nerves before a mission, but looking back I think it was my implant doing something to my brain. The unnatural waves countering memory produced a strange sensation, like a splash of cold water on the brain stem.
Brutus visited the Art Institute every Friday at 10am. The man's schedule ran like a finely tuned clock. He ran his gun empire like a Fortune 500 company. And like any overworked CEO, he needed time to decompress. He did this three ways: illicit drugs, beautiful women, and art.
I found Brutus in a large room near the center of the museum. Recessed ceiling lights splashed along the soft white walls. My heels clicked on the hardwood floor as I approached. He wore a heather gray suit, hands stuffed into his pockets, gazing up at a massive oil canvas. The painting depicted a weeping angel holding the dying son of God. The angel's feathered wings were painted with such haunting detail, I could practically hear the flutter of feathers.
The funny thing about big-shot, suit-wearing criminals is they like to think they are refined. They spend time at opera houses, walking through museums, getting lost in paintings while their blood money trickles through the city streets. They lie to themselves. They waltz through the halls of artistic intellectualism, trying to be more than thugs, trying to discover higher meaning
to this earthly existence inside the frame of a painting or the carved sculpture of a beautiful face.
The truth is, scum like Brutus exists for the same reason as the rest of us.
To feel.
In the end, a life is nothing but a series of feelings. Some emotional, some physical. All our efforts, everything we do, results in a feeling. And the feelings we make, the feelings we leave behind, and the feelings we make possible for others are what define us.
I was about to make Brutus feel a world of hurt.
Two bodyguards stood close by. As I neared, they stopped me. Brutus turned to see what was disturbing his peaceful art viewing.
I wore everything the Agency told me to. My coat was unbuttoned, revealing a shirt with a plunging neckline. A gold necklace fell across my chest and a tight skirt left little to the imagination. The stockings and high heels were uncomfortable, but they made Brutus look. I loved taking the phrase "dressed to kill" literally. I could practically feel the air heat up as he undressed me with his eyes.
He waved the guards off. The rest was easy. I sat next to him before the weeping angel and listened to him drone on about the brutal nature of the human condition. For a glorified thug, his thoughts were riddled with big ideas. I smiled and nodded while looking pretty. When he reached for my hand, I let him take it and pulled him close. I whispered in his ear that I wanted to get out of there.
He took me to his private, high-rise condo. Before letting me inside, one of his bodyguards patted me down. Once they were convinced I had no weapons, Brutus took me in. The condo was like something out of a movie. It overlooked the Chicago skyline and the view of glass skyscrapers was breathtaking. I slipped my heels off and kicked them aside. The plush carpet felt nice between my toes. I ran my fingers along the expensive white leather couch as I walked through the living room, taking in the scenery. There was so much extravagance it made my stomach turn.
Brutus wasted no time. He grabbed my arms and pinned me against a nearby wall. His breath was hot against my neck as his hands explored my body. My heart rate elevated with excitement. It wasn't attraction, it was adrenaline before the kill.
My skirt fell to the ground. Brutus tore my shirt off. I stood against the wall, exposed in lacy lingerie. My skin broke out in gooseflesh. Brutus took a step back to look at me. I faked a smile. As he lusted over my body, I reached behind my back and undid my bra strap.
I had no gun. I had no poison syringe. But I knew how to kill Brutus six ways from Sunday.
The strap came loose and I let my breasts fall into the open. Brutus reached and groped. I let him come close. He clasped his hands over my chest. I felt sick to my stomach but kept up the act. I threw my head back and moaned with false ecstasy as my fingers searched for the hidden trigger in my bra strap.
Just as I was
about to hit a breaking point, my finger found the small button in the strap. I gave it a flick and a hidden four-inch blade popped out from within the lacy fabric.
I grabbed the back of his hair with one hand and pulled. He smiled with lust, not seeing the blade at my thigh. With one fluid stroke, I shoved the blade straight into his neck. The knife point punctured his flesh with ease. Blood spattered from the wound, droplets trickling across my hand and splashing on my cheek. My tongue flicked out and licked a droplet from my lips.
White terror engulfed his eyes.
Brutus fell to his knees and clutched the wound in his neck, desperate to stop his lifeblood from dumping onto the floor. I yanked the blade out and blood fell in a torrent. He tried to call out to his guards, but the only sound he could make was a muffled gurgle as blood pooled in his lungs. I shoved him onto his back and stood over him, naked with a dripping knife hanging from my hand.
There was a macabre satisfaction watching this powerful man drown in his own blood in his multi-million dollar condo. This view, this condo was a symbol of his unquestionable power. And yet here he lay, helplessly dying at my feet. Oh how the mighty can fall.
I got to one knee and whispered, "When you see the devil, tell him I'll be seeing him soon."
The life vanished from his eyes. I didn't bother to close them. I left him cold and dead on the floor, frozen terror transfixed on the ceiling.
The Agency mission directive gave detailed instructions. Toward the back of the condo, a door led to a stairway which opened onto the roof. From there, a fire escape ran down to the street. Climbing down forty-six stories on a fire escape sounded daunting, but the alternative was the front door with two armed men standing outside.
I left the bra with the bloody knife sticking out of it on the counter. I have no fingerprints, no identity to hide. Besides, there was no way to retract the blade and I wasn't going to wear it with a knife sticking out.
Once dressed, I made my way up to the roof.
When I stepped out into the open air, wind bit at my face. They call Chicago the Windy City. It's nestled on the shores of Lake Michigan. I gripped the ledge and looked out over the blue water. I couldn't believe I was looking at a lake and not an ocean. Lake Michigan rippled in the wind. I willed my heartbeat to slow and breathed deeply as the adrenaline coursed through my veins.
Cool air tickled my stomach. I looked down and the lower buttons of my shirt had come undone. I ran my fingers over my stomach and felt a series of faded stretch marks. The kind a woman gets from nine months of carrying a baby. Who was I before all this? Before the Agency erased me? Could I have been a mother?
It was a random thought. I shoved it aside. Speculation was useless. My next memory reboot would eradicate any wandering thoughts about the past.
I made my
way down the fire escape. Climbing down forty-plus flights on the side of a skyscraper in high heels while the wind tries to throw you over the edge is as difficult as it sounds. With each step, the adrenaline subsided. The closer to the ground I got, the more peaceful I felt. Killing Brutus didn't weigh on my conscience. I was devoid of guilt. His death brought a sense of fulfillment, a sense of justice for all the innocent people his gun empire had destroyed.
I was the instrument of retribution and even though I would never receive praise, I felt good about the work I was doing.
My heel clicked on the concrete as I stepped off the fire escape onto the sidewalk. The street buzzed with pedestrians. It was midday during the work week. Professionals with bags slung over their shoulders and briefcases swinging at their hips moved quickly through the crosswalks. None paid attention to me as I pulled my coat tight around my chest.
I was on the outer edge of the Chicago Loop. Everywhere I looked, glass buildings towered into the sky. Trains rumbled on elevated tracks, weaving through the buildings like something out of a science fiction novel.
I wonder if I hadn't stopped to admire the city, what would have happened. Those few moments, standing still on the sidewalk as the world turned around me, changed the course of my life. Did some higher power command me to stand still so I could be caught? Did the hand of destiny force its will?
More likely it was random chaos. I doubt the universe cares enough about any one person to single them out.
The entrance to Brutus's building was ten feet to my left. A black car was parked out front. Three imposing men stood on the sidewalk. One of them answered a phone. His eyes went wide and he scanned the passing crowd.
We locked eyes. In that fleeting moment we had an unspoken understanding. I had killed Brutus. I had killed his boss. And he knew it.
The guy was fast, I'll give him that. In the blink of an eye there was a pistol in his hand. I turned to run, but before I could take a step the bodyguard squeezed off a shot. I remember thinking how reckless, how bold these men must be to fire a bullet in broad daylight on a Chicago sidewalk. The bullet struck me in the chest. The impact felt like the angry clenched fist of God coming down on me.
I fell to the ground.
The city broke out in pandemonium as people scrambled to get away. My attacker didn't stick around. Neither did his friends. They jumped in their car and drove off, leaving me to bleed out on the dirty sidewalk like a dog.
I didn't know it at the time, but my memory was about to be unlocked.

A
gent 204 stepped out of the cab in front of the LA Metro Security Center. His backpack weighed heavily on his shoulder. The pistol and silencer tucked inside cried out to him, begging to be used. Agent 204 pictured his target in his mind's eye. The target had a military look to him, the eyes in the picture were focused but filled with hidden rage. The same rage burned inside 204, the rage of a killer.
To get into the Security Center, employees scanned a security card, their bags were searched, and they had to pass through a metal detector. The entrance gates looked like airport security.
Walking through the front doors wasn't an option. On his way over, he'd used the Agency cellphone to ask why they couldn't hack into the Security Center and pull the footage they needed. The distorted voice on the other end of the line explained the LA Metro Security Mainframe was a closed-loop system. It wasn't linked to Wi-Fi like a normal business or household. All the servers housing security footage operated in a fully self-contained and autonomous network that could only be accessed directly through computers within the Metro Security Center. There was no hidden digital backdoor. 204 would have to lift the footage manually.
This place wasn't the NSA or CIA. Breaking in wouldn't be easy, but it wasn't impossible. The security guards weren't vigilant and lived off a steady diet of bagels and donuts. They hoped their existence, not their professionalism, deterred any real criminal from trying to enter.
204 waited on the sidewalk until lunch time. Employees exited the building. He spotted a man that looked his size. The employee was wearing a cheap suit and walked over to a parking structure across the street. 204 followed.
The elevators were ahead. The man pressed the down arrow and waited. 204 stepped next to him and smiled.
"Long day?" 204 asked.
"It's just about over for me. My shift started at 4 in the morning. I can't wait to be off, go home, kick my feet up and watch ESPN until I pass out. The NBA finals are boring this year, but I feel like it's a sin to not watch, you know what I mean?"
The elevator doors opened. Both men stepped inside
.
"Basketball isn't what it used to be," 204 said. "Too many rules now. In the 90's, when MJ was playing, the game was physical and took true creative prowess to dominate. Now the game is full of airy-fairy ballerinas who whine about a foul being miscalled when a stray finger from a defender brushed against the back of their hand."
"Amen to that. But a cool beer on a hot LA day tastes just as good today as it did in the 90's."
The doors opened. 204 shot a glance up toward the security camera in the corner of the elevator. He let the man exit first. They were four levels below ground and alone. 204 let the doors slide shut behind him. They were alone in the sub-levels of the parking lot with a handful of cars.
"I didn't catch your name," the man said.
"That's because I didn't give it to you." 204's voice dropped from the bouncy sports fan in the elevator to a guttural growl.
The man's eyes filled with panic. He knew something was wrong, a sixth sense had kicked in. His fight or flight mechanisms went into high gear. He couldn't explain it, but he knew the man in front of him meant him harm. Before he could react, 204's hands were on his throat.
204 kicked the man in the knee and dropped him. There was no need to kill him. The Agency would be displeased if he knocked off an innocent civilian on his mission. 204 shifted his grip on the man's neck and instead of sending him to the afterlife, put him in a sleeper hold. Within seconds, the man was asleep.
204 fished for the man's car keys. He found the fob and pressed unlock. A generic sedan chirped two rows down. 204 stuffed the man in the back seat and stole his clothes. The man's security card bore the name "Simon Charles."
Before leaving, he tossed the car keys on Simon's chest. 204 estimated he had an hour at most before Simon woke up and called the authorities. 204 took Simon's phone and crushed it under his heel, hoping that would buy him more time.
He checked his appearance in the reflection of the driver's side window and pulled the tie taut. The security card was attached to a lanyard which he dropped over his neck. Simon's hair was blonde. 204's was brown. Hopefully no one bothered to look at the profile picture on the security card.
Before entering the building, 204 stashed his backpack in some bushes across the street. He could slide through security passing as an employee, but the weapons in his bag would set off the metal detectors. He didn't anticipate further violence ahead and prayed some LA homeless person didn't find his bag.
He made it through security without a hitch. Security was too busy laughing over some Instagram Reel of fat people falling to pay him any attention. All he had to do was tap his security card against a scanner at the kiosk, walk through a metal detector, and he was in like Flynn
.
Earlier, when he called the only number in the Agency-issued cellphone, a digitally masked voice answered. The distortion made it impossible to tell if there was a man or woman on the other end of the line. 204 hoped it was an attractive woman. The thought of a bombshell in a tight-fitting suit giving him orders excited him.
The voice explained, "Keep your footprint minimal. Getting in and out without leaving any trace is unlikely. I didn't have time to plan this mission out with my normal attention to detail, otherwise I would have provided a security card and identity you could use. You'll have to steal one. Be discreet. Once inside, take an elevator down to the basement. There is a server room that houses all the footage from security cameras of every Metro station and bus stop throughout LA. This is not a highly classified government server. All you need to do is plug the thumb drive you were provided with into a terminal and I'll have remote access to the whole system. A stolen security card should grant access to everything you need."
204 took the elevator to the basement. Computer servers housed in metal racks lined the walls. Along each row, a computer terminal accessed the servers. The place looked like a low-budget 90's movie set. The city wasn't chomping at the bit to spend its inflated budget on upgrading storage for LA Metro security camera footage. 204 picked a random terminal. There was a scanner to the right of the screen and he was prompted to tap his security card. The scanner beeped and he was in the system. Once inside, he plugged in the thumb drive. The Agency now had full remote access to the servers.
He took the elevator back to the lobby, exited the building, and made his way to where his bag was stashed. Everything was as he left it, tucked in the shadows behind a bush. He took off the blazer and dropped it and the security card on the ground. As he turned to leave, police cars with flashing lights pulled up in front of the building. Cops jumped out and scurried up the steps to the front door.
Simon Charles must have woken up. It was time to leave. He kept his head low and hopped on the first bus he could find. His instructions were to lay low until contacted. The Agency would need time to sift through footage and find a trail on his target.
As the bus meandered through the city, 204 tried to think back to earlier missions. He knew this was futile. His accessible memory only stretched back a few hours, but even with no recollection of past missions he got the sense that this one was different. The Agency appeared to have unlimited resources. With resources like that, his missions were likely meticulously planned. Even the distorted voice he'd spoken to earlier confirmed they didn't have time to sift through every detail on this one.
204 didn't know if he was going after a known rapist or serial killer. It didn't matter who he was chasing. It was all the same to him. Once he put a bullet into his target's head he could go back to sleep and wake in some rundown hotel with new mission instructions
.
This wasn't exactly the American Dream, but it sure as hell beat putting on a shirt and tie and getting micromanaged from 9 to 5 every day to barely make ends meet.

S
pade opened the footage.
The image was grainy, but she could see 917 pull up on the curb and ditch his car. 917 approached a kiosk, bought a ticket and stepped onto the first bus that arrived. She zoomed in on the license plate and line-number. LA's bus lines were numbered 1-99. This was the 34 line which ran straight through Hollywood. Once she pulled the plate, she was able to map out the bus's route.
917 would not have stayed on the same bus. He would have gotten off and on several buses in an attempt to hide where he was going. The LA Metro's Security cameras were now fully at her disposal. It took her four hours to trace 917's route.
She was right. After scrubbing through hours of footage, making educated guesses as to what cameras to check, she finally tracked 917 to a Greyhound station. There was a small security camera housed in a lamppost of the Greyhound station parking lot. She caught which bus he boarded and opened a new window.
Breaking into Greyhound's secure service was child's play. Spade had hacked into the most sophisticated government computer systems on the planet. There was virtually no firewall, no cyber defense network that she couldn't penetrate. Once inside, she was able to track the Greyhound bus in real time. A few hours earlier, the bus had made a pit stop in Bakersfield. It had since left and was halfway to San Francisco. Security footage showed 917 stepping off the bus. He didn't get back on before the bus pulled away.
204 was in LA still. It would take him a few hours to get to Bakersfield. A second agent would be touching down at LAX within the hour. She couldn't wait for him. 204 needed to get to Bakersfield before 917 left. If 917 left, she might not ever find him again.
Spade picked up her encrypted phone and fired off a text message.
Get to Bakersfield. Target likely in motel near the Bakersfield Greyhound station. Rent a car. More directions to come. Acknowledge.
A moment passed and a response came through.
Acknowledged. On my way
.
Right after she got the response, the phone rang. The shrill chirp made her jump. She set the phone down and pressed the button on the side of her headset.
"I'm doing everything I can, Robert."
The Director breathed deeply on the other end of the line. He didn't respond and waited for her to continue. She was the only one in the Agency that wasn't scared when he called and it irked him to no end. She was his only friend. He prayed she would never force him to choose between her and the Agency. He was a man of duty. And if duty called, he would answer unquestionably even if that meant burning everything he loved to the ground.
"Robert?" Spade asked, unsure why he wasn't responding.
"I'm not calling for a progress report on the mission. I'm calling to make sure your conviction isn't lacking. The Board isn't convinced you are totally loyal to the cause on this one."
"Fuck you and fuck The Board. I haven't slept in nearly 60 hours. My eyeballs are so dry I can barely see. I'm pretty sure there is more caffeine pumping through my veins than water and I am doing everything I can to get the situation under control. I don't need to play politics with you and have my loyalties questioned when I am up shit's creek without a paddle!"
The Director was unfazed by the outburst. "Agent 917 is your responsibility. I know you can clean this up and put him down before more damage is done. Your skills, intelligence and ability are unmatched. With the full power of the Agency resources, 917 should be dead within hours. The only reason 917 could escape us is if you have doubts. So I'm calling to make sure you don't have any. Because if your conviction and loyalty are intact, success is guaranteed."
Spade dropped her glasses on the keyboard and rubbed her eyes. Her body was shutting down from exhaustion. An exhausted mind could make mistakes. She could not afford to make mistakes. "Listen to me very carefully, Robert. 917 gave his life to the Agency. He willingly handed his life over and submitted to the memory wipes. The implant would never take if he didn't choose this. Why he is running now is beyond me. I've fixed him before. I don't think I can fix him again. A mind can only take so much before something breaks. It pains me to say this, but he needs to die. And I need to be the one to make sure it happens."
"Good. That's what I wanted to hear. When I hear from you next, I expect confirmation that Agent 917 is dead."
The Director hung up.
Spade resisted the urge to throw everything off her desk to the floor. She didn't have another spare keyboard after she broke the last one. Besides, the last time she destroyed her keyboard it didn't bring her any relief. It was like throwing gasoline on the fire. Losing her temper only fanned the flames burning inside her.
She signed up to work for the Agency to try and effect change. The wheels of the justice system were rusted, broken, slow and corrupt. It
was only natural that private industry would step in when government channels became antiquated. How much business did the United States Postal Service lose to FedEx and UPS? NASA had already fallen a decade behind SpaceX and corporate space programs. The old systems were eroding. The new were moving in. The Agency was part of the natural course of evolution. And Spade was at the forefront, ushering in a new era of justice for the unheard voices, the oppressed victims of rapists, serial killers and criminals who unleashed their darkness across the world unchecked by the government systems elected to protect us.
But now, the Agency had a crack in it. 917 was rogue. Sofia Vega had stepped out of line and murdered two agents. Spade could bandage the wounds, stop the bleeding, but these recent catastrophes were only symptoms of a deeper problem. Even before 917's recent recalcitrance, he'd shown signs of defiance. His natural self-determinism was pushing back against the artificial amnesia. Whoever he was, buried beneath the electronic memory wipes, was clawing its way to the surface.
Spade knew she could repair the cracks in the Agency. But the root problem was human nature. Whoever her agents were beneath the memory wipes never really died. The real person was always there, sitting beneath the thin layer of amnesia.
She could kill Agent 917. Then what? Business went on as usual until the next agent's memory blockers failed?
A migraine pulsed like a foreign heartbeat in her brain. Her coffee pot was empty. 204 would be in Bakersfield in two hours. She set an alert on her computer to chirp when 204's internal GPS was within 20 minutes of the Bakersfield Greyhound station. For now, there was nothing to do but wait.
Stealing two hours of sleep was the best way to spend the time. Spade was so exhausted, she doubted she would have any dreams.
She was wrong.
Spade dreamt of a great void. She stood in the center of endless black that stretched out into forever. Ache pulsed in her thighs. She wiggled her toes and stood on one leg. Dreams where her legs worked were the worst. When she started walking, the facade of reality vanished and she resisted waking up. Waking up meant fried nerves and a wheelchair.
She walked into the infinite black. It felt as if she weren't moving at all. There were no buildings, trees or landmarks to mark her progress. Nothing got closer as she walked because there was nothing to get closer to. There was only the void.
And then, something terrible manifested in the velvet night. Dripping tentacles materialized before her. Her legs stopped working. She was paralyzed as the tentacles moved closer. Suction cups dripped with menacing goo. The tentacles multiplied. Over a dozen of them bloomed and encircled the space around her. Whatever creature these tentacles were attached to was still hiding in the inky black dark
.
She closed her eyes. Wet, slithery rubber tickled her skin and the tentacles hugged her. Squishy slurps popped in the air as the goo trickling from the suckers fell on her skin. Her heart pounded. Her breathing quickened.
It's a dream. It's a dream. It's a dream. It's a dream.
Her self-assurance that it was all in her head did nothing to calm her down. Terror manifested like a wet blanket and wrapped itself around her mind, squeezing tighter and tighter with each passing second.
Laughter boomed.

A tongue flicked out of the beak and licked her cheek.
For two hours, she dreamt of being eaten alive as those dark tentacles squeezed the life out of her.

I
woke up in a hospital bed. There was a giant bandage on my chest and every breath was agony. The doctors said it was a miracle I was still alive. I was lucky the gunman used a small, .22 caliber bullet. If he'd used something like a .45, it would have torn my heart to pieces. The .22 struck the breastplate over my heart. Bone shattered. The bullet bent and warped. A small piece broke off and cut into my heart. The fragment clogged a main valve. My heartbeat became irregular, picking up too much speed and then slowing to an impossibly low rate.
An ambulance arrived on the scene within twenty minutes after I was shot. 37 people called 911. Makes you think not everyone in the world is an evil bastard.
I had blacked out from pain and blood loss well before the ambulance arrived. I was rushed into emergency surgery. By the time they cut me open, my heart was failing. The bullet fragment was visibly sticking out of my heart. They yanked it out and my heart beat froze.
Clinically, I was dead for eight minutes. I went into cardiac arrest. A less skilled surgeon would have let me die. Call it divine intervention. Call it the chaos of the universe. Call it dumb luck. Whatever it was, I had a radically brave and intelligent surgeon working on me. After this is all over, I'll track him down and send him a nice fruitcake. It's the least I can do to repay him for saving my life.
When a heartbeat stops, the body will die within ten minutes. The surgeon fixed me in eight. He removed the bullet fragment from my heart, stitched the resultant hole closed, took two tiny electrical paddles and shocked my heart back into a steady rhythm. My vitals came back online and within minutes I was out of danger. But during those eight minutes with no heartbeat, the acid in my implant triggered as it was programmed to do if my heart stopped. The implant liquified itself and dissolved into my body. And once that implant was gone, the electrical impulses it emitted to cancel out memories ceased.
The artificial amnesia was gone, but I wouldn't figure this out until four days later.
When I woke up, a detective came in to question me. They found Brutus Cassius dead in his apartment. He'd been stabbed to death. The murder weapon was a bra with a hidden knife sticking out of it. The detective pointed out that my bust size would fit snugly into the bra found at the scene of the
crime. There were no prints in Brutus's condo and there were no prints on my fingers. Several witnesses claimed I was walking innocently down the sidewalk out front when an armed man gunned me down.
The detective urged me to talk. He said he could help me. I asked him if I was in trouble. He said I wasn't, any evidence he had that I was involved was circumstantial and there wasn't enough to make a case that I'd murdered Brutus. That was the beauty of having no fingerprints, I never had to worry about leaving a trace of myself behind unless my blood was spilt.
The detective didn't pry too hard. The city was better off without the criminal gun running war lord and he was happy to see Brutus go. When we were done talking, he asked me if there was someone he should call to let them know I was okay. My first thoughts strayed to the Agency, but I wasn't going to tell him that.
The hospital staff were polite, but they tip-toed around me like I was some kind of demon. I had no name, no prints and whenever they pressed me for information, I told them I couldn't remember anything.
When I woke up from the initial surgery, all I could think about was the mission, fleeing the hospital and getting picked up by the Agency before this got any messier. I spent the next four days stuck in bed recovering. I would have left earlier but after you take a bullet to the heart, moving around isn't so easy.
And then, I began to remember.
The smallest and most innocent thing triggered it. A young nurse came into my room and set a tray of food down near the bed. I sat up and looked at the steaming hot plate. Nestled in between a baked potato and juicy meat loaf was a scoop of green peas.
As I stared down at those peas, I heard the faint sound of children laughing. I was alone in the room. The TV was off. The windows were thick enough to drown the city noises out. I knew the laughter was coming from inside, coming from a phantom in the past. I closed my eyes and tried to push the sound back down into the depths of amnesia. I had no intention of remembering and I trusted the implant to do its job and cancel out the memory with an electronic wave.
Instead of lessening, the laughter intensified. A picture formed.
I was sitting at a picnic table in a park. A little girl, no older than five, sat giggling across from me. The sun beamed down from the sky. Leaves rustled. Children laughed and chased each other in the background. A smiling Labrador ran away from them as their young legs scampered after him. The dog turned and wrestled a stick out of a boy's hands. The children cheered, the dog ran, the sunlight bathed everything in magnificent light. It was like a scene from a Norman Rockwell painting. Everything happy, innocent and covered with a thin, magical layer of nostalgia that would never fade away.
But I was locked on the little girl sitting at the picnic table. There was a plate of food in front of her and on it, there was a pile of peas. She was picking peas up one at a time, placing them on the table and flinging them with her plastic knife. She'd bend the knife back with her fingers, and when she
let it go the momentum smacked peas into the air. Her brow furrowed with concentration. Her tongue peeked out of the corner of her mouth as she aimed, then fired. The pea went flying and struck me in the forehead.
She erupted with laughter. And that laughter was the most joyous sound I had ever heard. I didn't want it to stop. I found myself wishing I could capture that sound in an empty jar and carry it with me for the rest of my life. If I ever needed a pick me up, I could crack open that jar and listen to the innocent giggle of a little girl shooting peas.
And it was then that I realized this was a memory and not some lucid daydream. I knew because the feelings I was experiencing felt too real, too genuine to be nothing but imagination. If you hit me with those peas one more time I might have to come over there and tickle you until you pee your pants!" My voice sounded different. It was unmistakably my voice, but there was no edge of killer instinct in between the words. My voice sounded soft and full of love.
The memory plummeted back down into the depths of my mind. Everything went pitch black and I watched that sweet girl's face wink away like a light being turned off. I opened my eyes and looked around the hospital room. The sound of children laughing was gone. The only noise was my own breathing and the electric hum of the heart monitor I was hooked up to.
As I sat there, I knew the memory was real. And I knew that Josie was my daughter. And I knew that I would never forget her again.
And I knew The Agency had taken her from me.

aniel Chafer rubbed his eyes. He'd been manning the front desk of the motel for over twelve hours. Boss paid overtime, so he didn't mind. In the dead of night hardly anyone came in, so Daniel would lean back in his chair and catch some sleep. The electronic bell over the door chimed when someone wandered in and he would snap awake, ready as ever to check in a guest at Bakersfield's very own 2 star motel that boasted wifi, cable and a pool with cracks in the bottom.
The strange man stepped into the lobby close to midnight. Daniel was on the verge of dozing off, but the bell disrupted his cat nap. The man paid him a wad of cash for a room and bribed him to keep his presence in the hotel a secret. The man promised to leave the following day.
Easiest cash Daniel had ever made.
The next morning, as the sun rose over the horizon, Daniel rubbed the sleep out of his eyes and thought of the stranger hiding in the motel. The guy was a drifter. He didn't seem threatening, but the way he carried himself screamed violence. You could always tell when a guy knew how to fight by the way he walked and talked.
Daniel couldn't help but smile when he felt the weight of the rolled up cash in his pocket. He'd been working overtime, pulling all nighters and saving up cash for a new car. There was a slick Toyota Tacoma with 80,000 miles on it that his neighbor was selling. Daniel begged hold the truck until he could come up with the fifteen grand to buy it. They shook on it. And here in farm country, a handshake between neighbors was a legally binding contract. The truck was as good as his.
The stranger's cash put him within spitting distance of buying that truck. Couple more weeks of long, overnight shifts and he'd have the cash. Dreams really did come true.
He stepped away from the desk and went into the staff kitchen where there was a pot of steaming hot coffee waiting for him. The coffee was stale and had been on the burner for hours, but he wasn't drinking it for the taste. He needed the chemical reaction that caffeine created in his brain so he could stay awake three more hours until the next schmuck could take over the front desk.
When Daniel came back to the front desk, there was a man standing in the lobby. The man's hair was buzzed short at the sides and long on top.
The military fade caused Daniel to gulp. His stomach tightened as a terrible thought dawned on him.
This man is here for the stranger. This is what I was paid for, to keep the stranger hidden from guys like this.
Daniel sipped his coffee and choked a little as it burned his mouth.
"How can I help you?" Daniel did his best to sound casual and shoved the nervous jitters into a deep place in his stomach. "Need a room?"
Daniel knew the answer before the man answered.
"No. I'm looking for someone."
"Are you a cop or something?" Daniel sipped the coffee again. He looked over the top of the desk and spotted his coaster. Boss was sick and tired of the mug stains on the desk and demanded coasters. Boss got what he wanted most of the time. All his employees were burnouts like Daniel or illegal immigrants that worked for less than minimum wage so no one ever stood up to his ornery mood.
Daniel set the coffee down on the coaster, pressed both his palms on the desk, smiled and waited for the man to answer. On the outside, Daniel was calm and cheerful. On the inside, his heart was going as fast as a hummingbird's.
"Not a cop. But I'm someone who it is in your best interest to help." The man pulled out his phone and showed Daniel a picture. It was a picture of the stranger who arrived the night before and paid Daniel to keep his whereabouts a secret. "This man staying here?"
Daniel's eyes betrayed him. They went wide as he looked at the picture. He tried to play it cool and sip his coffee. "Never seen him," he said. The man didn't believe him.
"We can do this one of two ways. I can reach behind my back, pull out my gun, threaten you and shoot you if you don't talk or you can just tell me where he is. And if you cooperate, I'll give you a thousand dollars. Choice is yours."
Daniel thought of the Toyota. A thousand dollars might do it. He tried to add up all the cash he had stashed in his closet in his head. He hadn't counted in a while, but he was so close. A thousand dollars plus the cash he got last night might be enough.
But Daniel also prided himself on not being a sell out. It was part of his code. In high school, his best friend Ivan got busted by the police at a party. Ivan had enough drugs on him to get ten people stoned for a week. He was holding onto the drugs for Daniel and all of his friends. Ivan stayed tight-lipped, didn't rat any of his friends out and Daniel was off the hook. Now, it was Daniel's turn to keep his mouth shut. The universe was testing his resolve and Daniel was determined not to fail. If Ivan did hard time for him, the least he could do was play dumb for a few minutes.
"Look, pal." Daniel's voice broke. All the toughness he'd mustered before speaking was gone and his voice cracked as if he were going through puberty all over again. "I already told you, I've never seen him. I couldn't help you even if I wanted to, now get the hell out of here.
"
The man tightened his jaw. Daniel thought he could hear the man's teeth grind. "Remember, this was your choice."

gent 917 paused and closed the journal, keeping his index finger between the pages to save his place.

Are there only five senses?
Sight, taste, touch, hearing and smell…seems like a limited view of existence. Can all perception be defined as a blend of these five senses? Is there more? Is there something else, something beyond bodily sensation?
When you are alone in your living room, thinking of an old friend and suddenly they call you on the telephone are you tapping into a precognitive ability? When you are deep in conversation and pick up on the thoughts of the person you are talking to just before they speak their mind, did your ears listen to their mental echoes? When there is a tingle on the back of your neck before something terrible happens, is that an extra sense, a spiritual detection of danger?
How does science explain the awareness a parent has of their child? Why do mothers get pangs in the chest when their children suffer? Why do fathers sense dread when their offspring get hurt? How are these connections biologically possible?
It is beyond chemical. There is more than electrical impulses firing off in the neurons of the brain. There is something beyond the material at work, a kind of telepathy. There is a life force, a sense of energy that pulls our attention as if destiny is tugging at invisible puppet strings that we can only vaguely feel but can't see
.

This knowledge, this utter certainty that this girl is his daughter does not stem from his senses. It's not like touching a hot pan and having the burning skin signal the brain it's hot. 917 understood what Sofia felt when she gazed upon her daughter Josie in her mind's eye. A parent always knows. A parent can always sense their child.
A pang of remorse for killing Sofia bubbled in his chest. What was she trying to do? She had set him free. He wasn't the first. There were two other agents she'd "unplugged" from amnesia. Shortly after freeing them, she killed them both.

rom the journal of Sofia Vega.
A stranger shot me. The bullet tore through my chest like an unholy storm. My heart stopped. The implant in my head destroyed itself. I was born again. The Agency washed its hands of me.
I didn't stay in the hospital long. I had to get out. My body wanted to rest, to bury itself deep in the folds of the bed and until my wounds were healed. But my soul yearned for the truth. And I knew the truth was out there waiting for me. I would go mad if I spent the next few weeks lying in a hospital bed while my memory trickled in. I needed out.
And I was afraid. I was afraid that I would wake up from a nap with a dark figure standing over me with a syringe full of puffer fish venom or a pistol with a silencer screwed into the barrel. I was afraid the Agency would decide my punishment would have to fit my many crimes and they'd erase me from this world forever. Would they send someone after me? In those early moments, it was all I thought about. Every bump in the night, every clang in the hallway and every unassuming shadow signaled my doom. I knew it in my bones, that someone was coming for me and in my current condition I was a helpless fish out of water confined to a hospital bed.
I dreamt of being smothered in the night. The nightmares were so real. Strong hands would press a pillow against my face, depriving my lungs of air. Burning suffocation pulled me into the depths of hell as my lungs dried up. Some nights, skilled hands would work me over with a knife while my mouth was sealed with duct tape. I'd watch helplessly, unable to scream through the tape as precision knife cuts weaved up and down my body in a sequence designed to kill me slowly while maximizing the pain. The dreams always shattered seconds before death. I read — don't ask me where, my memory is in pieces — that if you died in your dreams, you died in the real world. I was getting too close for comfort and started taking sedatives to suppress the dreaming. It worked…sort of. With the sedatives, I still woke up in a cold sweat but my sleep was so deep that my consciousness was buried so far below the dreamscape all I could recall was an empty void of black. Escape was easy. Hospitals aren't prisons. The security guards are mostly overweight, donut eating tubs of lard who couldn't run a city block without collapsing.

One lone security guard sat in the lobby downstairs. The front doors to the main lobby were locked, but the entrance to the Emergency Room was always open for business.
I knew where the nurses kept their uniforms. There was a staff lounge at the end of the hall with cubbies stocked with fresh scrubs. Each of them turning up the volume on their symptoms in an attempt to be seen sooner than the next guy. There was a fish tank in the corner with dozens of goldfish bobbing up and down. Poor bastards. I'd never met a goldfish that lived longer than a month. Their days were numbered.
I walked right through the front doors. Not a single head turned.
The Chicago night was like stepping into a frozen hell. Icy winds blew right through my thin scrubs and frosted my skin. This was not a mission. There was no encrypted phone, no stockpile of money and no weapons. I was on my own, with nothing but fragments of my past and a half healed bullet wound to keep me company.

This guy was a piece of shit, but I didn't want to kill him. I just wanted him to suffer.

I grabbed my chest. The gunshot wound screamed. It felt like a fire was raging inside my chest. That lone punch probably tore something open. Time healed all wounds, but only if they weren't mortal. I needed to get to a motel and rest.
I searched Anthony's body. His jacket was a labyrinth of pockets filled with enough pills and powder for a single person to overdose a dozen times. I found a wad of cash held together by a thick rubber band. It must have been at least five grand. A quick glance showed the wad was full of fifties and hundreds. There was also a pistol tucked into his belt.
I lifted the gun and checked the magazine. Eight bullets and one in the chamber. It was a cheap weapon, unkempt and probably hadn't been cleaned since the day he got it. Such a dirty weapon was prone to jams. My friend the drug dealer carried it for intimidation, not violence. I doubt he ever squeezed the trigger and sent a hollow-point into the soft flesh of a person.
I tucked the weapon under my shirt. I thought about taking Anthony's jacket as the cold air swirled around me, but changed my mind when I thought about what diseases might be festering in the fabric.
I walked three blocks and found a seedy motel. The guy at the front desk didn't ask me any questions. He was used to crazed drug addicts scrounging up enough money to get a room for the night. I shoved a fistful of dollars over the desk and he gave me a set of keys with hardly a glance.
The room was a shoebox. A bed was tucked in the corner. The walls had dark stains on them. But the sheets were clean and the blanket thick. There was a gas heater in the corner. I cranked it all the way up, placed the pistol on the ground next to the bed, slipped under the covers and slept.
When I woke up, I could remember everything.

he car skids across the rain-slicked road, hydroplaning in an uncontrolled spin into the intersection. Stephanie grips the wheel.
