he final journal entry of Sofia Vega.
Death is too decent a punishment for these people because a person can only die once. They all deserve to die a thousand times. What is the mantra the Agency is always saying?
The punishment must fit the crime.
When I came to in that shitty motel room in Chicago, my mind was open. The artificial amnesia was gone. Every piece of my past had returned. Some of it was dull and boring. I could remember what I wore to prom and how my date had a small weiner. I could remember my mother tucking me in at night and a green tricycle that I used to ride down the driveway. I remembered my 6th grade teacher teaching me geometry and how the fat under her upper arms jiggled as she drew shapes on the chalk board.
None of that mattered.
The Agency had taken everything from me. And as I remembered what they did, a flame sparked in me. And with each passing moment, the flame grew and grew until it was a burning inferno, fueling me with a thirst that could only be quenched by vengeance.
Before the Agency found me, I had a particular set of skills. Skills that were rare and valuable to them. Skills that I'd spent five years honing and another five using to kill people.
I killed for the first time when I was sixteen years old. It was self-defense. But I was too scared to see if that would hold up in court so I ran away.
I grew up with my aunt. My parents died in a car accident before my fifth birthday. The memory is foggy and I suspect that even before the Agency stuck an implant in my head and infused my brain with artificial amnesia that the memory of my parents was blurry.
I remember Aunt Jenny coming to pick me up the night it happened. I remember her tears as we collected my things from the bedroom and packed up to leave. I remember the soft thud of the front door of my family's apartment as it closed on our way out. Even then, as a small child, I knew I would never return to that place. The happy birthdays and handful of Christmas mornings we'd spent in the small apartment's living room were dead now. They would linger in the back of my mind for the rest of my life like half-remembered dreams
.
Aunt Jenny was absent, but kind. She lived alone and worked for some billionaire and ran around town running errands for him so I hardly ever saw her. But I was provided for. Clean clothes, hot food, decent schools and a roof over my head. I never wanted for anything, but there was no love in the house. We ate in silence. Birthdays came and went. The only gifts she ever gave were envelopes full of money. She did her duty, did right by her late sister and provided for me. I wasn't her daughter. She didn't hate me, but I always felt like a household pet instead of a loved child.
I learned early on that no one was coming to save me. The world breaks everyone. The cracks eventually mend. It's what you do after you crack that defines you.
Time passed.
Days after my sixteenth birthday a boy asked me out on a date. I was ecstatic. His name was Gordon River. Even his name sounded like it was lifted from the pages of a Danielle Steele novel.
I'd had a crush on Gordon since the 6th grade. I was always too shy to talk to him and he was always too absorbed in football and being popular to notice me. In high school, puberty hit me like rocket fuel. And all the boys noticed. My breasts swelled, my long legs grew and I started to realize the power my physical body had over men. I learned to smile with my eyes, sway my hips and cast seductive spells when I wanted attention.
Gordon was a year older than me. He picked me up in his pickup truck at 8 o'clock on a Saturday night. Aunt Jenny hardly noticed I left and gave a slight nod as I walked out of the house.
He took me out to dinner. He droned on and on about football, college scholarships and his bright future. I didn't mind. I could watch him talk all night as his blonde curls bounced over his excited blue eyes.
Later that night, he drove me up into the mountains. As we weaved up the winding road, my heart rate elevated. I knew where we were headed. Kids came up here for the view. On the mountain top, you could see the city lights stretching out into forever like scattered diamonds. They also came up here to make out and have sex.
He pulled over and parked his truck on top of the mountain. We were alone. Moonlight fell through the windshield and filled the cabin in a soft glow. We climbed into the backseat. His lips found mine. Our bodies crashed. His hands were in my hair. My fingers were trailing over his shoulders. One thing led to another and he started to unbuckle his pants.
I stopped him. I told him this was too fast. I told him I was a virgin. He said that was all right, he would be gentle. This should have been a dream come true. I was in the back of a car, under a starry night with Gordon River who I'd wanted to kiss since the 6th grade but it all felt wrong. But when I asked him to stop he wouldn't.
So, I shoved him off me.
His breath was hot. Fire burned his eyes. Confusion, rage and lust covered his face. He wouldn't be denied.
I
remember the bulge in his pants as he pinned me down and started to tear at my clothes. This wasn't happening. I wasn't about to be a rape victim. I wouldn't lose my virginity like this.
I thrashed like a grizzly whose paw was stuck in a trap with nothing to lose. My knee found his crotch. I struck him twice. His eyes rolled. My fingers found the door handle and I scrambled out of the car.
The night air was cold on my cheeks. I cut my knees on the gravel and got to my feet. Before I could get my bearings Gordon shot out of the car. He was enraged. Lust and anger drove him now. He charged. I ducked low and covered my head. Gordon's knees struck my side and he tumbled over my back, knocking me once more to the ground.
When I got up he was gone. I was alone on the mountain side standing a few feet away from the truck. I cautiously approached the edge of the mountain. Several feet down, sprawled out on the rocks below with joints bent at strange angles, was Gordon River. The moonlight that so romantically filled the truck's cabin now lit up Gordon's bloody face. The top half of his skull was cracked open. Blood waterfalled down a boulder. Dead eyes looked back up at me.
I killed him.
I told myself it was self-defense. The police would believe me. I was an innocent girl, they had to believe me.
But as I looked down at the blood seeping from Gordon's head, the reality of what I'd done sunk in. Gordon River was the starting quarterback, the local heart-throb. He was guaranteed a full ride scholarship and was destined for stardom. He lived in a house with a white picket fence, golden retriever and magazine cover parents.
I was the orphan who lived with an aunt who neglected her. Fear wrapped its invisible tendrils around my chest and squeezed.
I climbed into the truck and found the keys. I drove into the night and never looked back. I didn't stop driving for nearly two hundred miles. I found a diner behind a gas station off the highway. I wasn't stupid enough to leave the car in the parking lot, everyone would be looking for it.
I drove a mile past and took an exit that led into a nice suburb. I parked the car in a cul-de-sac, made sure no one was looking and walked a mile back to the diner.
I had sixty dollars in my pocket and no plan.
I ordered pancakes, eggs and coffee. As I scarfed down my food, I noticed a truck driver across the diner eyeballing me.
My eyes darted to the TV screens playing the local news. There was no mention of a dead high school football star. Gordon River was still lying in his own blood all alone. I'd left him there hours ago. Hopefully no one had figured out we were missing yet. Aunt Jennifer kept to herself. She was probably asleep or reading the paper without a thought as to where her niece was
.
The driver eyeballing me grunted and looked away. He tossed a hundred dollar bill on the table, got up and walked out of the diner. No truck driver would waste money like that and walk out without change.
I watched him through the diner window as he headed over to his semi-truck.
In life, there are forks in the road. Simple choices to go left or to go right can change the course of destiny. I decided to follow him. I flagged the waiter down, paid the bill and ran out to meet the driver.
"You're in trouble aren't you?" he asked, pulling his hat low.
"What makes you think that?"
"Only a girl in trouble asks a random truck driver for a lift."
"Let's say I am. Will you help me?"
He paused a moment and put his hands on his hips. His jacket flapped open for a moment and I glimpsed the butt of a pistol strapped to his belt.
"Get in. While I drive you will talk. You'll tell me everything. If you are honest, I'll help you. If I think you are lying, I'll shoot you and leave you to die on the side of the road."
A police car turned into the gas station and parked in front of a pump. The officer climbed out and started pumping gas. I could feel the officer's eyes behind his aviators canvassing the area. My heart skipped a beat as his eyes fell on me.
Did he recognize me? Had a call been put out to find the missing girl who killed Gordon River and stole his truck?
"I'll go. I'll tell you everything."
The truck driver's name was Titus. His mother was obsessed with Roman history and named him after the emperor. "Mom always said Emperor Titus only had two years to rule before fever took him. But he was a powerful man and a name has power so she gave it to me hoping it would give me good fortune."
"And did it?"
The truck slowly took the turn on the onramp and entered the highway. We were a couple hours away from Chicago. I didn't know anyone in Chicago. I had thirty dollars left. But I knew I could disappear in the city and lay low until I figured out what to do.
"Good fortune is all about perspective. Some people might call me lucky, claim that I have it all while others might think me a monster."
"What do you think?" My eyes kept darting to the weapon strapped to his belt. Was this man going to hurt me?
"A little bit of both. Now, let's hear your story. What's a pretty little thing like you doing stranded and asking strange truck drivers for help."
It was stupid, but I told him the truth. I spilled my guts. I told him about Gordon River trying to rape me, how I fought my way out of the truck and left Gordon's lifeless body in the middle of the night.
"Now, that's a hell of a story."
"Are you going to turn me in?"
"Yes, but not to who you think."
It wasn'
t a threat. Titus's voice was soft and reassuring. I didn't think he was going to hurt me. We drove for the next several hours in silence.
When we got to Chicago, Titus pulled the truck into a warehouse on the outskirts of the city. The big metal doors closed behind us. Men stepped out of the shadows and began to unload the truck. We climbed out and a man in a pristine white suit approached.
"What do we have here?"
Titus explained. I stood still as men continued to unload box after box out of the back of the truck. I couldn't believe what I was hearing. Titus was telling this man I had murdered Gordon River and had taken me in. They discussed murder like the weather.
My eyes wandered to the boxes being unloaded from the truck. A couple of men with crowbars opened one of them up and pulled out a pair of assault rifles. I wasn't in Kansas anymore.
When Titus was done explaining how he picked me up, the man in the white suit turned to me. "My name is Isaac Francis. You've been honest with me, so I will be honest with you. We do bad things here. We sell drugs. We traffic weapons. We make ungodly amounts of money. That doesn't make us barbarians. We respect our employees. The life you had before is over. There is no going back to it and this is one of those rare moments where the fork in the road will change the course of your destiny. I can turn you over to the police and you can try your best to stay out of prison. Good luck with that after fleeing the murder scene and disappearing. Or, you can close the book on your old life completely, become someone new and start working for me."
I didn't have much of a choice. I steeled myself for what lay ahead and left my old life behind.
I learned the trade. I rode shotgun next to Titus and moved drugs and weapons all over the country, always circling back to the warehouse in Chicago. After a year on the road, Isaac had something new in mind for me. Not many criminal organizations employed young girls. My eighteenth birthday had come and gone. I was hardened by the years on the road, the things I'd seen and the people I'd rubbed elbows with in this business, but the innocence hadn't left my face. I still looked like I was in high school.
This presented certain advantages to Isaac.
I could go places undetected. I could seduce men easily. I could gather secrets and move through the city in ways his trusted men could not.
Before he sent me out into the world, Isaac wanted to train me.
I spent a year honing my body, turning it into a swiss army knife. They taught me hand-to-hand combat. I could take on a man twice my size and win. The key was to keep your distance. If a man got his hands on you it didn't matter how trained you were, brute strength was going to win. But, if you could slip through his defenses and rapidly attack the soft parts he'd drop before he knew what hit him.
Palm of the hand to the chin or bottom of the nose in an upward jab. Medium force to knock him out. Full strength to kill, breaking the bone and driving upwards into the brain
.
Drive a knife under the lower rib and upwards into the vital organs.
Fingers into the eyes.
Balls crushed in a closed fist.
Heels driven down onto vulnerable toes.
They taught me martial arts. I fought violently. I blended the brutality of Krav Maga with the strategic throws of Jiu-Jitsu. I was small, but fast. The style played to my strengths and minimized all my weaknesses.
They taught me to use weapons. I focused on small knives. I learned to twirl them around my fingers like ballet dancers. Quick strikes were the most effective. Grip the handle, swing it upwards in an arch into the belly. I was shorter than most men and lighter. Driving a blade downwards like a serial killer in the movies required force and strength that I didn't have. I relied on precision and speed.
I could gut a man in a blink.
Firearms came naturally. Like small blades, I preferred small pistols. They fit easier into hidden pockets and the palm of my dainty hand. I must have fired a thousand rounds a month mastering a range of weapons from handguns to sniper rifles.
Isaac sent me on missions to kill his enemies. I was his secret weapon.
When a rival drug lord encroached on his territory and wouldn't back down, I was sent to deal with it. I tracked his movements, learned his routine and discovered his favorite coffee shop. He had brunch there every day at 10:15 sharp. I took a seat at a nearby table and opened a large paperback. Within minutes, he was talking to me.
I got him alone and slipped a knife between his ribs. His eyes went wide. Before he could say anything, I pulled the blade out and shoved it into his throat.
It was my first kill. It was a baptism. As the blood poured out his neck and down my arm I felt my old life wash away. Gordon River and my life with Aunt Jenny turned to gossamer in my mind and I wiped it away.
I embraced who I was becoming. The future lay bare before me.
A small arms dealer who wouldn't pay his dues to Isaac was getting cocky. He was undercutting our prices and word began to travel that he was talking big, threatening to usurp Isaac's empire. For this assignment, I didn't seduce the target. I brought a sniper rifle. He lived in a highrise condo that overlooked the Chicago skyline. I found a perch on the roof of a nearby building. I waited forty-five minutes for the perfect shot. When he sat down for dinner, I looked at the side of his head through my scope, lined up the crosshairs and squeezed the trigger.
Seconds later, the bullet punched through the glass and found his temple. He fell face first into his pasta.
And so it went.
Isaac paid me. I killed people. I grew older. My bank account swelled. I became a ghost. I did terrible things and with each mission I transformed into something black and gray. No high school girl wants to grow up to be an assassin, but that is what I had become
.
I only killed bad people. If I had been sent to put women and children into the ground I wouldn't be able to live with myself. Isaac was no gentleman, but he had a code. That code elevated him miles above the other arms dealers and drug lords out there in the world. I slept at night knowing I was killing people who put guns into the hands of criminals and drugs into the pockets of school children. I did my best to forget my employer did the same thing.
By the time I was twenty-one, I didn't recognize myself in the mirror. The girl who killed Gordon River was thin, bony and fragile. Her womanly features had only begun to bud. The woman in the mirror was still thin, but she was hard and her muscles were strong like tightly bound ripcords. I had curves in all the places men liked them. Dark hair fell over my eyes and I pulled it back into a pony tail so my vision wouldn't be obstructed when looking down the scope of a rifle.
But what changed most was my eyes. My eyes were deep oceans that had seen death. They were fierce and piercing, like a wolf's.
I was a hardened killer. Maybe that's why I fell so hard for a soft man? Because I needed to find a way back to my humanity?
I met Jules Verdana in the park. I was going for a morning run and saw him sitting on a bench, scribbling in a notebook. There was nothing special about him. He sat cross-legged with the notebook propped on his knee. His collar was propped up to shield his face from the chill blowing in from Lake Michigan. Dark hair fell over dark rimmed glasses as he wrote.
I think it was his plainness that caught my eye. I could tell from a mile away that this was a man who read his tax return a dozen times over before submitting it to the IRS to make sure everything was perfect. He was straight as an arrow. After spending so many years of my life surrounded by criminals, his extreme normalcy was like a magnet and I could not pull away.
I sat next to him. He half jumped out of his skin. I laughed and asked him what he was working on.
Jules Verdana was a writer. He had some minor success writing crime fiction. His first book topped the bestseller list for a month and then fell down the charts just as fast as it had risen. The publisher was disappointed. There were no movie deals, no viral social media trends to propel the book. But the reviews were good and the sales were decent. The publisher offered him a modest advance for a sequel.
He never intended to write a sequel, but he was broke and couldn't turn down the $30,000 advance so he signed on the dotted line and promised to hand over the first draft of a sequel within six months. But the sequel wasn't coming easy. Jules suffered writer's block. The first book seemed to flow out of him, but this next book felt forced as all things we do only for money feel.
I met him later that night for dinner. He talked about books. Every other word out of his mouth was about Hemingway, Fitzgerald and how they paved the way for modern writers like Tom Clancy and Stephen King. I had little time for reading so most of what he was saying went over my head, but I loved to listen to him talk. Jules spoke about books as if they were the most
important thing in the universe. As if all the world's problems could be solved by good stories.
I lied and told him I was an insurance broker. I couldn't tell him the truth that I was an assassin for an arms dealer.
That night we made love. It wasn't my first time. Like anyone, I had sexual impulses. I fulfilled those impulses with short term flings, one night stands and never fell into the trap of a long term relationship. My life didn't allow for intimacy. I couldn't let anyone in.
But, that night, I let Jules in and it felt wonderful.
My life is a series of tragedies. If past lives are real, then I must have been a real bitch in the last one because karma has it out for me this time around. If there is a God, he doesn't care much for me.
I had four weeks with Jules before Isaac killed him. Four weeks of bliss. When I was with Jules, the world didn't seem so dark. He taught me there was more than money and seedy dealings. He taught me there was magic everywhere and that good books were portals to other realms. In the early hours of the morning, after we were drunk on wine and good sex, he would curl over and read before sleep took him. He never went to sleep without at least reading a page or two.
And I loved him for it.
Isaac wasn't jealous. He didn't want me having contact with anyone outside his world. I was too valuable. I also knew too many of his secrets.
I missed my period. Two weeks later, I found myself staring at a positive pregnancy test.
I thought the baby was a gift. The first real gift this cruel world had offered me. The world had taken my parents, sent Gordon River after me and tossed me into the horrors of Isaac's drug running empire. I'd endured enough. Whatever sins I'd committed, I'd done my penance.
I wanted to marry Jules. I wanted to help him write his stupid books and live off pennies while exploring his imagination. I wanted to have this baby.
Isaac smiled when I told him. He congratulated me and told me to go live my life. And I did…for two days.
In the dead of night, two men broke into Jules's apartment while we were sleeping. They kicked the door down and barged into the room brandishing pistols with silencers screwed into the barrels. I heard six shots. Four struck Jules in the chest and they put two in his head for good measure.
I screamed until my vision turned red. They slapped duct tape over my mouth and hauled me away in the back of a van. Tears fell down my cheeks as they drove me through the Chicago night. We could only be headed one place.
Isaac was waiting in the warehouse when we pulled in. He was wearing the same cream suit as the first day we met.
They sat me in a chair, left the duct tape over my mouth as Isaac paced back and forth monologuing about how I forced his hand. He couldn't let me go. I was too important. My brash actions put Jules in danger and his life was a small price to pay to keep me on the payroll and doing my job.
And
then, like the fury of God, The Agency arrived.
The power was cut. The warehouse went pitch black. The air sizzled with the pop of gunfire. Blood swept across my face in a hot splash. The dull slap of lifeless bodies hitting the floor filled my ears. It only lasted a minute, maybe two, and then everything went silent.
As the killing played out, I tried to hone my senses. The chaos was over before my eyes could adjust to the darkness. The lights turned back on.
Four men stood before me. Three of them were young and wearing black suits. They each carried an MP5. From my training, I knew this was the weapon of choice for SWAT teams. It was the perfect blend of semi-automatic power, had range and fired tight in close quarter combat. It was also expensive. Only professionals carried them.
The fourth man was older. Wrinkles traced across his cheek bones like ancient caverns. His gray hair was groomed and his five-o-clock shadow bristled as he clenched his jaw. He carried no weapon. His hands were stuffed deep in his pockets. The other three men kept calling him "The Director."
His voice flowed like water over gravel. "Agent 917, Agent 429 please canvas the warehouse and make sure everyone is dead. Agent 083, pick up the girl and bring her to the car. We are going on a trip."
They took me to Agency headquarters in the Yukon. I told them I was pregnant. I begged them to let me go. No one listened.
The Director told me that my particular set of skills was too valuable. That I would never be allowed to live a normal life and he couldn't have someone like me running around unchecked.
I needed a leash.
I needed control.
I needed to be put to work.
He droned on and on about how righteous The Agency was and how my abilities would punish evil people doing evil things in an evil world.
They forced an abortion on me. I fought hard, but in the end there was nothing I could do. An injection knocked me out. When I woke up, the abortion was done and the baby gone. I'd never felt more hollow in my entire life. The emptiness didn't last. Within 48 hours, I was under the knife on the surgical table again.
They stuck an implant in me and erased my memory. I became their instrument.
I spent the next eight years jumping from mission to mission killing for the Agency just like I did for Isaac. Only this time, a tiny implant in the base of my brain wiped my memory clean after each kill.
And then the cycle ended.
I woke up with my memory intact and the final tragedy in my story burned bright in my memory. After the Agency installed the implant in my brain, they aborted my child. Killed it before it even had a chance to become a boy or a girl.
My one chance at a life was snatched from me
.
And I've swallowed the hard truth. I will never have a normal life. I will never be forgiven for my sins. There is too much blood on my hands. But I can get the next best thing — revenge.
And I remembered four faces, four people. Agent 917, Agent 429, Agent 083 and the Director. I'll kill them all. But those agents, I have to set them free first. They deserve that much. Like me, their lives were stolen from them. But also, like me, they have done terrible things that require retribution.
And when it's done, I'll fire a bullet into my throat and go back to sleep. Maybe Jules will be waiting for me? Maybe in the afterlife we can drink coffee and I can help him overcome writer's block? Maybe there will be a shade of happiness waiting in the clearing beyond life?
Or maybe it's all a black void when you die?
I don't care anymore. Revenge is a powerful motive. It's the only thing motivating me, and I will have it.
I'm coming for them all.

W
e exist in a digital frontier.
The average person scrolls 300 to 650 feet a day on their phone, the approximate length of the Eiffel Tower. Likes, comments, shares, cat memes, motivational clips, pranks, accidents, trends and thirty second videos dominate our attention. The average daily screen time is seven hours and three minutes.
We used to look to the stars and dream big, now we crane our necks and stare endlessly into tiny, hypnotic digital windows that lead to a space we can never fully enter.
How will the world end?
The world powers have stockpiled an estimated 9600 nuclear warheads. 90% of that is controlled by the United States and Russia.
The bomb that fell on Hiroshima packed 15 kilotons. Today, a nuclear warhead is 50 times more powerful. There are enough warheads to kill all life on Earth 10 to 20 times over. Nuclear detonation would trigger a radioactive ice age where nothing would survive…except for cockroaches.
When some advanced, alien civilization on a mission seeking new lifeforms stumbles upon Earth they would find a radioactive tundra, unable to support life. There would be little to no trace of who we were, what we did or what we believed in.
Ancients etched their histories into stone tablets and caves. These ancient civilizations recorded their stories in rock and embedded themselves in the salt of the Earth for future generations to find.
We haven't done this. Digital records don't last. If there is a mass failure of the digital frontier, our history and culture won't endure. We'd be wiped from history.
We could be erased with the stroke of a key.
These are the thoughts that echoed in Spade's mind when she awoke from the deepest sleep she'd ever had.
The exhaustion was so complete that her dreams were nothing but an empty, dark void. The black swallowed her soul and wrapped around her consciousness like a warm blanket. When she awoke, she felt refreshed.
Her stomach grumbled. She hadn't been eating. Food would make her tired. To stay awake, she'd been living off a steady diet of coffee and water
.
She rolled over and picked up her phone. There were 47 missed calls from Robert — or as everyone at the Agency called him, The Director. He must have given up because his last call was over five hours ago.
Spade needed a shower. She cleaned up and put on casual clothes — a pair of jeans and an old, oversized hoodie with a couple holes in the sleeve. It was the type of sweater you wore often and no matter how ragged it got, found a way to stay out of the hands of Goodwill and at the top of the drawer.
Agent 917 shot and killed his assailant. He'd done it with surgical precision. Then, he disappeared into the night. Spade only had an inkling of where he was headed. Security cameras at the hotel were limited to the common areas and the parking lot. Her best guess was that he was heading north up towards San Francisco. Whether he would stop there, keep going or divert his trajectory was beyond her.
Finding him in San Francisco was next to impossible. And the likelihood of him staying put there was near zero.
She'd spent the next three days looking for him but there was no trace. The fight left her system. She wasn't willing to break herself anymore and the next night she slept eight hours.
She finally decided to take the Director's phone call. He was displeased.
"You are not allowed to give up. You've been sleeping like a lazy cat while we have a wild cannon on the loose!"
"Robert, he's gone. I can't wave a magic hand over my computer and ask Google Maps to give me directions. If he doesn't want to be found, he won't be."
"I demand a solution. I swear to God, don't make me regret all the slack I cut you."
"We both know that's a hollow threat. Without me, you have no Agency. Good luck running your network without me. Your amateur computer engineers would last a month tops before the system crashed. And despite what you might think, I haven't given up. I've fed all of our digital systems everything I know about 917 from his physical characteristics to his psychological ones. I've designed Artificial Intelligence software programs that run on their own, scouring the digital landscape of the globe for any trace of him. The best we can do is wait."
"Wait!?"
Spade pictured the Director sitting alone in his cold office room full of mahogany furniture in the Yukon overlooking the snow frosted Canadian frontier. She imagined the bulging vein in his forehead that always appeared when he was frustrated.
"Yes, we wait. The A.I. will look faster and more efficiently than me. I'm going out to clear my head."
She hung up. Her smart watch ticked past 10 am. The bio readout of her heart rate was steady. That was saying something. Usually heated arguments with Robert spiked it. Maybe she was turning a new leaf
?
Outside her window, the day bloomed. The streets buzzed with people. The sun was shining. There wasn't a cloud in the sky. She longed to be outside. Her pasty white skin felt dry and deprived of nutrients. She hadn't left the apartment in days and she'd been glued to her computer desk chasing 917 and failing. The Director could blow his top, break furniture, have a god damn heart attack for all she cared — it wouldn't change her course of action. There simply wasn't anything to do but wait.
She was in the terrible position many homicide detectives find themselves in. When chasing a serial killer, and every lead and clue has dried up, sometimes the only way to catch the killer is to wait for another body to show up and pray it brings new clues.
Robert isn't going to make it to seventy. He isn't sleeping. He barely eats. The job is consuming him.
Spade wouldn't let the work destroy her like it had the Director.
The refrigerator was empty. She was out of coffee beans and had grown tired of the generic brand of tea bags in her kitchen. It was time to get some fresh air.
She skipped the makeup, tossed her hair up in a messy bun and put on clean clothes. She used the joystick to maneuver her wheelchair to the elevator and ride to the lobby.
Once outside, she rolled four blocks east to her favorite brunch spot. It was a place stuck in time. The Tin Can was a diner housed in the corner of an old brick building. The stools and booths hadn't changed since the 80's. The inside buzzed with the rush of people. The host noticed her and gave her a table.
Spade perused the menu and let the noise of the diner flood her senses. The clank of silverware, the chatter of old men telling tall tales at the counter, the laughter of a young waitress flirting with a patron, the sizzle of eggs popping on the grill and the delight of a little girl getting a Mickey Mouse shaped pancake distracted Spade from her work.
The distractions didn't hold her attention for long. After a few minutes of sitting alone her mind drifted back to the Agency.
She knew the best course of action was to wait, to let her software scour security cameras and search for digital traces of Agent 917. Manually guessing which city 917 was in and scrolling through video footage would be like trying to catch smoke with your bare hands.
917 was a trained killer. She meticulously planned his missions. He started each one with a good night's sleep, unlimited money, precise weapons and instructions to ensure he killed his targets. It wasn't the same as being a Navy Seal or super spy trained to disappear.
He told me to check my P.O. Box. Three days and something would be waiting for me.
Today was day three. Whatever package 917 was sending her was her only link to him. She'd kept this detail to herself and didn't want to share it with The Director until she knew what it was
.
She broke every rule of her strict diet and scarfed down a waffle topped with whipped cream, blueberries and maple syrup. She'd stuffed her full weekly carb limit into a single meal of carbs, gluten and sugar. Right now, she just didn't give a shit and savored every bite.
She left the diner and made her way to the post office.
An envelope waited for her in the P.O. Box. She hesitated before opening it. What if it was full of anthrax?
917 wouldn't send anthrax. He wanted her to see something.
She tore open the envelope and a composition notebook tumbled out. It was the journal of Sofia Vega.

17 drove without stopping. He left San Francisco before rush hour and was now coasting along the highway towards the midwest. The open road made him feel uneasy. Tall city buildings gave him a feeling of comfort. If the Agency found him, he could escape in the crevices of a sprawling urban jungle. Out here, he was a sitting duck. He needed to find a place to lay low, process his own memories and decide what to do next.
He kept expecting exhaustion to creep up on him, but it never did. The Agency tried to kill him. Anger and adrenaline flowed through his veins. There would be no rest until he reached his destination.
He needed to ditch his ride. He'd taken this car back in San Francisco. That was hours ago. By now, the owner had likely reported it stolen. Driving around in a stolen car was a good way to get pulled over and arrested.
He found a new ride a few miles outside of Vegas in the parking lot of a mall.
He found an old, but classic looking Camaro towards the back of the lot. Picking the lock and hotwiring the car was like finding his way around an old lover's body. He knew every curve of the car and muscle memory came back. As he slid into the driver's seat, he glimpsed a memory of training at the Agency facility in the Yukon. In the memory, he fought another man on a wrestling mat. His right cheek tickled as he recalled the pain of his opponent's right hook smashing his jaw.
His memory was working better and better with each passing second.
The Camaro tore through the desert heat as 917 shot through Nevada. The windows were down and hot air filled the cabin. The sensation brought a quiet smile to his face. Ever since his implant had been rendered inert, it was like his senses had been dialed up. Every sensation, every perception had taken on new meaning because he knew it would be remembered. Life belonged to him now. It wouldn't be snatched away at the end of a successful mission.
By the time 917 was halfway through Wyoming, the sun was setting below the horizon. Magnificent purples and oranges spread over the rocky landscape. It was like driving through a dream where everything was made of paint and touched by the hand of God. If he weren't on the run, stopping for the night and camping didn't sound like such a bad idea
.

The sun had all but disappeared by the time 917 got to Nebraska, replaced by a silver moon and a velvet blanket full of stars.
917 was running low on gas. He found a filling station off the highway. There were ten pumps. Two were occupied. 917 took the one furthest from the convenience store. The surrounding landscape was flat. Twenty hours ago, he was in sprawling San Francisco with impossible hills. Out here in Nebraska, it was flat for miles with looming mountains in the distance.
He couldn't use any of the credit cards to pay for gas. The Agency would flag the charge and pinpoint his whereabouts within seconds. He didn't think they had a large task force at their disposal otherwise they would have sent one after him back in Bakersfield instead of a lone agent.
He still had a bundle of cash and replenished his stash by taking Agent 204's money. He had over fifteen grand, but he needed to watch his spending. There was no telling when he would get his hands on more cash. Once he ran out, he'd have to get a job or steal it. Getting a job under these circumstances seemed ludicrous.
Before exiting the Camry, he reached into his backpack and shoved a fifty into his pocket. While digging through the backpack, his fingers grazed the sharp edges of the Beretta M9.
The weapon always brought him comfort. Even if there was no need for it, just having it on his person made him feel better. A piece of advice his father used to say echoed in his mind.
Better to be prepared than to be caught with your pants down.
It was nice to remember. There were secrets in 917's past he didn't want to look at, but there were also happy times.
He stuffed the weapon into the back of his pants and entered the gas station convenience store.
When he stepped through the automatic doors, the electronic bell over the entrance chimed. The cashier stared at 917 wide eyed. There was a man standing in front of the cashier pointing a gun at his chest.
The man with the gun had a hoodie pulled over his head. He licked his lips as his eyes darted to 917. "Don't you fucking move!"

he barrel of the gun pendulumed erratically between the cashier and 917. The gunman's hands were shaking, making the tip of the pistol do a little jig.
917 smiled. This was going to be fun.
For the past two days, he'd been bouncing from town to town stealing cars and rambling across the country. He hoped by the time he got to his destination, the painting would be complete.
"I will shoot you!" The gunman was nervous. That made him dangerous. Reasoning with nervous people with a firearm was tricky business. A mental switch could flip at any moment, sending the bullets flying. He could shoot 917. He could shoot the cashier. Predicting the gunman's choices was impossible.
917 raised his hands. "Just calm down a minute."
"Shut the fuck up and get down on the ground, palms down."
"What's your name?"
"Fuck you."
"That's a terrible name. Your mother really called you that? No wonder you are knocking off gas stations in the middle of nowhere for a living."
"You think you are funny?" The gunman walked over to 917, pistol raised in an arch and pointed at his head. Anger flared across the gunman's face. He pressed the barrel into 917's temple.
"Easy!" 917 kept his hands raised and took a step back. "You haven't killed anyone yet. There's no murder charge here. In the state of Wyoming, you'll get the chair for murder. Let's all just take it down a
notch."
"I'll only get the chair if they catch me!" The gunman's confidence and control of the situation increased now that he could press the pistol into 917's body with no chance of missing.
"They probably won't look too hard if no one dies and you just take the cash and leave."
"Say one more word and I will paint the walls with the insides of your brain. Are we clear?"
"Clear as day."
"Good. Now, get down on the fucking floor."
917 knelt slowly, one knee at a time. The cashier was middle-aged. His eyes were wide and his hands were frozen, palms down on the counter. 917 didn't expect the guy to do anything but stand and stare like a deer in the headlights.
The gunman pushed the back of 917's head with the gun. "All the way down. Last warning."
917 heard the click of the hammer being pulled back. If the gunman was bluffing before, he wasn't now. This guy had nothing to lose.
The linoleum floor was cold. It stank from years of neglect and foot traffic. The tile was sticky against 917's fingertips. He turned his head sideways and lay his cheek against the ground.
The gunman pressed the weapon against the bottom of his spine and barked at the cashier. "Open it up. Put all the cash in a paper bag and leave it on the counter. Do it now, or I'll put a bullet into this man's back."
The cashier did as he was told.
917 controlled his breathing and tried to keep his heart rate down. His mind was calm, but he needed his body to relax if he was going to get out of this alive.
"Just don't hurt us." The cashier dropped the bag of cash on the counter and backed away with his hands up.
There was no tactical move. By some miracle, the gunman hadn't noticed the pistol tucked in the back of 917's pants. For now, the best choice was to stay still.
As the cashier backed away, the bag of money fell off the counter and onto the floor. A few bills fell out. The gunman cursed and walked over, strafing sideways to keep 917 in his sight. It was about ten feet to the register. When the gunman got there, he knelt down and scooped the cash back into the bag.
917's movements were a blur. The moment the gunman's eyes darted off of him, he tucked his arms in and rolled across the floor into an aisle and out of sight. The Beretta M9 filled his hands as he stood up. He leveled the barrel at the gunman's face.
A red dot bloomed in the man's forehead. Blood poured out of the hole, down his cheek and onto the floor. The man collapsed.
The cashier whimpered.
917 looked up at the ceiling. There was a security camera pointed right at him. How long would it take for the Agency to find this footage? It didn't
matter. He couldn't hide forever. And he planned to be long gone before anyone came to investigate.
"What's your name?" 917 asked the cashier.
The cashier's back was pressed against the wall, his eyes locked on the dead man on the floor. He was frozen with fear, going into shock and unable to speak.
917 needed to calm him down. "Until a couple days ago, I didn't know my name. Turns out my name is Charles. I'm still working on the last name. But I think this is the part where you say 'Thank you, Charles.'"
"Thank you." The cashier nodded. "Everyone calls me Benny."
917 tucked his gun back under his shirt, hoping to help diffuse the situation. He approached the register. "You okay?"
Benny nodded. "I'm fine now, thanks to you. My wife is never going to believe this."
"Listen, Benny. I'm going to level with you. I have a criminal record. I'm on parole and I'm not supposed to be out here let alone have an unregistered gun on me. If the police catch me, I'll go back to the slammer. Do you think I deserve that?"
Benny shook his head, the fear that had so quickly passed was resurfacing. "No, I don't. You saved me."
"Most gas stations have security cameras that record footage on hard drives on site. I can't have the cops see that footage. Can you show me where it is?"
Benny took 917 into the backroom. There was a computer with a screen showing the feeds from the security cameras outside the gas station and inside the store.
"You understand why I can't have the police see this footage?"
Benny was at a loss for words. He gently nodded his head. He just wanted all this to be over and to go home and tell his wife about how he'd nearly died.
"I'm not going back to jail, Benny. They can't know I was here." 917 pulled out his weapon and filled the computer with bullets.
Benny shrieked. He shriveled in the corner and covered his ears.
"You can relax. The violence is over now." 917 reached into his pocket and produced the fifty dollar bill he'd brought with him to pay for gas. "Keep the change, I need to fill up at pump number 4. You can tell the police I threatened you, that you had no choice but to let me back here, but do me a solid and describe a black guy with no hair will you? I don't need them coming after me. Can you do that for me?"
Benny stood up and took the fifty. "Sure thing, Charles."
917 pulled out of the gas station grinning ear to ear. The gas tank was full. He wouldn't have to stop until he got to his destination.
Trouble seemed to follow him around. That was all right. He was getting quite good at dealing with it.

T
he Director sat alone in the boardroom. His suit was perfectly pressed. Sharp creases sliced down the pant legs. The long table sparkled with clean varnish. Every chair was tucked under the lip of the table exactly six inches apart. The room was made of sharp angles and felt machine like. The walls were empty. Wood paneling stretched from floor to ceiling.
It was a lonely room.
He hadn't slept in two days. A headache bloomed in his head like a cancer spreading its wings and infecting his nervous system. His only company was the steaming cup of coffee which was always by his side. Any time it was empty, he felt hollow. Even if he wasn't sipping it, he felt better cradling the warmth of a full mug.
Spade left months ago. She was in her apartment in New York being her normal, recalcitrant self. They'd sent Agent 204 after 917. 204 was dead. 917 was in the wind and his memories were returning. The Director wondered how much of the past had returned to him.
Hopefully not all of it or we are all fucked.
The Director had few regrets. There was blood on his hands, there was no denying that, but everything he did was for the greater good of mankind. If no one was willing to push the boundaries of right and wrong, the bad people The Agency had put down over the years would still be running wild taking innocent life.
Sometimes, the only way to save life was to kill.
He leaned back in his chair and took in the view of the massive window overlooking the valley stretching out before Agency headquarters. The snow was melting. Spring was coming. Flowers and wild brush punched up through the snow. A few deer galloped by in the sunlight. It was peaceful. Spring was full of hope and right next door, the death-dealing Agency loomed up into the crystal blue sky like a dark castle.
The Director pressed his palm flat against the window pane and fanned his fingers. The cold chill from outside the glass kissed his hand. He pulled it away and clenched his fingers. The artificially heated room was comfortable. He hadn't stepped outside into the Yukon's unforgiving climate in over a week
.
His mind wandered back a few months to the height of summer and the massive grizzly that he'd killed. He had wanted to stretch his legs and do something that didn't involve a digital touch screen. Every day he dealt in death. It was his stock and trade. He handled a dossier full of murder and assassins with the same casual interest of a stock broker reviewing balance sheets. Killing something dangerous and real sounded thrilling.
The Yukon is prime grizzly bear territory. There are an estimated 6,000-7,000 grizzlies populating the area. The Director wanted to kill one and make a rug out of it. Maybe he'd hang it up on the barren walls of the conference room. If the room got any emptier, it might cease to exist. It needed character and a trophy grizzly pelt rising up the walls sounded eloquent.
He took two men with him on the venture. He wasn't stupid enough to go bear hunting alone. Grizzlies were massive animals. An adult male can weigh up to 800 pounds and its claws grow into 4 inch knives that could carve a man like turkey on Thanksgiving.
The Director and his men each carried bear pepper spray, binoculars, a knife and a high-powered hunting rifle over their shoulder. His personal choice was a Ruger Hawkeye Alaskan .375 hunting rifle with a modified scope. It was a bolt action rifle with a control feed mechanism. This was not a weapon for war. You slid the bullets in manually which made for a very low chance of jamming.
Pulling the bolt action lever back and hearing the firing mechanism inside click into place was an added bonus. It was the sound of dominance and signaled that something beautiful was about to happen.
The three men trudged together deep into grizzly territory. The Director spotted a splotch of bear fur stuck to a tree. Grizzlies rub their backs against the bark to scratch themselves and their fur would get stuck in the sap.
There was dung on the ground. The grizzly was close.
One of the men climbed a tall boulder and scanned the area with his binoculars. He spotted a grizzly fifty yards away. The Director looked to where his man was pointing and spotted the hulking beast rummaging in the woods. He took aim and put the bear in the crosshairs of his scope.
It was a full-grown male. It weighed anywhere from 750 to 800 pounds. The bear was lurking on all fours, its nose close to the ground. A bear's skull is dense. If the shot is not perfectly placed, the bullet can glance off the bone and the bear will come charging. The cleaner shot was into the bear's broadside, just under the front legs. That's where the heart and lungs were, a clean shot would put the bear down.
The Director squeezed the trigger.
As the bullet flew the bear turned. The bullet struck it in the shoulder, missing the vital organs.
An earth shattering roar ripped through the air. The bear's long white teeth revealed themselves. It turned and spotted the Director. A grizzly can run up to 35 mph on all fours. This one was injured. It was angry. It rampaged through the foliage toward the Director
.
The Director pulled the bolt back on his rifle and the next bullet fell into the chamber. There were three shots left. He could hear his heart racing as he took aim. The bear would be on him in seconds.
His next shot struck the bear in the throat. It spun to the left and tumbled to the ground. The bear tried to get up, took a small step and collapsed.
The Director let out a breath and loaded the next bullet. He kept the rifle trained on the dying bear as he stepped closer.
Once he was a few feet away, he could see there was no threat. The grizzly was a dying mass of blood and fur. His second shot ripped a hole in its throat and it was choking on its own blood. Each painful breath filled its lungs with more fluid. It was a horrible way to go.
He flipped the safety on his rifle, slung it over his shoulder and unsheathed his hunting knife. It was a twelve inch curved blade with a serrated back. He knelt next to the bear and laid a hand on its side. He could feel the beast's hot breath on his cheek. There was no fight left in the creature.
He stuck the blade into the bear's broadside and punctured its heart.
There was no roar of defiance. The creature's eyes rolled and it died in silence.
His men congratulated him and said it would make a fine rug. In the end, he decided to leave the beast here in the wild. The forest would make use of the meat. He'd changed his mind about the rug.
Watching the grizzly trample towards him with bloodlust was the most terrifying thing he'd ever seen in his life. He didn't need a reminder of this moment hanging from his wall.
The phone rang, pulling his attention away from the memory of the grizzly hunt.
It was Spade.
"You've got something?" The Director asked.
"Does the name Sofia Vega mean anything to you?" Spade was angry. Her voice curt and full of sharp edges.
"Doesn't ring a bell." She shouldn't know that name. He worked hard to bury Sofia's past. Spade was no grizzly. He wasn't scared of her. He was more curious than afraid.
"Don't you fucking lie to me, Robert. I know what you did to her. You killed her baby, stole her life and stuck an implant in her."
"And you know this how?"
"She was Agent 048. You thought she was killed in a failed mission months ago but you were wrong. Her implant malfunctioned so we lost contact with her. You of all people should know that making assumptions in our line of work can be fatal. She remembered everything and recorded it in a journal. She was cleaning house, killing everyone who was involved in taking her life. 917 was next on her list and then she was coming after you."
"Well, it's a good thing she's dead then.
"
"You told me all the agents volunteered. They had to agree to the memory wipes or the implants wouldn't hold."
There was no use lying to her. Spade deserved the truth. His father always told him that honesty was the best policy. Ironic because liars always prospered. "In the beginning, I didn't know the implants wouldn't hold if the agents didn't volunteer. The first agents were forced. They were chosen and yes we took their lives. But once we learned the artificial amnesia wouldn't hold without the agent submitting we started only taking volunteers."
"She was pregnant, Robert. I didn't sign up for this shit."
"Don't patronize me. Your hands are just as dirty as mine. It's a dirty business. Don't grow a fucking conscience now. Stay focused on the task at hand. We have to get 917 under control."
"Do you remember when 917's implant malfunctioned?"
"Of course, back in San Francisco. We brought him in and fixed all the bugs."
"You told him that drug dealers murdered his family, killed his daughter and after that he couldn't bear the pain so he submitted to have his memory wiped. Was that a lie? Is that really what happened?"
"Why does it matter?"
"Because I think you are full of shit. I think his implant failed because he never volunteered in the first place. I think you took his life just like you did Sofia Vega's."
"You are treading on thin ice. I would choose your next words carefully."
"Okay how's this? You tell me the truth right now or I blow this all up. I send everything I have on the Agency to the FBI, CIA and NSA and we all go to prison for the rest of our lives."
"Go ahead. You think those government agencies would be able to do anything to us even with all that information. You think our investors don't hold high positions in those places. I'm afraid if you start tossing threats around an agent might be sent out for you. Spade, please. You are my oldest friend. You know the Agency has to continue its mission and 917 is now a danger to that mission."
There was a long pause. The Director could practically hear the gears in Spade's mind grinding through the silence of the receiver.
"Goodbye, Robert."
The line went dead.
The Director stared up at the blank wall and decided it looked better without the grizzly hanging from it. He could always hunt another one when the summer came.

C
heryl Cooper bought the wingback chair at a garage sale and tucked it into the corner of her living room near the fireplace.
Growing up, her father had one just like it with swooping edges and dark blue fabric held in place with brass studs. Every Sunday evening they would have family dinner. Mom would rotate between steaks, meatloaf and chicken with mashed potatoes and veggies. The best part was the end when she would break out the creamy vanilla ice cream and chocolate syrup. After dessert, it was family reading time. They would all pitch in on the dishes and then pick out their favorite book to read in the living room. Her father always had some tome open on his lap as he crossed his legs in the authoritarian chair. She would imagine that chair as a throne and the rest of the family were his loyal subjects. The best spot (other than the chair) was snuggled up on the nearby couch. She must have read close to a hundred Nancy Drew cozy mysteries as a child. She loved them. Nancy Drew was always in danger, but her wits were so sharp she always survived and put the villains away. Turning the pages of those yellowed paperbacks near a crackling fire was one of the happiest times of her life.
She'd always dreamed of having a big family and sprawling them out across the living room for family reading time. That life was snatched away from her when John was killed in the Middle East. He promised her he'd come home. Fate had other plans for them and their beautiful life was cut short.
And without a man to protect her, Dr. Thomas Philtzer tormented them. She did her best to not think about it, but the image of his sinister face looming over her often filled her dreams and she'd wake up in cold sweats. She'd get up in the middle of the night drenched with her heart pounding. Last night was one of those nights. Around three in the morning, the darkness of her sleep was shattered by his groping hands closing over her throat. When she opened her eyes in terror, she was alone in the bedroom but could feel the deadman's presence. She pulled the covers back and tip-toed into the kitchen to get a glass of water. The cool liquid trickling down her throat brought her back to reality.
Thomas Philtzer was dead.
That strange man killed him
.
Ever since then, her life had been peaceful. But the ghosts still haunted her.
She walked down the hall to her daughter's room and cracked open the door. A sliver of moonlight fell across her daughter's face. She looked beautiful in the silver light, like a Disney princess waiting for Prince Charming to come kiss her. Jessica was sixteen years old now. The horror Thomas Philtzer put them through was nearly six years ago.
After Thomas Philtzer, Cheryl bought the wingback chair to try and bring some life back into their home. She'd found her old Nancy Drew paperbacks in storage and hauled them into the living room where they collected dust.
Jessica wasn't interested in reading cozy mysteries anymore. At first, she would sit comfortably on the couch and read them next to her mother. After a couple years, she grew more interested in the iPad and watching Netflix than logging reading time with her mother.
There was an old cuckoo clock hanging on the wall in the living room. It had been in her family for generations. According to family lore, her great grandfather built it by hand. The clock itself still worked. There were a series of chains with weights that hung from the bottom. There was a way to work the chains so the clock would chime at a set time, the double wooden doors in the front would open and a rooster would pop out. She could never figure out how to work it and just left the clock there to tell the time.
For some reason, today when the hour struck noon, the cuckoo clock sounded off.
Bells dinged. Widgets inside whirred. The double doors flew open and a red rooster shot out, its beak bobbing up and down.
Cheryl had been sitting in the wingback chair, lost in a romance novel for the past hour when the clock decided to chime. She slammed the book shut and jumped out of the chair. The clock scared her half to death.
She walked over to the clock, but by the time she got there it had silenced itself. The rooster had tucked back inside and the small double doors had closed.
She wondered if Jessica had tampered with it.
Her nerves were shot. The hairs on the back of her neck prickled. Her heart was going. Her senses were elevated as if her body were going into flight or fight mode. She wasn't going to be able to sit back in her chair and read.
Jessica would be at school for two more hours. Cheryl needed to pass the time and clear her head. She meandered into the kitchen and found a pitcher of iced tea in the fridge. She poured a tall glass and dropped a slice of lemon into it.
As she gulped it down, she heard a car pull up in front of her house. She froze and listened. The engine shut off. The door opened and closed.
There was someone approaching the front door.

17 pulled into the driveway and got out of the car.

But he wasn't here for romance.

S
pade's blood was boiling. She knew Robert kept secrets from her. They were in the business of secrets. So, why did Sofia's journal shock her so badly?
Because somewhere along the way, I lost my humanity. I let the means justify the ends.
Sunlight filled her apartment. She'd pulled back the curtains and opened all the windows. Warmth and laughter from the spring day made its way inside. It was life. And she needed that life right now. Her body was recovering from sleepless nights hunched over a keyboard while getting lost in the digital maze in her computer screens. Her muscles felt knotted and torn. The mental strain of chasing down 917 had taken a toll. Even though she was operating from the comfort of her own home all of her mental faculties had been dialed up to eleven for the past several days. Her mind had a breaking point.
Sofia's ragged journal rested on her desk. A steaming cup of coffee sat on its leather cover.
A cool prickle slithered down her spine as she looked at the journal. Spade hadn't known Sofia. She had nothing to do with the forcible abortion of her child and subverting her into the Agency.
Why the guilt?
Because she'd done plenty wrong in her life. She rarely questioned the morality of the Agency's mission. She always did her due diligence and felt a sense of satisfaction with every despicable killer her Agents put down. But those acts of violence were punishments. Punishing evil is what she signed up to do. And a part of her knew that in order to defeat it, she would have to embrace darkness.
But there was a fine line that separated her, the Agency and the targets they went after. That line divided good and evil. Good people often do wrong things for the right reasons. She'd done plenty of terrible things. Her hands were tainted. She'd never pulled the trigger, but her keystrokes had resulted in more death than any of her Agents' violent deeds. An Agent could only execute one mission at a time, she could run simultaneous missions from the comfort of her own home.
What waited for her in the afterlife
?
Spade didn't believe in God. She'd seen too much evil, too much of the terrible things people were capable of to have an abiding faith in a Supreme Being. There was no ultimate power, sitting on a throne in the clouds overseeing the human race. And if there was, he was a sadistic God twiddling his thumbs while his creations were busy eating each other.
Her lack of faith didn't mean she didn't think there was no life after death. But she'd given up on the notion long ago that there was something up there guiding her towards heaven. There was more to her than her body. Something animated the corporeal, of that she was certain. But the spiritual motives of people weren't always good. She had a database full of dead serial killers, rapists, arsonists and criminals to prove the point.
Robert's voice echoed in her mind as she remembered a conversation they had years ago when the Agency was being formed.
He was so young back then with smooth skin and shimmering blue eyes. As she turned the memory in her mind, she noted the absence of wrinkles in his face. Such hope, such ambition and such ignorance about the toll the next few years would take on him. Robert had truly believed they could make a better world.
"Do you believe we are here for a reason?" Robert asked. He was wearing his typical dark suit with a double knotted tie. He stood in the newly built Agency Headquarters in the Yukon. The conference room smelled of fresh wood and the floor to ceiling windows were immaculate. How many times had they sat in that room and talked about killing people?
In the memory, Spade was in a different wheelchair. It was a heavy monstrosity and creaked when she turned. Months later, she would purchase a custom, motorized carbon-fiber chair with a seat that felt like sitting in a cloud.
"Robert, are you asking me because you want me to give you an answer or do you want to monologue about what you think your destiny is?"
Her friend smiled. She knew him too well. "This time, I want you to answer."
She wheeled her chair next to him, looked out the windows and took in the magnificent view of the Yukon sprawling out before them. "I think life is what you make of it. I think there is no preordained destiny. I've learned enough about this world to know the basic condition of life is chaos. I don't think we were put here for a reason. No God sent us. But, I do believe we have the ability to create a reason, to give life purpose. You are asking the wrong question. The riddle of life isn't 'why are we here?' but 'what order will we bring to the chaos?'"
"And what order will we bring?"
"Only time will tell."
Spade's computer chimed, snapping her out of the memory. Her fingers danced over the keys. She'd modified the Agency's Artificial Intelligence to scour every possible digital marker for traces of 917's whereabouts. It continuously scanned security footage, bank accounts and law enforcement databases for his image. It had found something
.
The police report was fresh, it had been submitted minutes ago. According to the report, yesterday a drifter stumbled into a gas station in Nevada while a robbery was underway. The drifter shot and killed the robber. Three facts jumped out at her. The robber had been shot three times — once in the head and twice in the chest. That was professional work. Agents, like most special forces and members of clandestine organizations, were trained to shoot targets twice in the chest and then once in the head. It guaranteed a kill. No one came back from two holes in their vital organs and a bullet to the brain. The second fact was the security footage was destroyed. All of the on-site hard drives that stored the security camera data had been shot to hell.
The third fact was the one that her AI caught. The bullets that killed the robber were fired from a Beretta M9. That was 917's pistol of choice.
Any one of these facts on their own were insignificant. All three of them showing up in the same incident were too much to be coincidence. 917 had been there.
The shooting occurred less than 24 hours ago. 917 was travelling by car. Getting on a plane required ID and her systems would have pinged him immediately. Car was the only way to lay low. He had to be close.
The phone rang. It was Robert. She ignored it and went to work. She now had a trail to follow. Her mind was sharp and rested. Her nerves had recovered.
All she had needed was the one breadcrumb to start the hunt again and now she had it.

heryl Cooper glanced at the clock on the wall. Jessica would be in school for another three hours. Seemed like plenty of time to find out why this mysterious man decided to stumble back into her life a year after killing Dr. Thomas Philtzer.
She shuddered at the thought of his name. She knew Philtzer was dead, but she still woke up in the middle of the night with cold sweats. Nightmares of Philtzer hunting them down haunted her dreams. The nightmares were slowly fading with time, but they weren't gone and seeing this man tore a massive hole in the wall she'd built between her and the past.

917 looked up. He was cleaning off his plate, scraping the last bits of egg from the edges and slurping his coffee down. His body cried out for sleep. He hadn't rested since he fled Los Angeles. Despite his exhaustion, he couldn't take his eyes off this woman. There was a desperate beauty to her, an inner strength that could conquer worlds. And he remembered her resolve. He remembered the fierce look in her eye as Philtzer attacked her daughter. And he remembered the satisfaction in her face when he told her that Philtzer would be punished in the worst way possible.
"I probably told you a few. Which one are you referring to?" 917 pushed the plate away and wiped his lips with a napkin.
"That you knew my husband. He was at the bottom of the totem pole in the military. You fed me a lie that you ran Black Ops missions together. My husband was many things, but he wasn't a special forces operative. He got blown up by a car bomb within three months of deployment to Afghanistan. When you showed up, claimed to know him and wanted to protect us…well let's just say the mind chooses to overlook certain things in desperate times."
917 nodded. "You are right, I did lie. I never met your husband."
"So why help us? And how did you know about Dr. Thomas? I never told anyone about it."
Memories were coming back. His wife's name flew into his mind like a bolt of lightening.
Nora…my wife's name was Nora.
Cheryl was so different than his wife. Nora was a strong woman who lived every moment of her life as if it might be her last. Most people lived
quiet lives of desperation. Nora squeezed every second of every minute for every ounce of joy it was worth. She found eternal happiness in the smallest things. Morning coffee, snuggling under a warm blanket while the cool morning air filled the room, combing their daughter's hair. She cherished every moment as if it were a gift from God. And 917 supposed it was. The divine had blessed him with a good life and the Agency snatched it away.
He would kill them all for what they did to him.
"I was part of an organization that helped people like you. They have vast resources and seemingly unlimited money. I'm not sure how it all works but I know this much. The Agency finds criminals the law doesn't know about or can't touch. And they send people like me out into the field to kill them. Most of the time, our brilliant hackers scour the internet and investigate unsolved mysteries, look for potential rapists, serial killers, arsonists, drug dealers and decide which ones need to go. That's where I come in. I get orders. I find bad guys and I put them in the dirt."
A million questions swirled in Cheryl's head. She would ask them all in time, but there was one burning question that fought its way to the front of the line. "How did you kill Philtzer?"
"Do you really want to know?"
She closed her eyes a moment and thought about it. She'd rest easier knowing. Maybe the nightmares would finally end. "Tell me."
"I drove deep into the woods with Philtzer tied up in the trunk. He'd killed a woman years ago and buried her out there. I dug her grave up and tossed him in there with her remains. And then I buried him alive."
"Was he conscious when you buried him?"
"There's a saying at the Agency. 'The punishment must fit the crime.' Philtzer was the worst kind of monster. Before terrorizing you and your daughter, he'd killed dozens of people on the operating table. Made it all look like natural deaths. The Agency caught onto him and wanted him to suffer, not just for what he did to you but for other families as well. When I buried him, he was alive, conscious and fully alert. Being buried alive is a terrible death. The dirt was loosely packed so I'm assuming there was an air pocket or space for him to breathe. After ten minutes, the small amount of oxygen would have been used up and he would have been sucking in CO₂, which would make his lungs and throat feel like they were on fire. I gave him drugs that probably kept him awake longer. So my guess is he sucked in poison gas and soil for a good thirty minutes while his brain was deprived of oxygen and his body shut down. It probably felt like swallowing hot flames while your brain bubbled."
"Jesus…." Cheryl looked away from 917 and out the window. She hated Thomas Philtzer, but hearing the gory details made her uncomfortable.
"He deserved it."
"I know. And a part of me feels all warm and fuzzy knowing he suffered. Does that make me a bad person?"
"That's a question I have been asking myself for a long time. I don't think you are a bad person. You didn't do anything.
"
"And you? Are you a bad person?"
917 hesitated. "I've done a lot of bad things. I like to think they were all done to bad people who deserved it. I guess I'll find out when I get to the clearing at the end of the path and the Supreme Being judges me."
"You think there is a God?"
"I'd like to. If there isn't, and we are all just meat bags full of chemical reactions with no soul, then what's the point? Life can't just be a series of electrical signals in the brain. There must be something more."
Cheryl was puzzled by this man. There was something different about him. He'd changed since they last met. When he'd first arrived on her doorstep he was laser focused on his mission. It was as if his entire existence revolved around killing Philtzer and protecting her. Now, there was an invisible weight hovering over his shoulders. A quiet desperation shrouded his features. She'd seen this look in men before. This is how they looked when they couldn't confront the past and were full of regret.
"You said you needed my help. Look, I'm grateful for what you did for me and my daughter but we have a life. I can't get mixed up in anything crazy."
"The Agency…they did something to me." 917 shook his head trying to find the words. "Years ago, they stuck an implant in my brain. This implant emitted electrical signals that wiped out my memory. I'd wake up in strange hotels with no memory of who I was or how I got there. They gave me a number — 917. I didn't have a name. There were always mission instructions, weapons and equipment waiting for me. When I completed my missions, they would wipe my memory again. I was their instrument, sent out into the world to kill evil people. But then my implant malfunctioned, or maybe a better word is hacked, I was set free. My memory is coming back, but it's all jumbled. I just need a place to lay low while I figure out what to do. If I could just rest and sit in place and think, I could put my memory back together."
Cheryl hesitated and glanced at the clock again. Jessica would be home in a couple of hours. Despite being so young, she would remember this man. She would want to help him.
"There's a guest bedroom at the end of the hall. You can use it as long as you like."
917 rose from the table. "I can't thank you enough."
"You saved my little girl. I've got a debt to pay. And I always pay my debts. Come on, I'll show you the room."
917 followed her down the hall to the last door on the left. Inside, there was a double bed and an empty dresser in the corner. The room was completely devoid of decorations. It reminded 917 of the blank motel rooms he'd awakened in before missions. That was all right. Any pictures or trinkets would be a distraction. And he needed to be able to focus on recovering all of his memories.
"It isn't much, but make yourself at home." Cheryl turned to leave
.
"Cheryl, it's imperative that you don't tell anyone I'm here and don't let anyone see my car parked in your garage."
"My lips are sealed."
"And Cheryl?"
"Yes?"
"How long ago was I here?"
She stood in the doorway and stared at him. There was a lost look in his eyes. "It's been seven years."
"Seven years…." 917 turned the memory of walking up her front steps over in his mind. In the memory, he could smell the chilled winter air and heard the chirp of birds in the distance. He remembered lying to her about running black ops missions with her husband. The lie had formed so easily. He'd done so many bad things in the name of a good cause.
"Should I still call you 'Stanley?' That's what I called you before, but I know it isn't your name."
917 hesitated. His name echoed in his mind. "My name is Charles."
"All right, Charles. Get some rest. You look like you've been through hell and back."
She closed the door. Charles kicked his shoes off and laid down on the bed without taking his clothes off. Every fiber of his being screamed with exhaustion. He was asleep in seconds.
In the kitchen, Cheryl stood by the window and waited for her daughter to come home.
How the hell am I going to explain this to Jessica?
