T
he phone rang again. And for the third time that morning, Spade ignored Robert's call. She was onto something and couldn't be distracted. Robert would have nothing to offer other than nagging her to hurry up.
When 917 first went rogue, she'd deployed three agents to track him. One had arrived well ahead of the rest. And that agent had met his demise at a crappy Motel 6 outside of a bus stop in Bakersfield. 917 had filled him with bullet holes, taken his cash, and vanished. He'd been heading north along the coast, away from Los Angeles.
The other two agents had just arrived in the greater LA area. She had instructed them to sit tight and wait for instructions. Her agents weren't highly trained trackers. She was much more effective at finding people using her computer, but now she had the beginnings of a trail.
She fired off a text to the two dormant agents:
Get to Nebraska as fast as possible. Instructions coming.
From Los Angeles to Nebraska it was a three-hour nonstop flight. By car, it would take twenty-two hours. She doubted 917 had hunkered down near the gas station — it would be beyond stupid of him to get involved in a crime and then hide near the scene. She directed one agent onto a plane and the other into a rental car heading east. She didn't want both of them on airplanes if she caught wind of 917, so she split them up to stay flexible.
Her fingers furiously punched keys. The mechanical clack of her typing fired off like machine-gun fire. There was an almost spiritual plane she tapped into when her brain was firing on all cylinders — a state where she became one with her computer. She'd tune the world out and let her mind sync with digital code. Outside her apartment, New York disappeared. The only sensations she was aware of were the feel of plastic keys and the rhythm of her own typing.
Then she stopped. Her fingers froze over the keyboard. Her eyes moved to the beaten journal 917 had sent her. Sofia Vega's tragic story was written on the worn pages.
Why had he sent it to her?
What was the message?
The Agency operated in a moral gray area. She already knew that. Sending controlled killers with artificial amnesia to assassinate criminals wasn't
exactly coloring within the lines of lawful justice. But what the Agency had done to Sofia, taking her child so her skillset could be brought into the fold, made Spade wonder if she was on the right side of this. It wasn't the first time her resolve had wavered.
In the early days, she'd had no qualms. She saw evil people doing terrible things, and it brought her great satisfaction to put them in the ground. She was willing to do evil to banish it. Sometimes that's what it took. Police were spread thin. The vast majority of horrific crime went unpunished. The Agency sought to change that, to exact punishment commensurate with the crime, without bureaucratic red tape or due process to slow them down.
But there had to be lines they wouldn't cross. Otherwise, what set them apart from the very killers, rapists, and drug pushers they pursued? Spade refused to believe this was a world where everything was simply settled by whoever had the bigger gun. There was a right and a wrong. And taking Sofia's baby was wrong.
917's memory was returning. What had he remembered? If his experience was anything like Sofia's, his past was coming back in layers. The implant shutting down wouldn't reveal his life in a single flash. Memories would trickle to the surface in random sequence.
Is this the message he's trying to send me? That something terrible happened to him? That we stole something from him?
She took out a notebook and clicked the back of a ballpoint pen. Even though she was the Agency's top hacker with carte blanche access to their systems, she wouldn't put it past Robert to spy on her. In the Agency, everyone was watched. Anything digital could be recorded. The beauty of good old-fashioned longhand was that it didn't wind up in the cloud or on some hackable server. It was the only way to keep her thoughts to herself.
She proceeded to write down everything she knew about 917, her hand scribbling furiously across the page.
She'd first met him about eight years ago. His mission success rate was the highest of the thousands of agents deployed in the field. There was a network of scars on his right arm that covered the skin from wrist to elbow. Approximate age: late thirties. About a year ago, his implant had malfunctioned. His mind had decided to fight back against the artificial amnesia. They'd brought him into Agency headquarters to repair it. Robert had told 917 about his past: the tragic story of how drug cartels murdered his wife and daughter, which had led him to volunteer for memory erasure.
She leaned back in her chair and chewed on the tip of her pen. It was a disgusting habit she'd picked up in college, but it helped her think.
She picked up Sofia's journal and thumbed through the pages again. Her mind kept returning to it.
Why had he sent it to her?
Sofia's story was tragic but came without instructions, without any statement of 917's intentions. She was missing something
.
The closing lines spoke of revenge. Sofia had wanted it. She'd gotten some of it by killing a few of the agents responsible for destroying her life.
Spade went back to her computer and searched for connections between Sofia Vega and 917 in her database. After a few minutes, she determined that their paths had never crossed outside of the first meeting mentioned in the journal. They'd both been active agents for years, but their missions were always separate.
Frustration brewed inside her. It was like hitting dead ends in a labyrinth over and over. She knew there was a critical clue right in front of her, but she couldn't see it. She'd already broken one keyboard in frustration. Violence and logic don't mix. The more frustrated she got, the weaker her analytical powers became.
The worn pages of Sofia's journal were rough under her fingers. She fought back the urge to throw it across the apartment. The animalistic rage bubbling inside her whispered that it was a perfectly good idea to hurl it as far and as hard as she could. She knew it was stupid. Throwing and breaking things was unproductive. But it was a basic human instinct — what we do when we hit problems we can't solve.
Fuck it.
She threw the journal as hard as she could at the wall. It fell to the floor with a thud. And something fell out of it.
She wheeled over and picked it up. The back flap had torn open on impact, and a micro SD card lay on the floor beside it. She held the card between her fingers.
Her heartbeat quickened. Adrenaline surged.
She rummaged through her desk drawer, found the card reader, and plugged it into her computer. It contained one file. The video was titled
Freedom
.
Her mouse pointer hovered over the icon.
The phone rang again. Robert. She mashed the red button and turned the ringer off.
She opened the file. A video filled her entire screen. Green code cascaded like a digital waterfall, and after a moment she recognized it. This was the code Sofia had used to shut down 917's implant in Los Angeles.
She studied the lines as they trickled across her screen. It was genius — a simple binary sequence that canceled out the implant's primary directives. Any agent with an implant reading this would fall into a hypnotic state and shut down.
Her thoughts locked up. Her body froze. Her mind went dark.
She sat petrified in her wheelchair as memories that weren't her own flooded in, a torrent she couldn't stop and couldn't escape.
Before she blacked out, one singular thought rang through her head.
I have an implant. And it's shutting down.

R
obert set the phone down on the mahogany table. He turned to face the window of his office. Yukon sunlight came through the glass and illuminated the room. It was midday. He scanned the wilderness and collected his thoughts.
Panic was not a feeling he was accustomed to. Anger, frustration, worry, and even a tinge of stress were emotions he'd gotten used to over the years. Panic was new.
Panic was an emotion felt in the bones. The heart quickened. Muscles tightened. The jaw clenched. Sweat beaded on the forehead and formed in the palms. If panic wasn't gotten under control, it escalated into terror.
Robert was in the business of inflicting these emotions on others. The Agency didn't just kill bad people. It made them suffer physically and emotionally before snatching their lives away in the worst ways possible. It was strange to be experiencing them himself. He stared down at his shaking hands and opened and closed his fingers to calm his nerves.
Is this how death feels? A panic grips the nervous system like a magnetic wave, seizing the muscles, turning the joints to concrete and then the heart simply stops? Is this the sensation we have been injecting into our targets?
In a strange way, Robert welcomed the feeling. He'd never inflicted pain on himself, but he understood why depressed people would cut themselves. They were numb. They took a blade to their skin to feel something. The sharp cut of metal piercing skin was better than the numb, lifeless existence their life had dwindled down to.
His profession demanded he be unfeeling. All his choices were cold and calculated to forward the Agency's agenda. Feelings were never part of the equation. Over the years, he'd trained himself to think like a machine. Machines didn't make mistakes. They acted on precision and never let emotions cloud judgment. It had been so long since he felt something that he almost forgot what emotions were.
He stuffed the phone into his pocket and walked out of his office. He strode past several doors. The Agency headquarters used to be a residence. It had belonged to some eccentric billionaire before they bought it. It was one of those obscene mansions with so many bedrooms and so much floor space that the owner hardly ever saw sections of the house. The mansion
had been repurposed into a data hub where Agency hackers could deploy agents across the globe and access any digital network they desired.
Spade had told him once that there was enough fiber optic cable running through the place to stretch to Mars and back three times.
The corridors were lined with bedrooms and parlors that had been converted into workspaces. Each room was stuffed with computer servers, cables, monitors, and keyboards. Technicians worked long hours in front of glowing screens exacting justice across the globe. Usually, these missions were of the utmost interest to Robert. But right now, they all seemed trivial — like commercials interrupting a movie right before the climax.
He stepped into a room where three programmers worked. Soft yellow light came through the lone window. The rest of the room was filled with the electronic glow of buzzing monitors. He stopped behind a technician who was furiously typing away.
Agent Anders was a worker bee. He did what he was told without question. This quality was both valuable and annoying. Robert appreciated the compliance, but the lack of individual thought often irritated him.
"Good morning, Anders," Robert said.
Anders and the two other technicians looked up at him. They said nothing.
"I need to see the status of a particular implant. Can you run a diagnostic for me please?"
"Right away." Anders punched in a set of commands. A new window opened on his screen. An empty field floated in the middle. "What's the implant ID?"
Every implant had a unique serial number. It was why the agents had numbers. Their numbers coincided with their implants. If Robert wanted to see the status of Agent 917, all he would have to do was punch those three digits into the software and it would tell him everything he needed to know.
"428."
Anders entered the digits. His brow furrowed at the screen. "This is strange."
"I don't need your input or commentary." Robert's voice chilled the room. The other two technicians kept their heads down and hoped there was no wrath coming. "Put the interface up on the big screen so I can see it."
A few seconds later, a giant wall-mounted monitor flickered to life. A dashboard readout of implant 428's status and metrics filled the screen. Robert could see the person's vitals, a 3D image of their brain, and the state of artificial amnesia. There was a box in the corner with cascading code. When the implant was functioning properly, that code flowed in an even trickle of orderly binary ones and zeros. Now the code was glitching in a chaotic dance
.
Robert forced the panic down. He closed his eyes and imagined a steel box around it. He then imagined a churning ocean and tossed the steel box into the waves. It sank into the deep blue, then it was gone.
Calm returned.
He forced himself to look at the screen again. All of the readouts blinked and then went to zero.
"We seem to have lost connection with the implant." Anders typed away, searching for the signal. "I've never seen anything like this."
"That's because you have never seen an implant fail and shut down before."
Spade's implant was destroying itself. Soon she would remember everything. She would remember what he did to her… just like 917.
The situation was spiraling out of control.

loud bang woke Charles up. His eyes shot open and he reached for the Beretta M9 nestled under his pillow. He sat up on the edge of the bed and flipped the safety off. He'd thrown The Agency off his trail. How had they found him so soon?
A voice from the kitchen bled through the door. "Try to keep it down. We have a guest."
Charles relaxed. It was Cheryl and her daughter. They were doing the dishes and something must have fallen. He put the safety back on and tucked the weapon into his bag.
There was no clock in the room and he didn't have a watch, but it was dark outside. He'd fallen asleep midday. Before crashing out he'd been on the run from The Agency for 72 hours straight. By the time he reached Cheryl's front door, exhaustion had permeated through his body. As soon as his head hit the pillow he fell into a deep sleep. There were no dreams. The sleep had been so deep the hours had felt like the blink of an eye. He didn't feel refreshed. One long nap wasn't enough rest for his body to recover from three days of zero sleep and violence. His internal clock was going completely haywire. He wanted to fall back under the covers and let his body rest until his energy was replenished. But his stomach was grumbling and he felt compelled to get up and eat. Getting on a sleep schedule was the last of his priorities right now.
He got up, slipped on his shoes, and walked out of the bedroom. When he entered the kitchen, Cheryl and Jessica were just wrapping up the dishes. They stopped when they saw him.
"Jessica, you remember this man?" Cheryl asked her daughter.
Jessica nodded and cracked a small smile. She wasn't a little girl anymore. When he was last here, she was a nine-year-old. He remembered Dr. Philtzer holding the terrified little girl at gunpoint. He remembered the satisfaction of burying the good doctor alive.

A growing woman, miles stronger than her peers, for she had seen death, she had seen evil, and she'd survived a horror. It had made her strong
.

Charles eyed the clock on the wall. It was just past eight o'clock. He'd gotten a solid eight hours of sleep. He felt like he could easily pass out for another eight, his body was so tired.

.

I came here to lay low and figure out who I am."

"Before this life, I had a wife. And I had a daughter. I was a member of Delta Force. I ran missions through the Middle East hunting Taliban. I was damned good at it. I took down so many terrorist leaders that the Taliban had a nickname for me. They called me The Banshee. I guess I earned it because they thought I was some kind of supernatural ghost hunting them in the night. Truth is, I'm just a man who's really good at finding people and putting them in the dirt. Not exactly a valuable commodity outside of a warzone.
"When I returned to the States, I picked up an entry-level banking job. I hated it, but my family was happy. I paid the bills, mowed the lawn, and spent as much time at home as I could, catching up on lost time with my daughter. I loved my family but my life felt numb. I went from the high-speed bombast of the hunt to a suit and tie and nine to five. It was like living in a movie. I was playing a role and pretending to be someone I wasn't. I would sit in front of a computer at the bank for hours wearing a cheap suit and tacky tie, mentally checked out and scrolling through endless spreadsheets while my subconscious toyed with battle tactics. Physically, I was right there in the bank, but in my mind I was running through buildings with a rifle, shooting hot lead at anything that moved.

I never preyed on the weak or harmed innocent people. I only killed those who needed to be killed. And I'd be lying to you if I said it didn't feel damned good putting bad guys in the dirt.
"One Saturday morning, I was at the park with my daughter. I sat on a bench and watched her bounce around on the playground. She loved to wave at me from the top of the slide and refused to go down unless she had my undivided attention. Watching her beam with joy at the simplest things was one of the only things that would break me out of my apathetic stupor. As she hit the sand at the bottom of the slide and ran off to the next adventure, a thin man in a suit approached me."
Both girls sat in silence, unsure what they should do. Did this man need comfort? Did he need to be encouraged to talk? In the end, they did nothing but listen. And for Charles, that was enough. His story needed to be told.
"That man changed the course of my life forever," he said.

welve Years Ago
Charles watched his daughter run from the slide to the swings. Her auburn pigtails were coming undone. He'd done a sloppy job braiding them. The laces on one of her shoes had come untied and flapped wildly as she ran. Charles thought he should get up and fix it before she tripped, but so far his little girl hadn't had any spills. She'd gotten to the swings and was now catapulting into the air with raucous laughter.
It felt wrong to interrupt. He was content to sit back and watch. He'd spent so many days in the violence of war. These tiny moments reminded him what he'd been fighting for.
But why do I miss the violence? I'm sitting here at peace, watching my daughter, and all I can think about is firing a weapon, hunting bad people, and purging the planet of evil.
The thin man in a suit stuck out like a sore thumb amongst the throngs of parents and children. He walked around the playground, staring right at Charles, and made his way toward him.
Instinct kicked in. Charles's mind focused on the small firearm strapped to his ankle. He'd been out of the military for six months but never left the house without the weapon. It was a small-caliber pistol with six shots. It almost looked like a toy in his big hands, but he felt naked without it. If things ever went sideways, he'd rather have the pathetic pea shooter than nothing at all.
The man was closing in. Charles scanned the area for marksmen or bodyguards. He saw none, but got the strange feeling they were around, hiding in the shadows with sniper rifle scopes trained on him.
"May I have a seat?" the man asked.
He was at least ten years older than Charles. His dark hair was neatly groomed and the suit was perfectly pressed and tailored. He didn't look military. The haircut was all wrong and he didn't carry himself like a person who'd seen combat. Charles had been around people like this before. This was the man who sat behind a desk in a dark room and issued orders to guys like Delta Force. This was a bona fide spook who pulled the strings in the background. This was the chessmaster who killed thousands with the
stroke of a pen and never bothered to get his hands dirty by picking up a weapon.
"It's a free country," Charles answered.
"Are you so sure about that?" The man sat down on the bench next to Charles and crossed his legs. He folded his hands over his knee and looked out over the playground. Charles noted the lack of a wedding ring and a silver watch on his left wrist. The watch was expensive but not gaudy. This man oozed quiet luxury and didn't want to stand out.
"I was in the military. I fought hard to make sure it was a free country."
"Did you know that 70% of Americans live paycheck to paycheck and don't have more than $8,000 in the bank? And did you know that the average debt of the American household is $105,056? People in this country have the illusion of freedom. They live quiet, comfortable lives of desperation and all their actions are dictated by bills that appear in electronic notifications or envelopes delivered to their mailboxes."
"Ever been to the Middle East?"
"Can't say I have."
"People over there would sell their soul to the devil to live paycheck to paycheck — to live a quiet life where gunfire didn't wake them up in the middle of the night."
The man held out his hand. "My name is Robert Mitchell."
"Charles Bennet."
Robert's skin was smooth, unlike Charles's calloused hands. Hours of weightlifting and trigger-pulling had toughened his skin up. Robert worked a comfortable desk job and the most stress his fingers felt were the plastic of a keyboard. But what he lacked in physical strength, he more than made up for in mental fortitude. Charles could sense strength in the man's intense eyes. Robert wouldn't hesitate to turn a town to ash if he thought it was the right thing to do.
"So, do you miss being Delta Force?"
Charles knew this was coming. Call it divine intervention. Call it basic instinct. Whatever it was, the moment Charles spotted this man he knew his life of violence was scratching its way back to him.
"Why am I not surprised that you know who I am?" Charles asked. His daughter had grown bored with the swings and was now working her way across the monkey bars. He never took his eyes off her and was ready to pounce if things got hairy. He doubted they would. Robert was here with an offer, not a threat. That could change if Charles declined.
"You're an intelligent special forces operative. One of the best. Three tours through the Middle East. Ninety-three confirmed kills. You're proficient with a sniper rifle and have an almost otherworldly ability to find people. The US Military put that skill to use. They'd give you a breadcrumb and you'd track terrorist leaders down. Sometimes you'd pick them off from a distance, while other times… it got more personal."
As Robert spoke, Charles's mind wandered back to a mission on a clear moonlit night. He'd tracked a leader of a terrorist cell to their home. In the
dead of night, Charles and eight other men slipped into the house. They moved through the shadows killing silently with knives and suppressed rifle shots. To the terrorists, they must have seemed like a demonic wrath drifting through the home like a shadow. Terrorist leaders were never alone. They kept women and children with them. Their bedrooms were often full of children sleeping on the floors — not out of love, but for safety. Soldiers wouldn't blindly fire into a room full of children. If it weren't for the children, the military would have dropped a bomb on the house and been done with it. The best deterrent wasn't an anti-missile defense system — it was a house full of kids.
Charles remembered stepping into the bedroom. The target was asleep. Three children slept on the floor at the foot of the bed. A woman rested next to him. She woke at the creak of a floorboard. Three men with glowing night-vision goggles stood in her bedroom. She let out an earth-shattering scream. The soldier to Charles's left grabbed her and covered her mouth with a gloved hand. The target's eyes shot open. He rolled sideways and reached for the pistol on his nightstand.
Charles grabbed his wrist, twisted it backwards, and snapped the bone. The man's glassy eyes looked up at him. Charles couldn't help but smile as he plunged a knife into the target's neck. This man had been responsible for the killing of countless children. He had ties to 9/11. And now his lifeblood was gushing out onto the musty floor.
His orders had been to take the man into custody for questioning. Charles didn't care. He wanted blood, revenge for the American people. He told the higher-ups there had been no choice but to kill him. Every member of the Delta Force team backed his story.
A few weeks later, the government gave Charles a medal and he went home. Ninety-three kills. Countless shots fired. Was the world any better for it?
Charles couldn't think of a better way to live. He was the scalpel that cut a growing cancer out of the world.
Robert had an offer for him. Charles didn't know what it was, but something inside him longed for the hunt. He knew his place wasn't sitting in a cubicle at the bank, sifting through papers and loan documents until his brain rotted. God had given him a gift. And that gift happened to be killing. It was a terrible talent, but that didn't mean he couldn't use it for good.
"You seem to know a lot about me, but I don't know a thing about you." Charles scanned the playground again for signs of danger. A man in a black jacket caught his eye near the play structure. His hair was cleanly cropped and his bulky shoulders suggested he knew how to handle himself. There was at least one armed man with Robert, probably more, but this was the only one Charles could immediately make out.
"And it will always be that way," Robert said. "I don't work for the military. I don't work for the government. I work for a private enterprise and we are intensely interested in your skillset.
"
"I'm no one's bodyguard."
"Bodyguards are cheap. I don't need protection. I am looking for an assassin. And you, at your core, are a cold-blooded killer."
None of this sounded legal. But Robert had gotten his attention. Charles wouldn't be able to sleep for weeks unless he at least heard the man out.
"So what's the offer?"
"I give you missions to kill bad people. You do it and get paid large sums of money. You get richer, the world gets better."
"Sounds too good to be true."
"Maybe it is. But sometimes fate reaches out a hand and you should take it. Unless you want to live out the rest of your days working at the bank?"
"What kind of bad people?"
"Rapist, serial killers, the scum of humanity that operate outside the law. The type of criminal the authorities can't catch.
Children's laughter filled the air. Tiny feet pounded the pavement as the playground hummed with joy. This was a place of innocence. A place Charles would fight to protect. A place of dreams where tiny minds could let their imaginations run wild.
This was also the place Charles surrendered himself back into a dark world of violence.
"How do I start?"
Robert reached into his breast pocket and pulled out a card. The only thing on it was a phone number. "You'll get a call from this number sometime in the next week. Make sure to answer."
Charles took the card. Robert got up to leave. He walked over to the man loitering by the play structure — the one Charles had made as a bodyguard — and the two of them walked to a black car parked on the curb, climbed in, and drove off.
A small body crashed into his leg. His daughter beamed up at him.
"Daddy! Can we get some ice cream?" Her small voice was so innocent. It was an innocence Charles would kill to protect.
He ruffled her hair. "Strawberry?"
"Yes!"
He tucked Robert's card into his wallet and took his daughter to the ice cream parlor. The rest of the day was a blur. He went through the motions of parenting and house chores and was a shell of himself at the dinner table.
All of his thoughts were consumed by Robert and the offer he'd made. Robert was right about one thing. Charles wasn't put on this Earth to waste his days in an office cubicle.

ain was a splinter in Spade's mind.
She woke up on the floor. She didn't get up right away but lay there for a moment trying to figure out what had happened. A headache pulsed in her brain like a war cry, causing waves of stress. Soft light came through the apartment windows and painted the floor in warm orange. She usually loved the Golden Hour light, loved to look out the window and watch the trees sway in the sunset as people bustled about in the city that never sleept. But right now, the headache was so bad she could hardly see past the pain.
Her eyes snapped shut as she winced, trying to suppress the headache. She rubbed her temples and lay still as it subsided. She envisioned the crash of waves against the shore and the receding tide as the white wash melted back into the ocean. As the water settled, so did her headache. The brain-splitting pain calmed to a dull ache.
She got up on one elbow and looked around. Her apartment was disheveled. Not trashed, but the normal messy. No matter how much she
tidied up, the place always seemed disorganized. Papers were scattered across her desk. Books were jammed on the shelves at random angles and the bed was never made. The cost of hyper-productivity. Work, late nights, and furious action didn't agree with a clean house.
Her social media feed was full of online gurus prophesying that making your bed first thing in the morning would set your day up for success. There was a particularly charming influencer with a beard who claimed making his bed every morning made him a millionaire. Spade thought this was bullshit. She hadn't made her bed in ten years and her bank account far exceeded that of any online guru.
But that wealth is blood money. You didn't make it with real estate or stock options. You earned that money by killing people.
Bad people that deserved it. She never pulled the trigger but she designed the missions. It didn't absolve her of innocence. Any judge or jury worth their salt would find her guilty. But deep down, they would admire her. They would admire that she helped build her own system to exact justice against the worst people humanity had to offer. Revenge rages inside all of us. Spade simply had the courage to act on it.
At least, that's the lie she told herself so she could sleep at night.
Her wheelchair had fallen over. She must have tumbled out of it. She knew how to right it and pull herself back in. This wasn't her first spill, but her body was so exhausted she didn't want to move. She collapsed onto her back and breathed slowly, letting her body gather its energy.
She could see over the lip of her desk and up at the computer monitors.
Green code cascaded down one of the screens. It all came back to her. 917 had sent her the journal. There was a flash drive inside. When she plugged it in, the same code that had disabled 917's implant appeared on her screen. When she looked at it, her mind locked up. Her body froze. In less than a minute she lost consciousness.
That should only happen to someone who had an implant buried in their brain. She was no agent. She wasn't running around with a gun killing people. Her memory was intact. The code shouldn't have had that effect on her.
But it did. She swallowed. As a young girl, she'd been an avid reader of detective stories. The famous Sherlock Holmes quote rolled through her mind.
When you have eliminated the impossible, whatever remains, however improbable, must be the truth.
The code was innocuous to people without an implant. Back in LA, when 917 was exposed to the code so was the detective that saved him. The code had no effect on the detective. It should not have had any effect on her.
A giggle echoed in her mind. A childhood memory came to the surface. She was lying in bed. Her body was small and tucked under the covers, eyes heavy as she fought off sleep. Funny how as a child sleep was a punishment and as an adult it was often a luxury. A beautiful woman with dark hair sat next to her. She brushed the hair out of Spade's eyes and kissed her on the forehead. The kiss was warm, full of life and love. It covered her like a blanket of protection.
Spade shook her head as the next thought tumbled out of her consciousness.
That can't be my mother. My mother is blonde. We grew up on Long Island. I ran to the big city to chase big tech. What is this memory?
The hard truth was becoming undeniable. The Agency had put an implant in her head. The code had melted it away. Now her true memories were flooding back. False ones were bleeding away.
She couldn't sort through this madness on the floor of her apartment. Robert would be sending people after her. She needed to get out, needed to lay low and figure out what to do next.
The sun was setting fast. When the code knocked her out it was midday. That was at least six hours ago. Time wasn't on her side. People would be on their way. She needed to move now. Every second counted, and crippled people didn't exactly move fast
.
And then something remarkable happened. She moved a part of her body that had been dormant for years. She moved her leg. It shifted below the knee a few inches.
Had she imagined it?
She sat up, leaned her back against the bed, and stared at her toes. They were covered in white socks. She pulled them off and took a deep breath.
The brain is a remarkable instrument. Its neuropathways operate minute electrical impulses that send relays through the body. It moves faster than any computer. With a thought, the nerve endings receive an electrical pulse from the brain and the muscles move. The car accident had broken her spine. The nerve endings travelling down into her legs had been severed. Her brain's messages never made it past the waist. Fate had dealt her a cruel hand, but she was no victim. Even without the use of her legs, she wouldn't let this life go to waste. She would mean something.
Spade stared at her toes. She tried not to give herself false hope. She didn't believe in miracles. She took a deep breath, closed her eyes, and concentrated on her legs. She envisioned electrical waves from her brain's neurons travelling through her body in a powerful flow, all the way down to her toes.
Her eyes opened.
The thought brightened in her brain like a floodlight, and it manifested in her body. The reaction was instant.
All ten toes wiggled.
Tears flooded her eyes. She let them flow unchecked. She sat unmoving for a solid minute, a smile the size of Manhattan across her face as she tested her motor skills.
Her toes moved. Her knees bent on command. She grabbed the edge of her desk and pulled herself up slowly.
She stood on two legs, steadying herself against the desk. Her knees wobbled and buckled under the weight. She plopped down on the bed.
A million thoughts shuffled through her head. She did her best to ignore them and focus on what was happening in the moment. Her legs were working. She could wiggle her toes and move them freely. But she'd been confined to a wheelchair for years. Muscle atrophy had been cruel. Her legs were bone thin, like a baby giraffe's.
She ran her fingers down her thighs and rubbed them.
Tears kept coming, cascading down her cheeks in a torrent. The icy numbness that had been locked in her muscles for years was melting away. The pain felt phenomenal.
She would need to rebuild the muscle, retrain her body, but her legs were working!
Spade didn't believe in miracles, the supernatural, or God. She didn't like the idea of some old, wizened man with a beard in the clouds peering down at her from heaven. She'd seen amazing things in her life, but all of it could be explained by science.
This was her first encounter with a miracle
.
She kept rubbing her legs and crying. The crying broke into joyous laughter as she imagined herself taking a stroll through Central Park while sipping hot coffee.
Her fingers clutched the sides of the bed. She willed her legs to lift at the knee. A fireball of pain shot through the joints, but she raised her feet a few inches off the ground and wiggled her toes.
She fell backwards into the sheets and cried some more. And then the harsh truth of reality began to set in.
This isn't a miracle. This was designed. The Agency did this to me.
Her head rolled to the side and she stared at the green code on her computer screen. It should have had no effect on her. But it did. It knocked her out completely. And when she came to, her legs worked.
That could only mean one thing. The Agency had placed an implant in her head. And now that implant was gone. They had wiped her memory and reprogrammed her life to align with their purposes.
They had
made
her a cripple. Not through blunt force trauma. Not by shattering her spine or slashing her nerves. No semi-truck had ever run through her car and crushed her legs. A dime-sized implant at the base of her skull had hacked into her nervous system and cut the power to her legs. And the implant filled her memory with an artifical past to keep her from discovering the truth.
The phone buzzed again. It was Robert.
She had to move now. Once she was someplace safe, she could collect her thoughts, piece things together, and form a plan. But that would take time and space — two commodities she didn't have right now.
The Agency would be coming for her.

There are no secrets in this house. She knows the world isn't full of rainbows and unicorns. She knows there are bad people out there."

orning broke in the Yukon.
A golden sun rose over the horizon, lighting the sky on fire over the distant treeline. The heat of the coming spring evaporated the last vestiges of snow. The view was like something out of a Robert Frost poem — a landscape covered in droplets of dew and blessed with the promise of a new tomorrow.
Robert sat in the big leather chair at the head of the table in the conference room where he'd spent so much of his life. He would pace back and forth in this room, lost in his thoughts as he sent assassins out across the world on dangerous missions. Screens lining the wall would feed him real-time information about dozens of ongoing missions, future plans, and diagnostics about the Agency.
He spent most of his time alone in this room, running the day-to-day affairs of the Agency. Spade would sometimes visit him, as would other Agency staff, to brief him on mission specifics.
And on occasion, members of the Agency board would gather to discuss financing and progress. Robert hated those meetings. The board talked about the Agency as if it were a Fortune 500 company. Their chatter reminded him of wealthy investors talking about the value of their equity and stock options. The Agency dealt in life and death. It wrote the destiny horrible people deserved but that society was too cowardly to deliver. It wasn't a business venture.
But even the Agency, no matter how righteous its mission, operated on the facts of life. And the fact was that without vast sums of money from its benefactors it could not function.
Robert peered out the window and took in the cinematic vista of the Yukon wild. He propped his elbows on his knees, steepled his fingers, and pressed them against his lips.
Spade was gone. Her implant had malfunctioned. Soon her memory would return, and when that happened, his best asset would become his worst enemy. She would never forgive him or the Agency for what they took from her.
917 was a loose cannon, rampaging across the country as a rogue agent. Spade was his finest mind. Her technical skills as a hacker were unmatched.
She had been his compass in the hunt for 917. Now she was lost to him. That didn't mean he was out of options, but Spade was the crutch he'd leaned on for years to solve delicate problems like this one — and now that crutch was gone.
He'd contemplated letting Spade and 917 go. So what if they had their memories back? They couldn't prove anything. The Agency was a ghost in the machine. It could continue its operations in the shadows and none would be the wiser. Why not let Spade and 917 live out their lives in peace? Why hunt them down like dogs? Hadn't they been through enough?
The board wouldn't have it. The risk of loose ends was too great. Whether he liked it or not, he would have to end this.

Robert answered. "Sir, I've got the situation under control."
A moment of silence. The Chairman didn't immediately respond. He sat on the other side of the line and breathed heavily. The audible inhale and exhale gave Robert the creeps. When the Chairman broke the silence it was like a loud gunshot in the dead of night. "You do not. We are on our way. Prepare our quarters."
"I can assure you that isn't necessary."

Redirect all resources to eliminating Spade and 917. At least one of them should be dead by the time I get there. Spade is a fucking cripple. How hard could it be to kill a woman confined to a wheelchair with a broken mind?"

He hung up and began putting together the kill order on Spade. 917's whereabouts were unknown. Killing him would have to wait until he could pick up his trail. But he knew exactly where Spade was — at least, as of eight hours ago, before her implant went offline.
Robert wasn't picky about which agent to send after Spade. 917 was armed and dangerous. Spade was a cripple. Anyone would do. There was almost always a sleeping agent in New York City.
Within the hour, they would be picking up a weapon, studying an encrypted phone with her picture, and heading to her apartment to put a bullet in her brain.

.
"Dear God, forgive me."
With the press of a button, he sent the kill order and watched on the wall screen as an agent received the encrypted mission orders. Killing was so easy when all it took was a few keystrokes. Robert had never killed anyone personally. He hoped he never would.
The monitor on the wall chirped. The nearest agent had been found. Agent 392 was in Long Island, about three hours from Spade's apartment. His mission would be simple — drive there, hopefully arrive before she left, and shoot her.
Usually the punishment would fit the crime, but right now all the Agency cared about was tying up loose ends. There would be no theatrical death for Spade. Bullets to the chest and head would do the trick.
If Spade was gone by the time 392 arrived, things would get more complicated. He would have to find both her and 917. Disappearing in Manhattan was easy. Even confined to a wheelchair, she could melt into the city and vanish.
Robert walked to the minibar. It was well stocked. He rarely drank — he preferred sparkling water and caffeine to alcohol. His line of work required a clear mind. Right now, he just didn't give a damn, and whiskey sounded like a good idea.
The shot was fire on his throat. A buzz fizzled in his head. The sooner this nasty business was over, the better.

G
od's love. God's wrath.
Two versions of the same being. And you never know which version is going to show up.
In the New Testament, God is gentle. The divine hand comes down from the clouds and spreads love, understanding, compassion, and peace. His son delivers the Kingdom of Heaven to the meek. Jesus helps the poor, heals the lame, and treats his detractors with empathy even while they nail him to a cross.
A forgiving God.
The one we all hope visits us on our dying day.
In the Old Testament, God delivers exacting justice. When the world grew corrupt and violence filled the air, God didn't spread peace. God didn't send a loving son to change the minds and hearts of men. He summoned a holy deluge to cleanse the world. The world shook as the great flood engulfed it. Liquid death mercilessly decimated humanity. The rising oceans were unfeeling and innocent women and children were drowned along with the corrupt.
God wiped the slate clean.
Even before he became Agent 917, Charles preferred the Old Testament. Some people deserved to be put underground. If a crazed terrorist threw a baby off a roof or sent kindergartners with landmines strapped to their chests into battle, Charles didn't want to forgive them. He didn't want to reason with them or show them love. He wanted to kill them softly. He wanted to send them to the devil where unholy demons would gnaw on their flesh for all of eternity.
The Old Testament. The time when future kings were handed a sling and told to kill Goliath to test their faith. Charles would have fit right in.
Charles hung up the phone and slid it into his pocket. The phone was a burner with an untraceable number. After 48 hours it would turn into a paperweight.
He was still a man. A man with a family, a life, and a past. He hadn't lost himself to the Agency yet. In the coming weeks, his life would be taken, his name would be replaced by three numbers — 917. But even then, as a
loving father working at a bank, he knew the world needed purging. And he would be the one to do it.
Only he wouldn't bring a flood crashing down on the world. He would be precise. He'd cut the cancer out of society and the world would be better for it.
His first mission was simple.
Two women had been taken. A mother and her daughter kidnapped in the night. Save them both. Kill the perpetrator. Upon success, $50,000 would be deposited into his account. It was nearly half his annual salary at the bank.
He accepted without hesitation.
The first step was to pick up his equipment. Along with the burner phone, the mysterious man had given him a key. There was a text message on the phone that said the key matched a coin-operated locker near the train station.
Charles drove his beat-up sedan and took the first parking spot he could find. He made his way to the lockers and stopped in front of them. He fumbled for the key. The top was adorned with an orange plastic cap. Three numbers were engraved on it. He scanned the rows of lockers until he found the one that matched. He slid the key in. The lock clicked.
He opened the door slowly. Inside there was a black duffel bag. He took the bag back to his car, sat in the driver's seat, and locked the doors.
Satisfied no one was watching, he unzipped the bag.
The contents brought a smile to his face. Whoever was running this operation knew what they were doing.
Two guns were nestled in the bottom of the bag. The first was a handgun — a Beretta M9 with two magazines. It was the same handgun he'd carried in the military. It was reliable, precise, and hardly ever jammed. The second was a standard issue MP5, the compact semi-automatic used by SWAT. It had a folded stock that could be swung into place.
Both weapons used 9mm Parabellum bullets.
Parabellum was such a beautiful word. Over a hundred years ago, a German gun manufacturer adopted the motto,
"Si vis pacem, para bellum."
Translation: "If you want peace, prepare for war."
The motto became a universal declaration for military units across the planet.
He checked the weapons to ensure they were loaded. The Beretta M9 packed less punch than a .45. It wouldn't blow someone's head off or put a massive hole in their chest like a magnum. But in Charles's experience, putting a tiny bullet in someone's brain versus a big one produced the same effect — death upon impact. The Beretta M9 suppressed recoil, was built for close-quarter combat and accuracy. A design for tight shots and speed.
The MP5 was for hallways and tight rooms. It had a sensitive trigger and could fire short bursts without much recoil. It lacked range and wasn't ideal for the open battlefield, but was favored by SWAT as the perfect weapon for breaching homes and sweeping buildings
.
The moment he saw the weapons, Charles knew the Agency was sending him into close-quarter combat. He would be up close and personal, in the enemy's face. In addition to the weapons, there was a set of nondescript black pants, a long-sleeved shirt, and a pair of boots in the bag. All of it was tailored to his size and the outfit fit like a glove.
The phone buzzed. This time it wasn't a call from the Director. A text message gave him further mission details.
The two women who had been taken were a mother and daughter. They'd been taken 72 hours ago. Law enforcement had scoured the city but was failing utterly to locate them. They'd been kidnapped by human traffickers. There was no ransom, no high-profile message to a wealthy father to "pay up or else." The long-term investment of filtering the women into a human trafficking ring was more profitable than trying to extract a ransom.
The Agency had detailed intel on the human traffickers unlike anything Charles had seen before. When he was operating in the Middle East, he would be given professional target packages and dossiers packed with data on targets — full of pictures, notes on buildings, possible locations, and known associates. They were useful for creating profiles and mission preparation, but they often had gaping holes. The Agency's intel was detailed to the extreme. The encrypted text message included a schematic of the building where the women were being held, a complete inventory of how many hostiles were holding them captive, the weapons they were carrying, and the specific room the women were in.
With intel like this, he could destroy these bastards as a one-man army. It would be like a clairvoyant supernatural force descending on them.
Like God from the Old Testament.
Philadelphia boasted the worst skid row in the country. East Kensington was infamous for blocks of abandoned buildings, empty warehouses, and homes riddled with unspeakable crimes and degradation.
Charles drove slowly between the dilapidated buildings rotting in the moonlight. Prostitutes heckled him. One pulled her shirt down revealing herself while offering him a good time for a hundred bucks. There was a cigarette burn on her skin above the collarbone. The scarring looked old and healed.
He thought of his wife and daughter. It pained him to lie to them. He'd told them he was headed out for a beer with some old Army friends and would be back before midnight.
Charles hadn't been to this part of the city in years. The only people here were the homeless, violent gang members, and criminals. Police avoided the area like the plague. There was no formal agreement between these dregs and law enforcement — but everyone knew the rules. As long as the criminals kept their nasty dealings contained to this hellhole on the outskirts of the city, police wouldn't interfere. They weren't all that interested in protecting people who were busy killing themselves, and a squad car would be outnumbered a hundred to one in this neighborhood
.
He found a parking spot behind a broken-down RV. The Beretta M9 slid comfortably into a holster on his belt and tucked under his jacket. The MP5 was nestled in the bag hanging from his left shoulder. He took a deep breath and got out of the car.
If confronted with danger, he could draw the handgun and fill an attacker with bullet holes in a blink. The thought gave him a little comfort as he moved through the dilapidated buildings.
The door of the RV he'd parked behind swung open. A disheveled man stepped out wearing a suit and tie. The tie was loose and dangled from his collar. The top buttons of his shirt were undone and he was buckling his belt, putting his pants back on. The man reached into his pocket, pulled out a wad of cash, and tossed it back into the dark RV.
A soft voice whispered from inside. "Come by anytime for a good fuck."
The man swept past Charles without a glance.
Charles moved with his head down. No one here knew him, but that didn't mean he was comfortable with his face being seen.
The two women he was looking for were being held in the basement of an abandoned warehouse two blocks away. He didn't want to pull his car up and park directly in front of it — that would draw too much attention. He needed to walk the two blocks, do reconnaissance on the area, and scout the territory.
He crouched in an adjacent alley, unzipped the bag, and pulled out the MP5. The weapon had a folded stock. He pulled it back and it clicked into place. There was also a strap and he slung the weapon behind his back. The final component resting inside the bag was a silencer which screwed onto the barrel of the Beretta. He would go quiet and avoid a firefight at all cost.
The Agency intel was flawless. There were nine men in the abandoned house. Three of them patrolled on the outside. This wasn't a tightly guarded compound.
The operation was simple and twisted. These men would kidnap women, hide them in the basement, and wait for them to be picked up by human traffickers. The traffickers would show up with a van, shove the drugged women into the back, pay the men in cash, and vanish into the night. Then they would rinse and repeat, lining their pockets with more and more cash. The number of women snatched by the underbelly of the city and processed into the horrific dark world of human trafficking was staggering. And once a girl was sold, it was nearly impossible to trace her.
That's why Charles was here before the exchange. He doubted the Agency, even with its vast resources, would be able to trace the women after they were sold.
The traffickers would arrive in approximately two hours.
The abandoned house looked like something out of a horror movie. The windows were busted, the roof was sagging, and the once white picket fence was smashed to pieces.
Three men moved in unpredictable patterns. They were dressed in baggy dark hoodies and jackets and armed with cheap, uncared-for handguns.
Taking them out would be like shooting fish in a barrel. Charles watched their movements from the shadows.
The three men were clustered near the front door. Charles assumed they were strung out. Their movements were erratic and jittery. There wasn't a molecule of professionalism in how they carried themselves. Even if they were drugged out and untrained, attacking three armed men head-on was suicide.
He bided his time until one of them broke away. The biggest of the three lit a cigarette. He lumbered around the corner of the building, looking up at the night sky. The man unzipped his pants and took a leak. It was the last one he would ever take.
Charles moved with speed. He circled around the opposite side of the house, avoiding the other two men in front. He sprinted around the corners with the Beretta M9 gripped between both hands. He could hear the trickle hitting the side of the building as the man relieved himself. The red cherry of the cigarette brightened as the man inhaled.
Three shots whispered in the night. Two punched into the man's chest and one struck him between the eyes. He fell dead on the ground.
Charles stood over him and snuffed out the cigarette with his boot heel.
He looked down at the dead man. The shots were well placed and killed him instantly. There was no dramatic wheeze or dimming of the lights in his eyes. Life was taken from him in a snap and before he could process he'd been shot, he was dead.
Charles knelt and searched the dead man's pockets. He found a wallet with cash, a credit card, and a driver's license. There was also a pocket knife, a set of car keys, and a pipe covered with black ash. A meth pipe. The bullets were almost a kindness. If this bastard was smoking meth, his life was already a miserable one.
City noise filled the night. In the distance, a siren blared and horns sounded off. The cloud-filled sky blotted out the stars. Charles could only see the brightest ones — those intense enough to break through the light pollution. He thought one of them was the center of Orion's belt but couldn't remember. When he was on active duty, they would often hunt targets deep in the desert. There was almost zero light pollution out there, and those desert skies were some of the brightest and most magnificent in the world — a blanket of sparkling jewels shimmering above them like some kind of ethereal spell. Such breathtaking beauty hovering a few miles outside the most war-torn cities on the planet.
Charles moved on. Gravel crunched quietly under his boot heels. His shoulder hugged the wall as he crouched low. When he got to the edge of the building, he risked a peek around the corner. The other two men were now sitting on a bench near the front door, passing a joint back and forth. One of them had a pistol openly resting on his lap. The overconfidence brought a smile to Charles's face.
He glanced down the street. There were a few prostitutes a couple blocks down but no one in the immediate vicinity. This was the perfect time to
strike. Charles stepped out, aimed, and squeezed the trigger. The man with the pistol on his lap died first. A red dot bloomed between his eyes and his head snapped back. Blood splattered across the face of his companion. Before he could move, a volley of fire drilled him in the chest and one caught him in the face.
Neither body fell. The two dead men slouched on the bench. The joint they'd been passing fell to the ground, a thin trail of smoke rising from it. It took three minutes to drag the bodies into the shadows around the corner of the building. Charles tried the doorknob and it was unlocked. Before entering, he loaded a fresh magazine into his weapon. There were shots left in the old one, but he wanted a full set before going inside.
Six tangos to go.
His heartbeat was steady. Years of high-stress environments had honed his body into a surgical weapon of war. Bombs, blood, pain, and death didn't shake his nerves. Adrenaline pumped through his system, but he was in control of every movement.
He slid into the building and found himself standing in the dark. There was a staircase that led downstairs to the basement and another which went upstairs to a corridor. He could see light coming from a couple of rooms upstairs. And there was laughter.
According to the schematics the Agency sent him, he was on the ground floor and it had four rooms — a living room, a kitchen, a laundry room, and a bathroom. He would need to sweep all four.
There was a movie playing in the living room. Charles could see the flicker of white from the TV splashing into the hallway. Someone was watching Scarface. Al Pacino's voice reverberated off the walls, followed by the gunfire spewing from his "little friend." Good. The noise would help mask Charles's movements.
Before he could pick a direction he heard a toilet flush from behind a closed door. That made things easy.
His footfalls were silent as he moved down the hallway. The soles of the tactical boots were crafted for silent movement and fell softly on the wooden floor. He stood in front of the door and aimed his weapon roughly where he thought the man's head would be.
The door swung open.
Al Pacino let out a volley of fire and the machine-gun blast from the TV filled the house.
The man inside the bathroom froze when he saw Charles and raised his hands. There was no discussion. The 9mm bullet punched through the man's forehead and exploded out the back. Brain matter coated the dirty tiles. He crumpled like a sack of potatoes. Charles pushed his legs inside and closed the door.
Five men left.
Intel said the women were downstairs. He fought the urge to go down there next. The two women needed him, but if there were armed men
upstairs the last thing he wanted was to be pinned down in a basement with armed men descending on him.
The corridor connected to the kitchen. Charles entered with his gun raised. The lights were off and the kitchen was empty. The door on the opposite end led to the living room where the movie was playing. Charles heard laughter.
He stepped into the living room. A skinny, strung-out man sat on the couch holding a beer. A rusted AK-47 rested on the cushion next to him. The guy was so fixated on the TV he didn't notice Charles had entered the room.
Three more shots. Two in the chest and one in the head. If the police ever ran forensics on this place it would be obvious a professional had done this. In every special ops unit on the planet they trained soldiers to put two bullets in the chest of a target. Center mass was a lot harder to miss than the head. After the two shots struck home, the third in the forehead was insurance.
Four men left to kill.
He moved like a wraith up the staircase and paused at the top. A floorboard creaked and he froze, listening for movement. Down the hallway were several rooms with the lights off. At the very end, one room had a crack of soft light coming from under the door. Charles heard laughter again. There was more than one person in there.
He perched himself outside the door and leaned his ear close to the frame. There were at least two men bickering, and he could make out the whimper of a woman. Charles knew there were four armed men left in the building. Two of them were in here. Before he busted down the door and went in hot, he wanted to know where the other men were. If he had a team this would be different — half his men would breach downstairs while the other half swept the rest of the house. They would move with speed and kill the armed tangos in under thirty seconds.
This wasn't a military operation.
There was no team.
Charles was on his own, so his tactics were different.
Breaching a room as a single man was risky. The element of surprise was on his side, but he would still be outnumbered. And if one of them got a shot off, or if the woman inside screamed, it would alert the others and he'd be in trouble as they converged on him. Worse, in the human trafficking industry, men had been known to panic and start killing the women when cornered. If he started unloading bullets, the men in the basement could get trigger-happy and kill the two women he was supposed to save.
He moved past the door and scouted the rest of the corridor. The remaining rooms were empty. Assuming there were two men in the occupied room, that left the other two downstairs. Option one — breach the room on the top floor and pray he could do it quietly, then deal with the men in the basement. High risk. There was a woman in there and he didn't intend
to harm her. If she screamed, the men below would be alerted. Option two — breach the basement first, secure the women, then kill the men upstairs.
Charles decided on option two.
He made his way downstairs. The basement door was shut. It was thin, but he couldn't hear anything from inside. He'd studied the floor plans before the mission. He knew behind this door was a set of rickety stairs leading down into the basement. It was a huge tactical disadvantage. If the men below knew he was coming, they could concentrate their fire on the staircase and he would be a goner.
He tried the door handle. Locked. The lock was crude, but he didn't have the tools or the time to pick it.
Before he could form a plan, a blood-curdling scream blasted through the thin door. It was the voice of a young girl crying out for help, pleading to be left alone. Pure desperation. These women had been taken 72 hours ago. They knew their fate and she was now lashing out with nothing to lose.
Through the door, Charles heard a man yell back at her. "You know what, I'm going to have a nice fuck before we ship you off."
More screaming from the women.
It was time to go hot.
Charles pulled the stock of his MP5 into place and nestled it against his shoulder. With one swift kick, his foot connected with the door next to the lock and the wood splintered open. Speed was imperative. He'd have to pray the men below weren't near their guns and he could put them down before they got any shots off.
The staircase was only six feet long. Charles got to the bottom in under a second. The scene was horrific. There were two dirty mattresses on the damp ground. A woman lay on one and a young girl on the other. They were both handcuffed, dirty, and terrified. A man stood over the young girl in the middle of taking off his pants.
Another man stood in the corner watching. Charles fired at him first. The MP5 let out a short burst. The bullets turned him into a red mist. His lifeless body slid down the wall as he fell, leaving a streak of blood behind.
The man standing over the young girl turned. He wasn't armed and his pants were half down. He raised his hands in surrender. The MP5 was loud. The two men upstairs had heard the shots by now. It was a risk worth taking. It was a firefight from here on out. Hellfire was about to rain down on these bastards.
Charles swept the leg of the man standing over the young girl and he fell on his back.
"Please…" Tears welled in the man's eyes as he lay on his back, arms raised, pants halfway down his legs. Charles almost felt sorry for him.
Almost.
The MP5 fired, turning the man's face into a bloody crater.
The women whimpered. Before freeing them, he needed to deal with the two men upstairs. He could hear their footfalls as they ran down
.
Charles positioned himself underneath the staircase in the shadows. He crouched low and aimed the MP5 upward. Within seconds the final two men appeared. Charles waited until they were both on the staircase and then squeezed the trigger.
He emptied the magazine into them. They both fell dead and tumbled down the stairs.
All tangos were down. Nine men killed and Charles didn't have a scratch on him. It felt good to be back in the fray and out from behind a desk.
The women were shaken but alive. They were crudely confined by handcuffs secured to pipes in the wall. Charles didn't have time to pick the locks. He fired a bullet into the chains, blowing them apart, and both women were free.
First, the women hugged each other and sobbed. Charles let them have their moment, but they couldn't linger. Curious eyes might come around after hearing the gunfire, or the human traffickers could show up early.
The mother thanked him. Through gasps and falling tears she asked, "Are you a cop? Are you alone?"
"Not a cop, but I'm here to rescue you. We need to leave. Can you two walk?"
The mother stood still, a look of confusion and shock spilling across her face. She didn't trust him. Charles didn't blame her. She'd been through a nightmare. There would be time for empathy later, but right now he needed to get them out of here.
He grabbed the mother's shoulder. "We don't have time. I'll explain later. We are almost out of this. You can trust me. Stay close. My car is two blocks away."
Charles held the MP5 up and moved up the stairs. The women followed. They stepped over the two bodies and the blood-slicked stairs. When they got to the top, Charles paused and listened. The house was still and empty. He turned to the little girl clinging to her mother's arm.
She was nine years old. Too young to have seen any of this. At nine, the world was supposed to be full of playgrounds and Saturday morning cartoons and the simple, burning question of what flavor ice cream to get. Life's cruelties were supposed to be years away — a distant shore she hadn't yet reached. Instead, the ugliest corner of the world had reached out and grabbed her first.
Charles prayed she hadn't been harmed before he arrived. He would never know for sure. But he knew this girl's life had been forever changed. He did his best to sound calm and imagined speaking to his own daughter when he addressed her.
"Listen to me, it's almost over. I'm taking you home. You and your mother need to stay close. We're going to move fast. Keep your eyes on me and just keep walking. My car is two blocks away."
The girl nodded. There was a touch of defiance in her eyes. That was good
.
The three of them exited the house. Two bodies were sprawled out on the bench out front. In the dead of night with low visibility they almost looked peaceful. Charles doubted anyone in this neighborhood would call the police in the middle of the night, but the last thing he wanted was flashing red and blue lights pulling up on him.
A couple of hookers cat-called him and quickly turned away when they saw he was armed. They got to the car without incident. Charles put the women in the back seat and started driving.
"I need your home address."
"Why?" the mother asked.
"I'm taking you home."
"Not the police station?"
"I just killed nine people with unregistered weapons. They were bad people. They deserved it. But I'm no cop and I'm not about to pull into a station. I'm taking you home."
The mother gave him the address.
It was a thirty-minute drive to their house in the suburbs. The home was nestled in a cul-de-sac. Charles parked out front and turned to the women in the back seat.
"You aren't a cop?" The mother was shivering, clutching her daughter tightly.
Charles shook his head. "No. And I know I said I would explain, but that was a lie. You'll never know my name or what I do. The less you know the better. All I can say is that I am a good person and you'll never see me again. Take your daughter and go home."
Without hesitation, the women got out of the car and ran up to the front door. It was the dead of night. The doorbell stirred whoever was inside. Through the front windows, Charles saw lights turn on. The front door swung open and a tired-looking man answered.
The women embraced him. The family was back together. The three of them stood there in the doorway holding each other.
Charles pressed on the gas and drove off into the night. On the way home, he dumped his weapons into the river.
His personal cell phone chimed with a notification. $50,000 had been deposited into his bank account.
His lips cracked into a wide smile.
Mission accomplished.
That night, he slept like a baby. The next morning, he quit his job at the bank.

gent 392's memory was a blank slate.
Earlier this morning, he woke up in a hotel room in New Jersey. There was an encrypted phone sitting on his bedside table. It unlocked after he read a string of strange words. Even with no memory, the action felt familiar and he supposed most of his missions started out this way.
The mission instructions were concise. A dangerous cyber-terrorist in Manhattan needed killing. She wasn't armed, and confined to a wheel-chair.
The Agency wanted this done quickly. There was no time for elaborate theatrics. Eliminating the target was secondary to any punishment they deserved.
That was fine with 392. Though he had no memory, he got the impression his usual missions were designed to ensure the targets were punished commensurately for their crimes. This mission would be a refreshing change, even if it would be erased from his mind when he was done.
A Toyota Corolla waited for him in the parking lot. There was no better car to drive if you wanted to be invisible. The keys were nestled on the back passenger tire.
392 popped the trunk. It was empty. He opened up the false bottom where the spare tire was stored. There was a small, hard-case bag resting inside the tire. He unzipped the bag and pulled out the small pistol. It was loaded and there were two spare magazines. It would only take one small caliber bullet to kill a woman in a wheel-chair, but the more ammo the better.
The drive from New Jersey to Manhattan took an hour. There was some light traffic to navigate as he entered the city. When he got to the target's address he stopped on the curb and craned his neck up at the apartment building.
The building was at least twenty stories, modest for this part of New York. The front door was locked and needed a touchless fob to open. His cell phone buzzed. There was a text message from the Agency.
Touch the phone to the fob scanner.
392 tapped the phone on the black box next to the door. A small red light flashed from red to green. The door buzzed and the internal lock opened
.
Once inside, he got into the elevator and took it to the eighth floor.
He found the apartment he was looking for and took a moment to assess the situation. He was alone in the corridor. The city was buzzing outside and he could hear the hum of cars and pedestrians going about their day. There was no sound from behind the apartment door.
Speed would make up for any lack of subtlety. He could breach, shoot, kill and be gone in under three minutes. There was a security camera down the hall, but that didn't bother 392. Doubtful anyone was watching the camera feed live and it was probably recording somewhere. His face and identity had been erased from every digital database on the planet. The Agency turned him into a ghost with no fingerprints, no name, no memory or connections.
392 drew his pistol and tried the door. It was locked. He took a step back and with a swift hard kick smashed his foot into the wood next to the handle.
The lock gave and the door swung open.
392 swept the apartment. There was no one home. And there was no wheelchair. His target was gone.
He scanned for any clue as to where she had gone. Perhaps he had been too hasty and she stepped out for groceries or coffee?
His gaze fell on the computer desk in the corner. There were three computer towers on the floor beneath the desk. The hard shell cases had been pulled off. Hard drives littered the ground. 392 crouched down to get a better look. There was a power drill under the desk. Each drive had several holes drilled through them.
He pulled out his phone. There was only one number in his contacts.
A masked and distorted voice answered. "Is it done?"
"She's gone. Drilled holes in all her computer drives and fled. From the looks of it she left in a hurry, might have packed a small bag. The drawers in the bedroom are open and there are clothes strewn about. No sign of a wheelchair."
There was a long pause. 392 could sense the anger hidden in the silence.
"Get out of there, await further mission instructions."
392 hung up. He made a final scan of the apartment and then left. Not knowing what to do, he got back in his car and drove downtown. His stomach was grumbling and he decided to grab shawarma while he waited for further instructions.
