W
hat is my real name?
Foreign memories flooded through Spade's head. Memories of a life long forgotten, memories that she knew were real but seemed to play out on a mental movie screen as if they had belonged to someone else.
She remembered being a little girl eating ice cream. The sun had burned bright. She sat on a swing next to a beautiful woman at a playground. Chocolate chip swirled in her mouth. The woman's fingers twirled in her hair and they laughed and laughed like there was no tomorrow.
When she was sixteen, a boy kissed her. Her prom dress furrowed around her legs in magnificent pink folds. It was her first kiss. The boy was tall and handsome. Dark hair and dark eyes and a soft smile. His hands were gentle around her hips. Her eyes were closed and heat pulsed in her chest. She knew she didn't love this boy. He wasn't the one. But she was in love with the idea of being in love. It was a confusing time. But that was all right. Why rush into being an adult? It seemed so stressful. Right now all she wanted to do was kiss this boy and explore his tongue. Her father would be mortified. Her mother would want all the details.
As it all came back to her, a singular question continued to ring out.
What is my real name?
It couldn't be Stephanie. That life was a lie. The car accident, the loss of her ability to walk; all of it had been implanted into her mind. Her disability had been nothing more than a psychosomatic trick.
She needed time. She needed space. With those two precious commodities, she could close her eyes and think. Put pen to paper, journal the past into ink and decipher the labyrinth of information churning in her head. She wanted to write long hand. Years of hacking into digital journals people foolishly thought private made her leery of typing anything personal into a computer.
But she had no time. She had no space. The Director, The Agency…they were coming for her. She was a loose end now like 917. Once 917 was broken, the Agency unleashed their resources to track him down and kill him. They would do the same to her. 917 was a master assassin, a force to be reckoned with. She was a computer hacker confined to a wheelchair.
917 had a fleeting chance of evading capture. What chance did she have
?
Leaving New York was risky. Despite The Agency knowing she was here, the city still felt safe. Even with her mind half broken, she knew the city well and being invisible inside this beehive of people wasn't too hard. People did it all the time. But most of those people could use their legs. She could wiggle her toes, but years of muscle atrophy rendered her legs inert. She would have to build the muscle back up. Who know how long that would take.
She'd left the apartment hours ago.
It had taken her less than twenty minutes to leave. If her legs worked, she could have gotten out of there in less than five. Like every good spy, she had a bag packed with everything she would need to go on the run. There was a safe in her bedroom closet. Inside the safe, there was a duffle bag filled to the gills with $100 bills, a handgun, two magazines, fake IDs, passports and other miscellaneous items she might need.
All she needed to do was change her clothes. Before her mental breakdown, she'd been wearing the same pair of sweats for three days and slaving over her computer trying to track 917 down. She wiggled her way out of them and into a fresh pair of jeans and t-shirt. Once dressed, she removed the gun from the bag. There were two slim pockets under each arm rest of her chair. She tucked the weapon into the one on the right so she could draw it with ease.
There was a storage pouch on the back of the chair. It unzipped and the bag of money fit snugly inside. There was a lock which secured the pouch and after snapping it closed, she tucked the key into her pocket. She took the journal and flash drive 917 sent her and put them in the left hand pocket opposite the handgun.
Satisfied everything was in place, she found her power drill and proceeded to destroy her computers. She didn't want to leave any breadcrumbs for Robert to follow. He must know her implant was malfunctioning. He was likely monitoring it the same way she monitored 917's. But that didn't mean she wanted him having any of her data or research on 917's whereabouts.
After her computer station was destroyed, she took one last look at the apartment. It was a false life, a life the Agency manufactured for her, but that didn't mean it had been all bad. She loved her workstation. And loved the city view through the nearby window. She loved the way the light poured through that window during the autumn days with a soft tint of orange and the way the rain would pitter patter on the glass as she slept.
She closed her eyes and said a silent goodbye to this apartment. The future was uncertain and full of mystery. She didn't know what tomorrow held for her, but one thing was certain—she wouldn't be returning to this place.
For good measure, she locked the door on her way out.
Manhattan has the complex nervous system of a living being. You only see half of it from the surface. Skyscrapers kiss the clouds. Streets thrum with people and traffic. And it never sleeps. It is never quiet. The volume
tones down at night, but there are always pedestrians and cars moving about the city. What you don't see is the vast network of subway train tunnels, miles and miles of cabling woven beneath its sidewalks and the bottomless subterranean infrastructure. There was an entire civilization of people that lived underground in those tunnels. New Yorkers called them the Mole People. About a year ago, she'd sent 917 into those tunnels to find those people and get their help in a mission to rescue a young girl from a satanic cult.
The city was easy to blend into, but it was also a surveillance hotspot. There was a camera on every street corner. She knew The Agency systems well. It would take a few keystrokes for The Agency to turn every one of the cameras in this city into a looking glass honed to track her face. And being stuck in a wheelchair would make her stand out even more.
Twenty-one bridges and fifteen tunnels led in and out of the city. The Agency would be monitoring them all. Each bridge had its own unique set of problems and she didn't see a way to get over them undetected. They were covered in cameras and every face in every car could be scanned as people traveled over. And if she was caught on a bridge, there was no running. Not that she could do much running in her wheelchair, but if she was going to be spotted it would be better on the city streets than being pinned on a bridge.
The tunnels were equally challenging. Every train was jam packed with cameras. She'd hacked into the security systems countless times to monitor targets and run missions. The array of surveillance was impressive. Entering the tunnels was a sure-fire way to get caught.
The best plan was to leave by boat.
Her apartment was in the middle of Lincoln Square. The 79th Street Boat Basin was twenty blocks away. The sun was bright. The crowds were thick. She wore a Yankees baseball cap and sunglasses. She doubted it would do much to hide her identity from the searching eyes of the Agency, but it was better than nothing. They would be scanning camera footage looking for anyone in a wheelchair around her apartment. An agent was likely already on their way to kill her.
She pressed the joystick on her wheelchair. The tiny motor hummed as she crossed the street. Throngs of people buzzed around her. She took comfort in the crowd. It provided the appearance of cover. It also made her anxious. She half expected a killer to emerge from the crowd with a weapon in hand. She wouldn't put it past the Agency to kill her in broad daylight. A well trained assassin could cut her throat or put a bullet in her head and vanish into the city before anyone knew what was happening.
Spade didn't believe in God. She was certain that whoever she was before the Agency stuck an implant in her also didn't believe in a higher power. Faith, religion, belief…these were things that were so innately part of your DNA that no memory wipe or artificial amnesia could totally erase them. And Spade knew she didn't have them. Her mind was built on tangible logics; she only used information she could touch, feel and see to make ch
oices. But that didn't mean there weren't lessons to be learned from every walk of life.
She'd met a priest years ago. They'd gotten into a friendly debate about the existence of God over coffee in Central Park. It was a fun sparring match. Father Daniel was trying to change her perspective, get her to see the divine hand of God at work in the universe. She argued it was all randomized chaos and the only order was the result of people trying to survive. They disagreed on just about everything but there was a lesson that stuck out to her. She could hear Father Daniel's raspy old voice in her head.
God, grant me the serenity to accept the things I cannot change, courage to change the things I can, and wisdom to know the difference.
God or no God, she couldn't argue with that. When faced with a problem, it was always best to focus on the controllables. Staring at things she couldn't do anything about only made things worse. Focus on what you can control. Use those things to bring about the best outcome. It was a philosophy not only for the faithful, but for every lost soul out there trying to survive.
The battery in her wheelchair would last a full day. After that, it would need to recharge for a few hours. She reached the boat yard with plenty of battery left.
She left the crowded sidewalks of Manhattan and rolled towards the docks. This was not a tourist hotspot. There were roughly forty boat slips here. She hoped to find a fisherman who was done for the day, someone she could bribe. She found her man right away.
A rundown fishing boat was docked and tied up near the entrance. A greasy man stood in the back of the boat in big black rubber boots. His skin was leathery from harsh years out at sea. If you looked up fisherman in the dictionary you'd see his picture. He wore a tattered beanie, had a full beard and his eyes were world-weary. But they still had a shine to them as if each new day was an adventure. And Spade supposed for the old fisherman, each day was a new adventure. Even if he'd navigated the same fishing routes all his life, Old Man Sea always found something new to show you.
Spade carefully rolled her wheelchair along the docks. The water was calm, but the gentle ebb and flow of the tide still caused tiny warbles in the dock as it moved with the waves.
She called out to the fisherman. "You have fuel in your engines?"
He looked up and paused spraying his boat down. His brow furrowed as he examined the crippled woman. "I used a lot of it this morning, but there's enough."
"Enough to get me upstate?"
The fisherman eyed her carefully. She could practically hear the gears in his head turning. "You in trouble, Miss?"
There was no point in lying. And this fisherman seemed like the type that would take pity on her. She probably looked pathetic and in desperate need idling on the docks in a wheelchair. "The worst kind. Terrible people are after me and I need to get off this island. I have cash.
"
He nodded, as if this all made perfect sense to him. "How much?"
"Two grand now, two grand when you drop me off somewhere up the Hudson."
"The Good Lord would never forgive me if I denied a woman in need. Let me lower the gangway and I'll help you aboard."
Thirty minutes later, Spade was aboard the fishing boat, coasting along the Hudson. The deck reeked of fish guts and the railing was covered in bird shit, but the boat was sound. The captain knew the waters like the back of his hand. The shore was shrinking in the distance as the ship blended in on the Hudson. The river was chock full of ships and saw a regular flow of daily boat traffic. She let her nerves relax ever so slightly as she planned out her next move. It would take about three hours to get to their destination. Once she was back on land, every second would count. But for now, she leaned back in her chair, took in the city view and formulated a plan.
Across the city, Agent 392 gobbled up the last of his shawarma downtown. If he'd arrived at Spade's apartment an hour earlier, he would have found her helplessly sprawled out on the floor. An easy shot. Mission accomplished and he could go back to sleep. But he'd missed her.
For the time being, fate was on Spade's side.

harles leaned forward, his forearms rested on the kitchen table and his hands were wrapped around the coffee mug. It was empty. He'd been drinking coffee non-stop since he woke up. Three cups? Firing a weapon without steady hands was stupid. But sitting here, in this kitchen with Cheryl and Jessica, he felt comfortable letting his guard down. And as he spoke it was like a weight slipped off his shoulders. He'd harbored these secrets for too long and they were eating away at his soul.
Charles looked up at the clock. It was the early hours of the morning. His internal clock was all messed up. He'd been up for 72 hours running from The Agency and then slept in Jessica's guest room for fourteen. His body ached from laying down for so long, but he was far from well-rested. Bodies are frail things. Charles was in impeccable shape, but his body felt completely drained. And no matter how strong or fast he was, a bullet to the head still killed him like anyone else. He was no super soldier. This wasn't a comic book. He knew more blood would be spilled before this was over. And some of it would be his.
"Thank you for listening to me drone on and on," Charles said. "I know it's late and I've just barged into your lives totally unannounced."
This time it was Jessica who answered. The young girl had fire, Charles would give her that. "We would both be dead without you. You are always welcome in our home."
He cracked a smile and nodded in appreciation. "You want to hear the rest of the story?"
"Bet your ass," Cheryl said. "This is a good one and you can't leave us on a cliffhanger.
"

There was no denying she was attractive. Hell, she was stunning. But this wasn't a love story. This wasn't some kind of romance where Charles snuck away from the Agency and built a life with this beautiful woman. The longer he stayed, the more potential danger he put these two women in. It was probably a mistake sharing all this information with them, but there was no turning back now. And there was an unmistakable truth Charles was grappling with.
I'm not going to make it through this alone. I've been alone for so long and my soul has paid the price. If I take one more step forward by myself the darkness will consume me.
He needed friends. Was it selfish to drag Jessica and Cheryl into this? Maybe. But what was done was done and he felt a companionship forming. It was something strong, something intangible that might just help him get out of this alive.
"I quit my job at the bank. The Agency would contact me through the encrypted phone. About once a month, I would receive a set of mission orders. They were all very similar. A terrible person would need killing. The missions would be meticulously planned and I would be deployed to assassinate the target. I hunted killers, drug dealers, rapists and every type of criminal you could imagine. These were people the law had missed or wouldn't touch. It never weighed on my conscience. You don't lose sleep over killing a human trafficker who smuggled five year olds into the sex trade. And the money was good. I was getting 50k deposited into my account after each mission and making near five times my old salary. And I felt I was making a difference, using the skill set the US Government had invested so much in.
"I told my family I'd gotten a private security job working for some high-profile celebrity. I needed to travel around and protect him once a month. They pried and tried to figure out who it was, but eventually accepted that I wouldn't tell. They enjoyed the money. They also enjoyed that I was home all the time save for when I was out on missions, so the questions stopped. Life was good. But I knew it wouldn't last. And it all came crashing down like an avalanche.
"The mission orders came in like they always did. The text message laid out the details. Within hours, I was on a plane to Los Angeles. There was a man there who needed killing. His name was Xander Truss. He was a well connected drug dealer, smuggling copious amounts of heroin along the Western Seaboard. The law wouldn't touch him. Agency intelligence uncovered that Xander was an FBI informant. They were letting him operate with immunity in exchange for information about the cartel. The FBI considered this the lesser of two evils. The heroin pipeline Xander was running would be capped in its expansion, but would never be shut down as long as cartel members were being arrested. It kept the war on drugs on the front page of the LA Times, made the FBI look like heroes while
Xander lined his pockets and the flow of heroin would never explode out of control.
"This didn't sit right with the Agency and it damn well didn't sit right with me. So, I loaded up and went after Xander. As usual, the Agency intel was flawless. Everything had been meticulously planned. All I had to do was follow the directions and the mission would be a success.
"Xander lived on a yacht off the Los Angeles coast. He'd moved there a couple of years ago for safety. Even though he was protected by the FBI, there were other threats outside of the law. Being at the top of the food chain isn't as safe as it sounds. Everyone is always trying to topple the king. The yacht was loaded with armed guards and you'd have to be crazy to attack it; crazy or incredibly well-prepared and trained. Xander had lived there for a few years without incident. Prolonged comfort and safety can make even the most vigilant person drop their guard.
"The Agency provided me with all the fancy toys I would need to execute my mission. They gave me a diver propulsion vehicle which is basically a motorized water scooter that pulls you underwater. And I also had a complete Navy Seal kit with a rebreather that suppressed all air bubbles. I could move through the ocean waters like a ghost.
"I left the shore just after midnight. The swim was easy with the propulsion vehicle. I pulled up next to the hull of the ship just under an hour later. Getting onto the boat would be the hardest part. The yacht had three anchors, each with a massive chain linking it to the ocean floor. I had to climb up one of them. To avoid being seen, I had to climb upside down with a full kit of weapons strapped to my body. The climb took me twenty minutes. It felt like an eternity. Once on board, it was easy to find Xander. I crawled up a chain that connected to the boat near his personal living quarters. I could see his big flat screen TV playing through the window of his cabin.
"There were two guards patrolling his cabin at all times. One would roam the decks and the other sat in front of the cabin. Killing them was easy. This was a cushy job. They got paid handsomely to stand around on a yacht and carry weapons while looking tough. No one was crazy enough to attack…until I got there.
"Agency intel detailed the roaming guard's route. I knew exactly where he would be and could predict his movements. I took him down without incident."
Jessica leaned forward. She was totally immersed in Charles's story. "Like you punched him? Or you killed him?"
Charles sighed. He had no business telling this story to a teenager, but there was no turning back now. And Jessica was no ordinary kid. She already knew the world was a bad place. Might as well tell her the whole truth. He was done with all the lies.
"I snuck up behind him and killed him with a knife. A quick jab to the neck and he went down. In the movies, guys will sweep buildings using pistols with suppressors and no one seems to notice. Suppressors
muffle gunshots, but they still sound like gunshots. A suppressed pistol shot sounds like a large textbook being dropped right next to you. Far from silent and a good way to get caught. Being cornered on a yacht full of armed men with no backup was a death sentence, so I avoided using a firearm until the last minute.
"When I got to Xander's personal quarters the guard I was expecting was sitting on a chair in front of the door. He was crouched over his phone and doom-scrolling. There was a large window opening up to the living room inside. A couch faced a wall mounted TV. Xander was sitting on the couch with his back facing me. His head and shoulders popped up above the top of the couch. Mission Impossible played out on the screen. Tom Cruise was scaling the famous skyscraper in Dubai.
"I did some quick math. I would shoot the guard. Even with a suppressed pistol, there was a strong possibility Xander would hear. Taking the guard out with a knife would take too long and time was more precious than silence. I could shoot the guard, keep moving and close the gap to Xander in less than five seconds. He'd be dead before he knew what hit him. And within seconds, I could get to the edge of the yacht and dive off. No need to climb back down. I just needed to get to the water, put on my rebreather and the propulsion vehicle would zip me out of there. A clean getaway.
"The guard didn't notice me until I was four feet in front of him. His social media rotted brain pulled away from the phone and he looked up at me like a deer caught in headlights. I shot him and moved into the cabin.
"As I entered, gun raised, Xander turned. I took aim and was about to pull the trigger when I saw the little girl. A girl snuggled under his arm. She was no older than ten and fast asleep. Xander glared at me. His eyes moved from the gun, to his sleeping daughter and back to the gun. There was a fierceness to him. This was a man who had built an empire and climbed the ladder as a drug lord by killing everyone that stood in his way. He didn't cower away from me, but he didn't attack either. He knew I had him. But he was also a father. And I could sense the plea in his eyes, the silent begging to spare him because of his daughter.
"I'd killed a child before. I didn't have a choice. It was during the war. We were fighting terrorists, religious fanatics, and a boy charged a military unit with an explosive vest strapped to his body. I shot him before he could mash the detonator. I don't know if that kid believed he was doing the right thing or if his family was being threatened and he was forced to suicide bomb us. But it came down to letting that kid run into my friends and killing himself while taking out US soldiers, or me just shooting him dead right there. I didn't hesitate. That doesn't mean I liked it.
"This was different. Xander's girl wasn't a product of war or religious zealotry. She didn't choose this life. It wasn't her fault her father was a drug lord about to be assassinated. And as I stood there, gun in hand, pictures of my own daughter flooded into my mind. I thought of her holding me after I'd been shot dead. I imagined her pain, her torment as her old man
died in her arms and all the memories she never got to make being taken from her.
"I ran. In the end, I couldn't do it. It didn't matter that Xander was a drug lord. It didn't matter that the Agency wanted him dead or that he was corrupt or that the FBI was in on it. Killing him wasn't worth destroying that young girl's life. So…I ran. Twenty seconds later, I was catapulting off the yacht and sailing through the air towards the ocean. I cut through the surface like a knife, found my gear and got the hell out of there.
"Two days later, the Agency came for me."

ometime in the Past….
Xander's yacht shrank behind him.
The diver propulsion vehicle hummed in the water. His head was above the surface as it dragged him through the salty Pacific. He felt the liquid underworld teem with life. In the dark, he could hardly see anything, but he could sense the pulse of life all around him. The ocean was an inky black. He imagined a massive shark shooting up from the depths and swallowing him. They often mistook divers and surfers for seals.
His heart raced. The distance between him and the yacht was growing rapidly. He could hear the yacht wake up. Xander was up and about barking orders to his men. They were getting on speedboats to look for him. Their efforts would be in vain. Charles was in a full Navy Seal diving kit. The Agency equipment was state of the art. He aimed his vehicle down and it pulled him beneath the surface. With his rebreather, he could travel miles underwater with ease. His rebreather also suppressed all his air bubbles, effectively making him invisible.
Charles approached the shore long before Xander's boats were remotely close. He'd pulled up to the wild, rocky shore far from the docks of touristy beaches. It was the dead of night, but he was taking no risks of bumping into people. The duffle bag with dry clothes was right where he left it. He stripped off his gear, dumped all his weapons and changed. The cool night air bit at his skin. Gooseflesh broke out. He barely noticed his teeth chattering as his mind raced. Once he was in dry clothes he rummaged the bag for his wallet and two cell phones. One phone was his personal line and the other was the encrypted phone The Agency provided.
He climbed up the rocks and made his way towards the highway. There was a diner in the distance, its glowing neon sign beaming that it was open twenty-four hours a day. It was a four mile hike to get there, but Charles needed the time and space to clear his head. Once he arrived, he ate a meal and pulled out The Agency encrypted phone. There was only one number saved in the phone.
He sent a message.
Couldn't do it. Couldn't kill Xander in front of his daughter.
I'm sorry.
Minutes passed. He scarfed down more food and stared at the phone on the table, waiting eagerly for a response. Ten minutes passed. Twenty. Thirty.
No calls. No text.
Charles didn't know what to make of it.
Two police officers entered the diner. Charles dipped his head down. He had a handgun tucked under his shirt. He'd left the rest of his fancy equipment on the beach. If these cops were looking for him, there weren't many options. The officers sat down at the counter and ordered hot plates. They didn't take any notice of him.
Charles took out his personal phone and ordered an Uber. It wouldn't arrive for another twenty minutes. He slipped into the bathroom, took his gun apart and dropped all the pieces into a trash can. He paid his check in cash and waited outside for his Uber. It arrived twenty minutes later. He climbed into the back and set the destination to the nearest airport. It was an hour drive. Charles closed his eyes and tried to think.
The driver had been working this gig for a couple of years. He knew when a passenger wanted to chit-chat or be left alone. This one wanted silence, so he kept his eyes on the road, his mouth shut and the music off.
Sleep was impossible. Adrenaline coursed through Charles's veins. His heart started to calm, but his system was in fight or flight mode.
All he could think about was his family. It was a Tuesday evening. They were sound asleep. Nora had a list of errands for the next day stuck to the fridge with a magnet and Audrey was probably sleeping on the couch, snuggled under a warm blanket. She was crossing the bridge between being a child and a teenager and sleeping on the couch was her way of being trendy and rebellious. She was growing like a weed. Charles could remember it like yesterday when she was a chubby toddler sneaking into his bedroom in the middle of the night, seeking comfort after a bad dream. Now she came up to his chest. Her Barbies collected dust in the corner of her room and her desk was littered with makeup. Soon she would be off to high school. Boys would be coming to visit. He wondered how he would handle that.
He checked the encrypted phone. No messages from the Agency. He didn't know what to make of that. Something told him they weren't the forgiving type.
A horrible sense of dread filled his chest. It moved through him like an apparition and a cold chill nestled in his body. The driver pulled up to the airport and Charles clambered out of the car.
It was two in the morning. Back home, the clocks were two hours ahead. He called his wife. With each ring, the icy chill pulsed larger and larger in his chest.
Nora finally answered. Her voice was exhausted and full of concern. "Charles? Is everything all right?
"
"I'm on my way home. Listen to me very carefully and I'll explain everything when I get there. You need to pack a bag. Do it now. Get Audrey and get to a hotel."
"What's going on, Charles?" The sleep in her voice was gone now. She was alert and dialed in. "You are scaring me."
"Everything will be okay if you move fast and trust me. Remember the motel a few blocks away?"
"The Red Flamingo? The dingy place we can't believe anyone ever stays at?"
"Pack fast. Take Audrey and go there. In the bedroom closet on the top shelf there is a shoe box. In that box, there is a pistol and 10 grand of cash. Take the cash and the gun. When you get to the hotel, bribe whoever is at the front desk to give you a room without providing your names or ID. It's imperative you don't give your name or ID."
If The Agency was keeping tabs on them, their computer systems would flag his wife's ID or credit cards popping up in any networked computer system. Charles couldn't risk it.
"Charles, tell me what is happening right now. I'm not moving a damn muscle until you give me an explanation."
He took a deep breath. "I've gotten us into trouble. My new job isn't exactly legal. Things went sideways and I'm moving you guys as a precaution. I'm at the airport and I'll meet you at The Red Flamingo. Bribe your way with the cash into a room. Once you are settled, add 5 to your room number and text it to me. If you are in room 20, text me 25. Understand?"
"How far away are you?"
"I'm at the airport getting on the first flight I can. Do you understand everything I've told you?"
"Charles… I'm terrified. My hands are shaking."
"I know and it's going to be okay. I don't think you guys are in danger, I'm just being extra cautious. Pay cash, remember to add 5 to the room number and after you send that text, ditch your cell phone. Throw it in the pool or something. Understand?"
There was a pause and Charles could make out a soft sniffle. His heart was breaking. He had brought this madness into his home. He worked to stay calm. Regret, remorse and reflection could come later. Right now, he needed to make sure his family was safe.
There was a long pause. Charles broke it first. "I love you, babe."
"I know." After seeing Star Wars and watching the iconic scene where Princess Leia confesses her hidden love for Han Solo, his wife always responded this way when Charles told her he loved her. Even now, when her world was about to fall apart, she was still being playful with him.
"May the Force be with you. I'll see you two soon."
Charles hung up. Before purchasing a boarding pass and going through security, he dumped the encrypted Agency phone in a garbage bin. They could likely track his personal cell, but there was no point in making
himself easy to find. And he needed his phone for the final text from his wife with her motel room number.
He had to use his ID to get on the plane. There was no avoiding that. All he could do was hope to vanish in the crowds when he arrived and get to the Red Flamingo without incident.
Charles leaned back in his chair and scanned the airplane. It was a full flight. Passengers were packed tightly. He'd read an article in the New Yorker about how airlines worked to fill every plane, canceled flights that were half booked and strategically maneuvered the system to fill every seat. They maximized profits while price gouging the American consumer. You were more likely to win a small amount of money playing the lottery than to get a full refund on an airline ticket.
Capitalism was the American way. And with every airline working in conjunction with the same policies, the consumer never stood a chance when trying to change a flight or get a refund on a ticket. The only choice if you wanted to fly across the country to see your old Aunt Gertrude was to pony up a few hundred dollars, slap it on a travel rewards card for a few travel miles that were impossible to claim and slip into the packed tin can of an airplane like a drone.
Charles sat in the window seat. It wasn't ideal. Two people filled the middle and aisle seat to his left. If things went sideways, he was pinned in against the window. He thought about asking to switch with the guy in the aisle, but decided against it. If The Agency wanted to kill him on this plane, being in the aisle seat wouldn't make a big difference. There wasn't anywhere to run. And he didn't think there was a threat here. They'd been in the air for an hour and people were reading magazines, watching movies on the small TV screens and napping.
Napping sounded nice. The mission was taxing. The diving equipment had enabled him to traverse the ocean without much physical exertion, but his mind had been firing on all cylinders for hours. The surge of adrenaline was beginning to fade. The inevitable physical crash was starting to set in. His body cried out for rest, but his mind was screaming in fear.
When he closed his eyes, horrible pictures materialized in the darkness of his subconscious. His family dead on the floor of his living room. Blood splattered across the walls. Bullet holes in his little girl. Armed men raiding his house before he got there.
The steward brought him an herbal tea. It helped to calm his nerves.
They are okay. They grabbed the cash and are holed up at The Red Flamingo. You'll get the text when the plane lands. Nothing you can do now. Body needs rest.
When Charles was in the military, his sergeant had a hard and fast rule. When they were down range in the battlefield and the opportunity to nap arose, you took it. You never knew when your next chance at getting shut eye would come again. It was good advice. He'd once leaned up against a wall in full gear with his rifle in his lap and napped for four hours. He went the next thirty-six hours without sleep in the heat of battle with bullets
flying. That four hour sleep gave him the burst of energy he needed to survive.
War was different. His family was never at risk. They were always oceans away, tucked safely in the States. Now, the battle was at home.
He managed to sleep the rest of the flight, but his dreams were red and terrible. When the plane landed he jolted in his chair and startled the passengers around him.
He apologized. "Sorry, I hate flying."
The man next to him cracked a half-smile and nodded. Most people hated flying, but he could tell Charles was under a lot of stress. The flight was over and they would be getting off in a minute. There was no point in further conversation. They were about to part ways, never to see each other again.
Charles pulled out his cellphone. There was a text from his wife. It was a single number — 45.
He let out a sigh of relief. They'd made it to The Red Flamingo. Nora was in room 40 and had added 5 before sending it, just as he'd asked. As soon as he got the text, he dropped his phone into a trash bin and walked outside.
There were rows of yellow cabs at arrivals. Charles climbed into the first one and gave the driver the address. The ride was only twenty minutes but it felt like days. Every second elongated and painful. When they got to The Red Flamingo, Charles shoved some cash into the driver's hand and told him to keep the change.
The motel was a dilapidated mess. Faded green paint covered the building. Pink neon lights lined the lobby windows. Three cheap flamingo statues were jammed into the astroturf in front of the motel. They were painted red. The paint was faded and peeling from years of neglect and harsh rain.
This was the kind of place you did a drug deal in or brought a hooker to. It was one of the last places the Agency would be looking for his family. And the unsavory guests weren't dangerous.
He found room 40 and knocked on the door. At first, he didn't hear anything. It was early morning. The sun was peeking its head up over the horizon. Maybe they were asleep?
He knocked again and listened.
Shuffling came from behind the thin door. The peephole flashed black and then clear as someone on the other side looked through.
The door swung open. His wife stood before him. Her hair was thrown up in a messy bun and her eyes were red from crying. She'd never looked more beautiful in her life. He spotted Audrey under the covers, fast asleep.
His wife slapped him. The blow was soft, but cut deep into his soul. Tears welled in her eyes. She'd been crying for hours and thought she didn't have any tears left, but she was wrong. She threw her arms around him, buried her face in his chest and sobbed.
"I'm sorry, Nora." Charles squeezed her. "I'm so sorry.
"
He closed the door behind him and locked it. The Agency was coming. He needed a plan, but right now his family was safe.
And that was all that mattered.

Nora. I will kill them for what they did to you. I will kill them all.

ometime in the Past…
The Red Flamingo wasn't as bad as Charles thought it would be. It was no Ritz Carlton, but there was nothing unsanitary about the place. The carpets were clean. The furniture was cheap and stiff, but functional. The room had cable TV and there was a teardrop pool. It was the tail end of winter so the pool was cold and empty, but its existence made the place feel a tad bit more upscale than it was. There was a complimentary breakfast spread with hard boiled eggs and an array of croissants. An instant waffle maker gathered a line.
Charles loaded up a couple of plates and walked back to the room. Once inside, he and his family scarfed down breakfast.
They were all exhausted. He was too, but was on edge to sleep. Throughout the night, he half expected the motel room door to blow open and armed men to raid the room. Nora managed a few hours of sleep after her husband arrived. It was a broken and restless sleep. Her muscles felt drained as she raised a croissant to her mouth.
Nora looked up at her husband. "What now?"
Disheveled hair and red eyes; she'd looked like she'd been through hell. And Charles supposed she had been. After his arrival the previous night, they'd stayed up late and talked in low voices while their daughter slept. Charles explained everything to her. She listened without reaction as he told her about his first meeting with the Director, his missions, the large payouts…he didn't leave any detail out. When he was done talking, she closed her eyes for a few moments.
Charles could practically hear the gears in her head turning.
She'd opened her eyes, took his hands and asked, "Now, what do we do?"
This woman is one in a million. Even after all this, after all the lies she is still in my corner.
She believed in him. She stood by him. It was real faith. Holding onto belief even after it fails you.
"I haven't worked that part out yet. First, was getting us all to safety. Thank god you found my stash of cash back at the house. We have about 10 grand. We can make that stretch. I think going back to our old lives without some kind of resolution is risky. I don't know how the Agency
will react. Every mission I did before was executed perfectly and I haven't had any contact with them since I ran."
Audrey finished her croissant. She'd been watching TV. "Daddy, you said they had waffles."
His daughter was growing into a teenager. Soon, she would be a woman. The boys would start knocking on his door. It was an unavoidable part of life. That didn't mean he had to like it. Audrey hadn't questioned him too much. All this was a sort of exciting adventure for her. She always loved hotels and leaving the house. He wondered how she would react when she realized her old life was gone. She would likely never see her friends again. Unless he could broker some kind of deal with The Agency, they would need to start over. New names, new social security numbers and new lives. It could be done, but it wouldn't be easy.
"Dad! Waffles!" Audrey demanded. Her eyes were glued to the TV. She was watching some teenage rom-com. No matter how stressful the situation, his daughter's teenage hormones and adolescent rebellious attitude found a way to rear its head.
He kissed Nora on top of her head.
"I won't be long."
"Get me a waffle too."
He winked at her and headed back to the breakfast room. When he entered, the Director was waiting for him.
In the corner, at a round table, the Director sat alone. There was a coaster with a full coffee mug on the table. In his hand, he held a folded newspaper and was reading. He was wearing a dark suit and fedora. He stuck out like a sore thumb. Most of the guests moseyed about in sweats and yoga pants with unkempt hair. The Director was the epitome of refinement and his movements were sharp and precise.
Charles scanned the room. The Director appeared to be alone.
Charles took a seat across from him. "Hello, Robert."
The Director dropped the newspaper. Charles glanced at it and saw it was open to the Finance Section. The S&P 500 was down and Wall Street was upset about it. For as long as he could remember, Wall Street was in some kind of panic. It didn't seem to matter if the market was crashed or booming, those finance people were always on the verge of a heart attack.
"Are you here to kill me?" Charles asked. His heart was slamming against his chest. His family was alone back in the room. There was a handgun in the drawer. Nora had grabbed it with his stash of cash. Years ago, he'd taken her to the range and taught her how to shoot. She could handle a firearm, but he doubted she was sitting by the door on guard with the weapon in hand. If someone barged into the room to kill them, she'd never get to the weapon in time.
"I don't have a desire to kill you." The Director creased his paper and dropped it on the table. He picked up his coffee and took a sip, swallowed and set it back down. He steepled his fingers and leaned his chin down on his thumbs. "But that doesn't mean I am happy about what you did.
"
"I wasn't going to kill him in front of his child. I know he was an evil bastard and the world would be better off without him, but that child didn't deserve to have her father's brain matter splattered across the floor before her eyes. If I become a heartless killing machine, then how am I different than the targets I hunt?" As he spoke, he calculated his options and looked for weapons. The hotel provided metal cutlery but there was nothing but forks and dull butter knives. Those could be deadly up close and personal, but knowing the Agency there was a hidden gunman somewhere nearby and Charles would be shot down before he could do anything.
"I have a question for you, or rather…let's call it a test." The Director took another sip of his coffee. He took a moment to collect his thoughts before speaking again. Charles's mind kept racing back to the hotel room and his family. He thought about demanding to know if they were safe before answering any more questions, but he thought better of it. There was a slim chance the Director didn't know his family was here, so he kept his mouth shut.
"And if I pass?"
"All is forgiven. Your family will be safe. We move on and I give you another mission."
"What if I pass and I don't want to go on any more missions?"
"You are hardly in a position to negotiate."
"What are you going to do? Hurt me? Shoot me dead in the middle of breakfast in front of all these people?"
The Director's face was unreadable. Charles was trying to get under the man's skin, but he remained stoic. "Do you really think I couldn't kill you right here and right now? Eye witnesses, security cameras, local law enforcement, the government, FBI, CIA…none of them have any power over me. They are part of a broken system. I say the word and you are dead. The hotel guests scream and run. The security footage is wiped within minutes. Police response time would be at least fifteen minutes. I'd step over your dead corpse, walk to my unmarked car in the parking lot and take a nap as the driver took me to the airport. But I know you don't want to die. You are a family man. What would happen to Audrey and Nora if you weren't around?"
Charles gritted his teeth. His blood boiled. He wanted to leap across the table and strangle the life out of this man. "And what happens if I play your little game, give it my all and I fail?"
"The road to hell is paved with good intentions, Charles. There are consequences for everything." The Director thumbed the edge of his newspaper. Mind games were his specialty. There was nothing more invigorating than mental chess and he rarely lost. "So, let's begin. There are an estimated 8 billion people on the planet. Most of these 8 billion people go about their daily lives without much thought to the global picture. Their sole purpose is to make ends meet, pay the bills and put food on the table. Maybe have a couple of kids and save some money. It's not much of an existence really.
Mankind has so much potential, yet he spends the majority of his short time on earth providing a roof and food for his kin.
"A small percentage of the human population is psychotic. This small, crazy portion kills other humans. They kill other humans through violent acts such as murder or on a larger scale on the theatre of war. You've been to war, I don't need to remind you how unpleasant it is. And The Agency targets the small portion of our population who want to become serial killers, rapists or human traffickers.
"It is a noble mission, a mission I want you to be a part of, but the truth is this small psychotic population of mankind that specializes in death actually only accounts for a small portion of the loss of human life on a global scale. Serial killers, murderers and war are responsible for roughly 500,000 deaths a year. Heart disease and stroke make these numbers seem like nothing. An estimated 15 to 18 million people die a year from heart disease.
"If I could eliminate heart disease I would. But that isn't my mission. My mission is to kill bad people. Bad people took something very precious from me and I have a personal stake in punishing every last serial killer and rapist in this country. But I digress.
"Let's get back to the test. There is a six year old girl. She has unique DNA. There is something in her genetic code that contains the cure to heart disease. With it, doctors could cure heart disease once and for all. 15 to 18 million lives a year would be saved. But here's the kicker — in order to get the genetic code from this girl you have to kill her. It's the only way the procedure works. I give you a handgun with a single bullet in it. And I tell you to save millions upon millions of human lives, but in order to do it you have to shoot this innocent girl right between the eyes. Would you do it?"
Charles sat still. He did his best to look calm even though he was in a total panic on the inside. His mind kept racing back to his family. Did The Agency know they were here? It would have been a smarter strategic move to send them elsewhere, away from him. That would have been safer, but he wanted them close.
"No. I wouldn't do it."
The Director sipped his coffee. "You disappoint me. Yes, the killing of even one innocent young girl is a tragedy. But to save millions of lives, I would strangle the life out of her with my bare hands. History is made by the strong doing terrible things for the greater good."
"I want out. I never want to see you or anyone from The Agency ever again." Even as Charles spoke, he knew it was hopeless. These people didn't answer to anyone. The Director had already told him there would be consequences for his actions.
"That isn't up to you. Despite your recent foul-ups, my superiors are impressed by your natural talents. You have combat experience. We don't let men of your caliber go. You belong to us."
"I don't belong to anyone.
"
The Director shrugged. "If you say so. You have no idea the power The Agency holds. There is no limit to its reach, its resources are endless. We are the shadows and we are everywhere. With the click of a button I can send trained killers to eliminate any threat with absolute anonymity and precision. This is a power not even the great governments of earth possess."
"How does the Agency have such power?"
"A terrible thing happened to me, Charles. About twenty years ago, I was a young father like yourself. I'd built myself a real estate empire and was worth billions. On a trip through South America, my daughter was kidnapped. She was held for ransom. I paid ten million dollars to get her back, hired all the best negotiators. In the end, the kidnappers took my payment and sent my daughter back to me in pieces."
For the first time, The Director's demeanor changed. His normally stoic face hardened. His right eye twitched ever so slightly and there was a hatred burning in his eyes. "Can you imagine what that is like? To have your child sent back to you in parts? My wife went insane. She threw herself off a bridge the next day. And I was alone with nothing but money. I almost put a bullet in my mouth and probably would have in the coming weeks if The Agency hadn't approached me.
"The Agency gets its power from private industry. Becoming a billionaire isn't the safest thing in the world. Jeff Bezos spends upwards of 20 million dollars a year in private security to keep his family safe. But even with all that private security, kidnappings and killings happen. Just a month ago a crypto billionaire was kidnapped in the middle of the night, right under the nose of his private security. He was tortured for 17 days with power tools before finally submitting and turning his fortune over to his captors. Rich people are just people with money. And when they are wronged, they want revenge like anyone else.
"Billionaires who lost family, who were hurt by the vicious underbelly of humanity, pooled their resources and formed The Agency. They built it through shell companies and funneled limitless resources into creating an organization capable of operating outside the law, fully in the shadows, with the sole purpose of killing bad people. We are here to make sure the punishment fits the crime. We balance the scales.
"My family was dead. The life I once knew was gone. The Agency asked me to take over as head of operations. I handed over my assets to The Agency and took on the role of Director. And now, I oversee day-to-day operations wiping scum from the face of the earth. Will it bring my daughter back? No. Will I ever cure something as massive as heart disease? No. But I sleep like a baby at night knowing some people out there don't deserve a fair trial. They just need to be shot and put six feet under."
The rabbit hole went much deeper than Charles could ever have anticipated. If what The Director was saying was true, The Agency would never let him walk away. They needed secrecy to operate. He was a loose end
.
"So what now?" Charles asked. "I wouldn't kill the girl to cure heart disease, just like I wouldn't kill Xander in front of his child. I failed your test. I'm no use to you."
The Director turned his head and looked out the window. He stared at a pair of hummingbirds hovering over the flowers. He kept his eyes on the window as he spoke. "Go check on your family, Charles. You have been gone a long time."
His heart stopped. The Director knew. He knew this whole time his family was here. Charles bolted from his chair and ran. The Director sat patiently and finished his coffee before getting up.
Charles blew through the double doors leading out of the breakfast room. His family was in a room across the hotel in a separate building. As he ran, his eyes darted back and forth looking for threats. Nothing jumped out at him, but any of these people could be working for The Agency and carrying concealed weapons.
He got to his family's building and shot up the stairs. There was no time to wait for an elevator. Their room was on the fourth floor. When he entered the hallway his heart was pounding. Sweat poured down his forehead. The hallway was empty. The door to his room was slightly ajar. The thin wood of the door was splintered inward. It had been kicked down.
Charles was unarmed. There was nothing in the hallway he could use as a weapon. He didn't have time to go look for something to defend himself with. His family was in that room. He approached slowly, staying close to the wall and listening.
The sound of Audrey's TV show eked out into the hallway. The artificial laughter of a live audience bounced off the walls.
Charles stepped inside, fists clenched and ready for a fight.
Two armed men stood inside. They each held a silenced pistol pointed at his girls. His eyes stole a glance at the dresser a few feet to his right. The top drawer was shut. That was where he'd stashed his pistol. Could he get to it before these men executed his family?
A small circle poked the back of Charles's neck. The Director stood behind him and pressed the metal barrel of his pistol into his skin.
"Don't do anything stupid, Charles." The Director said. "Now, sit on the bed."
Charles walked slowly, hands raised. He sat on the edge of the bed closest to the dresser where he prayed his pistol still rested. When the opportunity struck he would reach for it and shoot these men.
Audrey and Nora were in tears. They were on their knees in the corner with their hands on the floor and their fingers fanned out. Less than five feet in front of them the Agency men held their guns steady.
Nora lifted her head and looked at Charles. Her eyes were glazed. She was going into shock.
"It's going to be all right," Charles said.
The Director scoffed. "Don't make promises you can't keep.
"
"Please…I'll do anything you want." Charles begged. "You want Xander dead, consider it done. I'll run missions for you, I'll kill whoever you want, please just don't hurt my family."
The gun barrel bobbed lazily in The Director's hand. "You failed my test. You wouldn't take one innocent life to save millions. You see, The Agency isn't trying to rule or shape the world. All we are trying to do is cut out the cancer. People are divine. They are blessed with free will and given the opportunity to do whatever they please. Unfortunately, some of those people have chosen to exercise their free will to harm their fellow man in the most brutal ways. And we are the punishing arm of destiny. We have set out on the impossible task of burning out these evil, cancerous people so that the majority of good people can thrive. We are doing our part to let the world shape itself, without the horrible influence of unspeakable crimes. The police and so-called government agencies aren't getting it done. The Agency was born out of necessity. It was born because good men didn't have the stomach to get their hands dirty.
"Have I become evil? Maybe I have. But I am not without direction. And my cruelty is precise and aimed at those who would kidnap and murder."
Charles felt the weight of every passing moment. With each second that ticked by, he felt he was losing his chance to fight back. He kept his eyes fixed on The Director. As long as the man was talking he wasn't shooting. Charles didn't see a way to get to the firearm tucked in the dresser.
"I thought you and I were the same," The Director said. "When I first met you on that park bench you seemed like a man of progress, a man who knew the ends justified the means. Which is why you were willing to get your hands dirty. And then you went soft and the world eats people who go soft. But that doesn't mean you don't have a valuable skill set that The Agency needs, a skill set that can do a great deal of good in this world. If you are worried about your family's safety, I'm going to give you the opportunity to protect them."
Faint sobs came from the two women. Charles looked over at them. Audrey was frozen stiff, like an animal caught in a pair of headlights bearing down on her. The shock in Nora's face was melting. Cognizance returned. There was a silent plea in her eyes.
"What do you want me to do?" Charles asked.
"I didn't bring you back to your room to execute your family in front of you. Contrary to what you might believe, I am not a barbarian. I brought you here to say goodbye. From now on, The Agency owns you. You are our property. You are coming with us and will execute missions at my command for the rest of your natural born life. You will never see your family again. So, say your goodbyes and we are leaving."
"Please—"
"Don't protest," The Director cut Charles off. His patience was wearing thin. "This is a generous offer. Your wife will receive an automatic deposit of $250,000 into her checking account once a year. They will be taken care of, but this is it…this is the last time you will ever see them.
"
This time it was Nora who spoke. "Don't…don't do this. Don't take him away from us."
The Director crouched low in front of her. He looked into her dark and beautiful eyes. "I wish I didn't have to. But your husband has proven to be disobedient. And if he knows the only way to keep you safe is to obey, then he will. It's the only way, darling."
He took his index finger and brushed the hair from her eyes.
"Don't touch her!" Charles took a step forward. One of the armed men raised his gun. Charles lifted his hands and stepped back.
"Charles, do something!" Nora demanded. "They can't take you. I won't let them."
Charles stared helplessly at his family. There was no way out of this. Leaping towards the gun in the dresser was too risky. The armed men would kill them all before he even got a shot off. He would need to wait for another opportunity.
The Director checked his watch. "We are leaving in 90 seconds. Go ahead and say goodbye. You won't get another chance."
Charles fell to his knees. His wife and daughter embraced him. They both squeezed and sobbed into his chest. Nora whispered into his ear. "Do something…please, do something. Don't let them take you."
He held her face and kissed her. "You've got to let me go. I'll find a way out of this, but right now you've got to let me go."
One of the armed men grabbed his arm and yanked him backwards. His wife and daughter clung to him. The second armed man pulled them off. Both women started crying.
Audrey leapt forward. A rage exploded inside her. These men were not going to take her father. They were not going to destroy their lives. She would fight and claw and scratch their eyes and make them bleed before she let them take her father.
She drove a heel into the closest man's foot, then she sprinted at the second one who stood there with a gun in hand. He raised the weapon and took aim at the charging child.
Nora dove in front of her, tackling her to the ground. The two of them rolled across the floor.
A deafening gunshot blasted through the air. The man aiming at Charles's daughter had squeezed the trigger. The silencer muffled the gunshot, but the hot streak of the bullet left sizzling air in its wake. The bullet struck Nora in the left side of her head and punched out the right.
She fell dead in a puddle of blood.
Audrey screamed. All sanity left her mind. Her mother was dead. Her father was about to be taken from her. This was a fight to the death and she would either win or die in the attempt.
Both armed men stood stunned.
The Director shouted. "You fucking idiot! I didn't want her blood on my hands!
"
Charles grabbed the man who'd shot his wife. With one hand, he gripped the man's throat between clenched fingers and clamped down on his windpipe. With the other, he grabbed the forearm of the hand gripping the pistol and slammed it against the wall. The man's grip was steady and the gun didn't fall.
Charles didn't relent. He was more animal than man now. He let ancient fight or flight instincts take control of his body. Tearing this man apart, ripping his eyes out and ending his life in the worst possible way dominated his thoughts.
They crashed into the window pane near the front door. Glass fell as both men went through. When Charles hit the pavement, hot liquid splashed across his arm. Shards of glass had turned the skin on his right forearm into ribbons. A six-inch blade of glass stuck out of his forearm, dozens of smaller pieces punctured his skin. He didn't know if it had struck a vital artery or not.
"Stop!" The Director shouted. "Make one more move and I will put a bullet in your daughter's head."
Charles looked up in horror. There was shattered glass everywhere. The man who'd killed his wife was unconscious on the floor next to him. His head was split open. Blood pooled.
The second armed man held his daughter. The Director pointed a gun at her head. His wife was unmoving on the floor behind them. She was gone.
"I didn't want her to die. But the difference between you and I is that if I had to kill a handful of innocent people to save millions, for the greater good I wouldn't hesitate. Is it unpleasant? Yes. But it is necessary."
Charles got to one knee. He was dizzy with blood loss. "Mark my words, Robert. Before the sun sets on this life, I will kill you. I will kill you horribly and terribly. It will be the stuff of nightmares. The punishment will fit the crime."
The Director raised his hand and brought the butt of his pistol down on Charles's head.
Everything went black.
When he woke up, he had no memory.
OceanofPDF.com

60
C
harles was crying. He sat at the kitchen table in the middle of the quaint Nebraska home and broke down. The final pieces of the puzzle were falling into place. His memory had returned.
The memories were overwhelming. The whisper of the gunshot that took Nora's life. His daughter's muffled screams. The shards of glass cutting into his arteries. It was all happening to him again. The terrible memory was playing on loop over and over again in his mind.
He looked down at the scars on his right arm. The mystery of how he'd gotten them was finally resolved.
The two women sat across from him in silence, not knowing what to do to comfort this strange man.
Cheryl reached out and clutched his hands. "My husband was taken from me too."
"I know," Charles said. "I remember. He died in the war."
She nodded and fought back the tears. She hadn't cried over her late husband in years. When he died, she'd mourned him. But she knew her husband wouldn't want her to mourn him for the rest of her days. There was so much life to be lived, so much time ahead of her, he wouldn't want her to waste it all grieving. So she'd done her best to make a good life for Jessica. To protect her from the cruelties of this world and to find joy in the smallest things.
"When my husband died, it was like a piece of me died with him. Everyone tells you they understand, but unless they have lost someone close they don't. How could they understand when their loved ones are standing right next to them? This is a beautiful world. It's full of wonder, but it can also be terrible and cold."
"It's funny, having my memories come back," Charles said. "I am remembering the tragedy, and that hurts. But I am also remembering some of the good. Before bed each night, I used to read my daughter stories. She was obsessed with The Lion, The Witch and The Wardrobe and if the book didn't have a wizard or a dragon inside she hated it. But there's a passage from a special book that sticks out to me now."
Charles closed his eyes. He remembered being a father sitting on the edge of his daughter's bed. Audrey looked up at him with eyes full of wonder. She was full of so much innocence. He knew protecting that innocence
was impossible. She would grow and the world would break her like it broke everyone else since the dawn of time. Getting knocked down was inevitable. It's what we do after we get knocked down that defines us.
He recalled the feel of the paperback in his hands. The spine was cracked from years of page-turning. The book had belonged to his mother and now it belonged to his daughter. A Wizard of Earthsea by Ursula K. Le Guin. The famous line reverberated in his memory and he spoke it aloud to Cheryl and Jessica.
Charles spoke. "'Only in silence the word, only in dark the light, only in dying life: bright the hawk's flight on the empty sky.' It means you can only know a thing because it has an opposite. We only know what is cold because we have felt heat. You can only truly know love after feeling hatred. Pleasure and pain are two sides of the same coin. The Agency took my family, they took my life from me. But they didn't take all the good. That good is still in me. I can feel the warmth of my wife even though she is gone.
"But I don't have it in me to forgive and forget. I know Jesus said to turn the other cheek but I'm ready to go Old Testament. I'm going to burn the Agency to the ground and God help anyone who gets in my way."
Cheryl was taken aback by his sudden change in mood. This man used to have a name. It was Charles. But she wasn't so sure Charles was back. The professional assassin who'd put Dr. Philtzer in the ground all those years ago was sitting across from her now. And in that moment, she was afraid of him.
"Do you think destroying The Agency, killing The Director will give you peace?"
Charles's eyes were glassed over, devoid of emotion. "No. But it isn't peace I'm after. I don't think I'll ever be at peace. There is too much blood on my hands. The Gates of Heaven will never open for me. I'm going to settle for the next best thing."
"And what's that?"
"Revenge."
The END

Book One - The Body
OceanofPDF.com

1
T
he body was found at sunrise.
The dead man sat in the sand, leaning against a cliff as gentle waves bubbled around his legs. A surfer stumbled upon him at dawn.
When later questioned by police, the surfer first thought the man had gotten drunk and fallen asleep. This was Venice Beach. Drunken and disorderly conduct was the norm. The surfer had a slight hangover himself and was hoping the waves would neutralize the migraine scratching at his brain.
It was his routine to dip his toes into the water at dawn. The fire-apple sunrise burned over the horizon. Morning light refracted over the Pacific. It was the perfect morning to carve the glassy water with a fiberglass board and escape the toils of life. He was about to plunge into the chilly water when he spotted the body.
The dead man was dressed like a pallbearer: a white shirt, a black suit, and a skinny black tie strung tight around his neck. The surfer waved and called out, but the man did not respond.
"That's when I knew something was wrong," Joshua told the police officer.
Joshua's blond hair blew in the wind as he answered questions. This was his secret surf spot. He came at sunrise at least three times a week to catch a few waves before work. If he didn't get into the water to unwind, the pressures of work would drive him insane. Surfing was therapy. But there would be no epic rides this morning. This morning, Joshua found a corpse.
"Did you touch the body?" the officer asked.
"I checked his pulse. I walked over and he was just sitting there, leaning against the cliff like he is now with the water pooling around him. When I got close and saw all the color washed out of his face and the swelling in his fingers, my heart stopped. His lips were too blue. When I felt no pulse, I panicked and called 911. I've seen enough movies to know better than to tamper with a crime scene. You guys showed up twenty minutes later."
"Was there anyone else here? Did you see anything strange?"
"No one else. Just me. I didn't see anything but this dead guy in a suit leaning against the rock like he was taking a nap.
"
The officer clicked his ballpoint pen. He was done questioning the surfer, who had nothing useful to offer.
"Am I going to be in the paper?" Joshua asked.
A gruff detective approached. "Only if you want to be. Cops don't write that drivel. Deadbeat journalists who make their living writing about blood splatter type the front page. I don't give a damn what they publish as long as I'm left alone to do my job. If you want to be famous, talk to them. Say something stupid and I guarantee you'll wind up in the paper."
Chewing gum smacked and popped inside Detective Andrews' mouth as he sucked on his teeth. It was nicotine gum. He promised his wife he'd quit smoking by forty. He was pushing fifty. It was high time he made good on that promise.
She cried when he smoked. She watched her father die a slow and painful death from lung cancer. The old man puffed on the death sticks until his dying day. She couldn't watch her husband go through the same agony.
Why he ever started smoking was beyond him. He was clean as a whistle when he joined the force. Never touched a drop of alcohol or puffed a cigar. But when he made detective and started spending his time around murder scenes, something inside him darkened. He transformed into the cliché on the silver screen: a drinking, cursing, perpetually smoking detective pacing over a dead body, trying to get into the mind of a killer.
"What's your name again, surfer boy?" Detective Andrews asked.
"Joshua McDaniels. Are they going to call me into court to be a witness?" This was the most exciting thing to happen in Joshua's life. He was milking every second of it. Detective Andrews wanted to punch the pearly white teeth out of his mouth.
"Get off the crime scene. Unless you have anything else to share that might help us catch the killer, we are done talking to you."
Joshua's eyes widened. "You can't talk to me like that. Isn't your motto to protect and serve? My tax dollars pay your salary."
"I don't get paid to have manners. I get paid to catch criminals. No one cares how nice I am as long as the bad guys wind up in a steel box. Besides, income tax in this state is so high that most of the cushy salary you think you pay me winds up in Uncle Sam's pocket. Now I'm only going to ask nicely one more time."
Detective Andrews shoved his hands into his pockets. His dress shoes dug into the sand as he leaned closer. Stale nicotine gum swirled in his mouth.
"Are you dull?" Andrews barked. "Piss off. There are plenty of reporters behind the yellow tape who would love to talk to you."
Joshua opened his mouth to respond, but when he saw the rage burning in the detective's eyes, he thought better of it. He turned and left.
The officer who had been questioning Joshua before Andrews arrived raised an eyebrow.
"Did he tell you anything useful?" Andrews asked
.
The officer flipped through his notes. "Joshua came down to surf. He comes here two or three times a week before work to unwind. He arrived around 6 a.m. and found the body. He checked the pulse and called us. He didn't see anyone else and didn't tamper with the scene."
"Okay. He's useless. I need gloves."
Detective Andrews snapped latex gloves over his stubby fingers. He was getting fat. A bulge had grown in his belly and spread to his extremities. Knocking out smoking didn't help. People bloat when they quit.
They also bloat when they die.
Yellow police tape blocked off the crime scene. Three or four reporters lingered at the edge trying to get statements.
Running around Los Angeles attempting to be the first reporter on an accident or horrific crime scene was a sick way to make a living. The station that aired the bloodiest catastrophe first got the highest ratings. It made Andrews' stomach churn.
People feared killers. They were also drawn to them. News ratings skyrocketed anytime a serial killer was loose. In the '60s, the Zodiac Killer drove the San Francisco Chronicle's circulation through the roof. Ted Bundy was a media sensation, earning more airtime than most Hollywood actors during his brief career.
Becoming a crazed serial killer was a guaranteed path to fame. The fame never lasted. There was no big paycheck. And if you revealed your identity, you wound up facing the death penalty unless some fancy lawyer said fancy things to protect you.
Not that it mattered much in California anymore.
The governor's policies made the death penalty nearly impossible. Killers lived out their lives in prison, their meals paid for by taxpayers.
Detective Andrews hadn't always been this bitter. He started his career bright-eyed and bushy-tailed, convinced police work could make a difference. Two decades taught him otherwise.
Los Angeles might be the City of Angels. But where there were angels, there were demons. And this city had been overrun by the underworld long ago.
The world was fucked. All he could do was try to unfuck his small piece of it.
He reached into his jacket pocket and pulled out a penlight. He crouched before the body.
The dead man's hair was light brown and freshly trimmed. His face was clean-shaven. His shoes were polished to a mirror shine. He looked fit. The suit was tight around his arms.
"Has anyone searched the body?" Andrews asked a forensics officer nearby.
"No. We waited for you."
They made the right call. First responders sometimes contaminated a crime scene before Homicide arrived. Luckily whoever got here first taped off the area and kept it contained
.
Not that it would matter much.
This murder oozed professionalism. This was planned. And the saltwater likely washed away anything useful. There were no signs of struggle. No blood. No visible contusions around the neck or face. It was as if the man had simply sat down, closed his eyes, and died.
Andrews lifted the dead man's coat lapel with the penlight. The pockets were empty. All the tags had been ripped from the suit.
Strange.
It wasn't as if the brand of the suit would matter. He checked the pants pockets. The back pockets were empty. The left front pocket was empty.
The right was not.
There was a folded piece of paper tucked inside.
Andrews carefully removed it and unfolded it in his gloved hand. The paper was no larger than his palm.
Five words were typed in bold letters.
SLEEP NOW.
IT IS DONE.
Andrews sealed the note inside a plastic bag and handed it to a forensics officer.
There was nothing else on the body. No wallet. No wedding ring. No watch. No phone. Not even loose change.
Next, Andrews lifted one of the man's hands and shined the penlight across the fingertips.
"What the hell?"
The forensics officer who took the note stepped closer to get a better look. "His fingerprints have been burned off," the officer said. "But these aren't new burns. The skin healed a long time ago. Chemical damage."
Andrews placed the hand back in the dead man's lap and stood over the body.
The facts turned in his mind.
Dead on the beach.
Black suit.
Tags removed.
Strange note.
No identification.
Burned fingerprints.
No visible injuries.
No sign of struggle.
"Has the surrounding area been searched?" Andrews asked.
"Thoroughly. No weapons. No prints. Dogs came up empty."
The nicotine gum tasted like burnt rubber. Andrews chewed slowly.
The sun rose higher. Waves crashed along the shore.
"Retirement's two years away," he muttered. "I wish it was tomorrow. I'm getting too old for this."
No one answered.
