he Director cursed.
Foul language was unprofessional. He considered it a sign of low IQ when people used excessive profanity, but he was alone in his office on the top floor of Agency headquarters in the frozen Canadian Yukon and no one was here to judge him. Winter was over, yet snow lingered well into July in this place. Only a handful of staff remained in the building, moving quietly through dark rooms lit by computer screens, planning missions, tracking criminals, and assisting agents in the field. They were obedient and dull. They feared him. Every conversation was riddled with "Yes, sir" and "Right away, sir" and "I'm on it, sir" that he was beginning to think his name was sir and not Robert.
Spade was different.
She could think. She had a mind of her own. And when she felt like it, she told him he was old, crotchety, and a coward. He had never respected and hated someone so much at the same time.
He had angered her, and she had gone back home to New York. That was normal. She rarely stayed longer than a few weeks and did her best work from her own apartment. Still, he had hoped her last visit would last longer.
As snow drifted down in the depths of spring, he found himself missing her. He could use her unfiltered advice. If he listened hard enough, he could almost hear the faint hum of her wheelchair motor as she moved across the room.
She was a pain in the ass, but sometimes the most difficult people were the most effective.
A file lay open on his desk. Inside were reports and crime scene photos. A junior staff member had been monitoring the case. Hacking into the LAPD database had been effortless. The Agency had near-limitless funding and technology years ahead of anything else on the planet. It could run circles around the NSA. The LAPD's firewalls might as well have been an anthill waiting to be kicked over.
The file had reached his desk three hours after the body had been discovered
.
The image inside was haunting. The dead man leaned against the cliff, legs sprawled in the sand. His chin tilted to the right, as if he had simply grown tired and drifted into sleep.
The Director recognized the face.
He paused and remembered the day he had met the man. Back then, the agent's eyes were bright and ambitious. Now they were dull and lifeless, like a shark's.
The LAPD knew almost nothing. No sign of struggle. No weapon. No leads. No threads to pull. The media would feast on it. Amateur sleuths would swarm.
The police had detected trace elements of poison in the victim's bloodstream. It was unfamiliar to law enforcement, but the Director knew exactly what it was.
The toxin was derived from a rare puffer fish off the coast of China. Once extracted, it was blended with snake venom and a complex chemical compound few could pronounce. The poison immobilized the body without immediately killing it. The brain stem lost control of the nervous system, making movement impossible except for breathing. Sensation remained intact. The victim became a conscious prisoner inside his own body.
Agent 917 had used the toxin once before on a bloodthirsty surgeon named Doctor Philtzer. He injected it into Philtzer before burying him alive.
There were no signs of struggle at the beach. Whoever did this poisoned the victim and left him there to fade slowly into death.
The burnt fingerprints confirmed Robert's suspicions.
Robert tried to convince himself the technicians were wrong. A clerical error. A glitch in the system. A misplaced zero in an endless stream of code. But when he read that the fingerprints had been chemically burned off, the truth was undeniable.
He reached for his tablet and propped it beside the file. He called Spade.
Her face appeared moments later.
She looked composed despite the exhaustion. A pressed suit jacket framed her shoulders. Dark-rimmed glasses rested on her nose. Her makeup was subtle and precise. Everything about Spade was deliberate.
"And why is the Director calling me?" she asked lightly.
"What time is it in New York?" he said.
She glanced at the corner of her screen. "Almost six in the morning."
"Are you up early or still awake?"
"The latter. I've been up all night. If I change clothes, I'll collapse into bed, but the work isn't finished. I've been planning 917's next mission and reviewing targets. There's a mechanic in Kansas who loosens lug nuts after servicing tires. He hasn't killed anyone yet, but he's caused serious accidents. I don't think he qualifies as a target until he crosses that line."
"Stop what you're doing. I sent you a file. It should be in your inbox. I'll wait.
"
Her expression shifted. She had known him since the Agency's beginning and could read him like a book. He was curt and to the point. This couldn't be good.
The LAPD case file had been digitized and transferred to her inbox.
Robert watched her eyes move across her monitor as she absorbed the details. She removed her glasses and rubbed her eyes.
"Fuck, Robert."
"Fuck is right." He exhaled. "Someone killed Agent 429."
"And used Agency poison," she said. "What's the fallout?"
"None. The LAPD has nothing. The implant dissolved upon cardiac arrest. They can't trace this back to us."
She scrolled through the file. "Detective Andrews is the lead investigator assigned to the case. Late fifties. Overweight. Strong record."
Robert had barely considered the detective. "I doubt he'll pose a problem. Our resources surpass his. Everything he knows is in that file."
"Good detectives don't write down every thought," she said. "They carry them in their heads."
She zoomed in on the image of the body. "This is a statement killing. Even if the body is clean for forensics, the presentation is deliberate. Why leave a note? 'Sleep now. It is done.' What is that supposed to mean?"
"I was hoping you could tell me."
She leaned back, studying the screen. "This isn't the first time we've lost an agent."
"This is different," Robert said. "Agents die on missions. They don't get executed and displayed for the public. This is a loose end. We need to close it quickly."
"Send me everything on Agent 429," she said. "No omissions. I want his birth certificate, psychological profile, and mission logs. Don't tell me you erased who he was before. I need to know everything about him."
He grumbled. "You'll have it within the hour."
"What was his last mission?"
"He eliminated a human trafficker in Los Angeles. Routine. Completed successfully. He'd been idle for a month."
"I'm monitoring Detective Andrews."
"Why?"
"Because competent detectives don't document every deduction and he might uncover some of the vital clues for me."
"Where is 917?"
"On standby."
"Can you move him to Los Angeles?"
"917 isn't an investigator. He's an assassin. Without a target, he's ineffective."
"Then find a target," Robert said. "Identify whoever did this and give 917 a name."
He ended the call.
Spade stared at the dark screen
.
Someone had hunted and killed Agent 429.
The Agency erased identities thoroughly. No external entity should have been capable of this.
A cold thought formed.
Only someone inside the Agency could have done it.
She reopened the file and read the note aloud.
"'Sleep now. It is done.'"
There was no visible trail, but her instincts told her something else had been left behind. The killer wanted the Agency's attention.
She reopened Detective Andrews' profile. Maybe she did not need to find the killer first. Maybe she only needed to follow the detective.
Her fingers moved over her laptop keyboard and transmitted a secure message through the Agency satellite network to Agent 917.
She would need him.

T
he media called the case "The Venice Beach Spook." The press got their grubby hands on every police detail. The public knew about the strange man in a suit found dead on the beach with the labels ripped from his clothes. The mystery consumed the news cycle.
After processing what little evidence there was, Detective Andrews headed to the bar. It was ritual. A cop bar full of old, tarnished wood furniture. Low chatter. Patrons hovered over pool tables. A jukebox played vintage records, filling the room with a warm timbre.
When he married, he thought he would skip the bar and go home to his wife and a hot meal. He had been wrong. The job had hollowed him out over the years. He loved Patricia fiercely, which was why he shielded her from the darkness he carried. It was hard to sit across from your wife at dinner when earlier that day you had pulled a dead baby from a junkie's microwave or stood over a murdered prostitute stabbed by a man collecting slaves for the afterlife.
Los Angeles felt cursed. After thirty years on the force, Andrews believed one thing with certainty: the world was cruel, and it broke everyone eventually.
Patricia did not deserve that weight. Scott Andrews buried it deep in his chest, in a place she would never reach. A year into their marriage, she wanted children. He had once dreamed of having a son, playing catch in the yard. That was before he made detective and saw what people were capable of. He could not bring a child into a world like this. The thought of some predator taking his kid would haunt him daily. It was a burden he refused to carry.
The bartender set an Anchor Steam in front of him. He disliked most things about San Francisco, but he liked the beer.
He drained it and tried to push the case from his mind.
He had worked so many murders that little shocked him anymore. Bodies, weapons, fingerprints, DNA. In his head, they were office supplies. Pens. Paperclips. Post-it notes. It was how he coped. It was the cost of catching killers.
After he was good and drunk, he ordered an Uber. He nearly fell asleep in the back seat while a talkative college student drove him home. The kid rambled about writing detective fiction and driving rideshare until he sold
a manuscript. When he learned Andrews was a detective, he would not stop talking.
Andrews offered advice.
"Write fantasy or romance. Stay away from police work. It takes whatever purity you have and twists it until you become bitter and angry."
He stepped out before the driver could reply.
He left five stars and a modest tip. Silence would have earned more.
Patricia had stopped waiting up years ago. She went to bed early. Waiting for him, night after night, depressed her. She barely stirred when he slipped under the covers at two in the morning.
The next day, he slept in. The Police Chief tolerated his loose schedule. Andrews always gave whatever hours the case required. He lived on cheap coffee in a Styrofoam cup and spent late nights under a desk lamp, scanning files long after others quit. As long as killers ended up in prison, the Chief did not interfere with Andrews' schedule.
When Andrews woke, Patricia was gone. So was the dog. He had bought her the dog years ago. They had abandoned the idea of children, and the dog filled the quiet space. She often walked it in the mornings.
He sat on the edge of the bed and pressed his fingers to his temples. A hangover vibrated behind his eyes. He wanted a cigarette but had promised Patricia he had quit. He reached into the nightstand, peeled a nicotine patch from its backing, and pressed it to his arm.
The phone rang.
He glanced at the clock. Nearly noon. The department would be buzzing with activity by now.
He answered.
"Yeah?" His voice sounded thin. Every boxer had a limited number of fights in him. Maybe detectives did too. There were only so many cases a man could work before he threw in the towel. Maybe he was too stupid to know when to quit.
"We've got something on the Venice Beach Spook." It was forensics.
Andrews exhaled. "On the body?"
"No. The body is clean. No injuries. No struggle. It's the note. The ink is standard ballpoint, but the paper isn't. It dates back to the early 1500s. We believe it was torn from a rare book called The Lesser Key of Solomon."
"The Lesser what? Explain this to me like I was a fifth grader."
"The Huntington Library reported a break-in days before the murder. Their copy of The Lesser Key of Solomon was stolen. Paper from the 1500s isn't common. Whoever wrote that note likely stole the book and tore out a page."
"Have you told anyone else?"
"Only you."
"Keep it that way."
Andrews ended the call.
He needed a shower, a shave, and the strongest coffee his machine could make. A puzzle like this required a sharp mind.
His felt dull.

D
etective Andrews worked alone. The last time he rode with a partner was over ten years ago. Greg was a good man. He avoided getting killed on the job and leaving Andrews a bitter loner. Greg simply grew old and retired.
In the movies, detectives always had partners. The banter made for good cinema. Reality was quieter. Andrews spent most of his time driving around Los Angeles listening to NPR and chasing dead ends on cold cases. He had drawn his firearm four times in three decades and never once pulled the trigger. God willing, it would stay that way. Most officers retired without firing a shot. Gunfights belonged in books and movies.
When his radio crackled to life, it usually meant murder. Being a homicide detective was reactionary. Nothing about his job prevented crime. Killers made the first move. Detectives followed.
He was never first to a scene. By the time he arrived, uniforms had strung yellow tape and forensics hovered over evidence. He would step in like he owned the place. When he was younger, a cigarette hung from his mouth as he studied the dead. Now nicotine gum smacked in his cheeks while he tried to enter the killer’s mind.
He pulled into the Huntington Library parking lot and shut off the engine of his Ford. He had been here countless times. The grounds included a vast library, an art museum, and winding botanical gardens that felt pulled from a children’s book. On weekends, hundreds of visitors filled the place.
It was Monday afternoon, so the crowd was thin, though dozens of people still wandered the paths. Most were elderly, enjoying the air. Andrews supposed it was better than rotting in front of television.
Before stepping out, he checked his weapon. He carried an FN 509 MRD-LE, the department’s standard-issue handgun. The safety was engaged, and the leather clasps of the shoulder holster were secure. He was not a gun enthusiast. Knowing how to point, shoot, and reload was enough. Years earlier, when he first made detective, he carried a revolver like Clint Eastwood. The modern handgun was lighter, steadier, and held seventeen rounds.
He did not expect trouble at a library in the middle of the day, but the weight of the weapon was reassuring
.
Inside, he approached the front desk where a thin man waited with a polite smile.
“You must be Detective Andrews. My name is Bartholomew McPherson. I’m the head librarian.”
McPherson wore a dark suit that contrasted sharply with Andrews’ heather gray jacket. McPherson looked angular, almost brittle, as if he had been pressed flat between the pages of a heavy book.
Andrews shook the librarian's. “The LAPD received your report about a missing book. I’m here to investigate.”
“It is a tragedy,” McPherson said. “The Lesser Key of Solomon is priceless. Fewer than a hundred copies exist from the early 1500s. Any legitimate sale would be public. Whoever has it now would need to use the black market and hide it carefully. The situation is deeply troubling.”
“First theft here?”
“Yes. Security is state of the art. Cameras cover nearly every angle. We maintain twenty-four-hour patrols. There is no trace of the thief.”
As McPherson spoke, his gold-rimmed glasses slid down his nose. He nudged them back into place with a single finger, a practiced motion.
The Huntington Library housed millions of books and rare documents. Original letters from George Washington rested in glass cases. Drafts of speeches by Abraham Lincoln hung along the walls. As they walked the halls, Andrews felt the weight of accumulated and historical knowledge around him. He carried the internet in his pocket like everyone else, but even he respected what these shelves held.
Then McPherson said something that stopped him.
“Your partner arrived a few hours ago. He’s in the rare book room where the theft occurred. I’ll take you there.”
Detective Andrews had not had a partner in years.

T
he Chief would never blindside him with a partner on a crime scene. They would have told him back at the precinct. Andrews knew exactly how it would have played out. He would have told the Chief to kiss his ass and leave. The Chief would have blocked his path, told him to shut up, and explained that taking on a partner was part of winding down a career. Andrews was nearing the end of his time on the force. He would need to pass along what he knew. There would be no avoiding it. Eventually he would give in and take the rookie under his wing.
None of that had happened. Instead, a librarian casually informed him his partner was waiting in the rare book room, as if commenting on the weather.
Sweat gathered at the back of Andrews’ neck. Whoever waited inside was not a cop. The weight of the sidearm in his shoulder holster seemed to double. He knew he could draw fast if needed. A library was the last place he expected to fire his first shot on duty, but stranger things had happened.
The walk to the rare book room felt endless. His mind churned with possibilities. He barely noticed the artifacts lining the walls. He was a history buff, and normally a handwritten letter from George Washington would have stopped him cold. Today, he walked past it without slowing.
McPherson stopped at a velvet rope barring a doorway. He unhooked the clasp and pulled it aside. A keycard flashed against a scanner. The lock clicked. The door opened.
A man stood in the center of the room with his back to them. Dark navy suit. White shirt. Black tie. His hands rested in his pockets as he surveyed the shelves. Dark hair fell across his forehead. He turned.
“Can you give us a moment?” Andrews asked.
“Of course.” McPherson dipped his head and stepped out, closing the door behind him.

“Are you always this polite?” the man replied.
“Rarely. Start talking.” Andrews closed the distance between them. He moved quickly for his size.

“I’m going
to ask once more.”
The man removed one hand from his pocket. Andrews tensed, expecting a weapon. The hand was empty.

“The same thing you are. Looking for a killer. Murder outranks theft.” Horace’s eyes held steady. “Why would someone use a page from The Lesser Key of Solomon to leave that note?”
“I don’t know what you’re talking about.”
Horace’s jaw tightened. “Don’t insult me. I’ve reviewed everything from your crime scene. No fingerprints. No struggle. A rare page used for the message. I came for two reasons. Find the book thief, and we likely find the killer.”
“And the second reason?”
“You. You catch killers. I want to help you take this one down.”
“Do I have to call you Benjamin?”
“Ben works.”
“Alright, Ben. The FBI doesn’t show up on a local murder without cause. Either this crossed state lines or you’re hunting a serial killer.”
“Both.”
Andrews' heart skipped a beat.
“Another victim surfaced yesterday in Arizona,” Ben continued. “Tucson. Same presentation. Black suit. Tags removed. Prints chemically burned off. No struggle. Trace poison. A note folded in the pocket. Same message written on a page ripped from The Lesser Key of Solomon.”
He handed over a photograph.
The body leaned against a boulder in the Arizona desert, legs angled awkwardly, sun beating down. It looked staged.
Andrews had never seen anything like it. This was not a typical hit. No bullets. No garrote wire. A sophisticated toxin. Deliberate fingerprint removal. The victims were professionals. No one burned their fingertips for fun. That meant concealment.
This smelled of shadow operations and sealed rooms.
“What else?” Andrews asked.
“That’s everything.”
“I doubt that.”
“Anything more would be speculation.”
Andrews studied him. The FBI had broader reach than the LAPD, deeper funding, fewer cases. But Ben seemed sincere.
“Have you canvassed the library? Cameras?” Andrews asked.
“Whoever took the book is a ghost. Cameras show nothing. Guards saw nothing. No forced entry. No logged access. There are loads of valuable
books in here. Lincoln's journals. Revolutionary documents. The Lesser Key was not the most expensive item in the room.”
“So it wasn’t about money,” Andrews said.
“Agreed.”
“What’s the book about?”
Ben’s expression shifted slightly. “The Lesser Key of Solomon is tied to biblical lore and medieval occult tradition. It describes King Solomon commanding demons through a magical ring. Seventy-two entities cataloged. It is chalk full of rituals for summoning.”
Andrews did not believe in demons. He had seen too much real evil to waste time on the supernatural.
“You think these were ritual killings?”
“No I do not. No symbols. No markings. The only link is the paper.”
Silence settled between them. Sunlight streamed through the skylight, illuminating dustless air. Climate control preserved the texts. These books would outlive them both.
“How did the thief get in and out?” Andrews asked.
Ben gestured toward the ceiling and vents. “No viable entry points. Door is keycard controlled. Logs show no access the night of the theft.”
Andrews walked the perimeter. No broken glass. No damaged locks. Clean.
“If I had answers, I’d give them,” Ben said. He pulled out his phone. Andrews’ phone buzzed seconds later. “I just shot you a text. Now, you have my number. Call if there is a development. I’ve been up all night with the Arizona scene. I need rest.”
“How many serial cases have you worked?” Andrews asked.
“Not many.”
“Then you know what comes next when we have nothing. We wait. And someone else dies.”
“I intend to stop that.”
Ben turned and left.
Andrews watched him go. He had seen the scars circling the man’s wrist. When they shook hands, he felt the smooth absence of ridges where fingerprints should have been.
Ben was not FBI.
And whoever he worked for was losing people.

S
pade’s brain was running on fumes. A knot the size of a golf ball throbbed beneath her right shoulder blade. When she was younger, she could pull all-nighters, sleep in the next day, and wake up refreshed. Her late thirties were different. Aging was no longer theoretical. Her body did not bounce back the way it once had.
She raised her arms and stretched, trying to loosen the tightness in her back. She had been up all night resetting 917’s mind, routing him to Los Angeles, and analyzing the recent killing in Arizona. Two pots of coffee kept her functional. Her cortisol levels were off the charts. When this was over, she was getting a massage. An expensive one with hot stones and steamed towels.
Another agent had been killed in Arizona. This time it was Agent 083. Agents received their numbers at random. There were one thousand of them scattered across the globe, though most operated in North America. A few years earlier, Spade had designed the dime-sized implant embedded beneath an agent’s pituitary gland. With a command, it erased memory. Once the Director saw how efficiently agents operated without emotional interference, the program expanded rapidly.
Recruitment was disturbingly simple. There was no shortage of violent people eager to forget their past and surrender themselves to a cause. Candidates had to willingly submit to the memory wipes for the implant to function properly. The mind, no matter how fractured, was resilient. The implant worked only in tandem with a willing psyche. Resistance destabilized the subject.
Spade hoped 917 had stopped resisting. Months earlier, she had shut him down in San Francisco and rebooted him. Since then, he had operated like clockwork. She needed it to stay that way.
Someone was targeting their agents. One body surfaced in Venice Beach. Another appeared in Arizona days later. Both were killed with the same toxin she developed for controlled executions.
Who could replicate it?
After hours of research, she still had no answer. Her role at the Agency involved meticulous mission design. Nothing began until every variable had been accounted for. Agents deployed with specific equipment, parameters, and instructions tailored to the mission. Planning missions was like
constructing a screenplay. They always ended with fitting punishments. Execution never began until she was ready.
This was different. This was a race against someone else’s clock.
The Agency’s strength had always been omnipresence and foresight. Wealthy investors on the Board ensured the resources remained limitless. The blank checks never stopped.
These killings inverted that advantage.
What was she missing?
What was the message?
IT IS DONE.
YOU CAN SLEEP NOW.
It felt like an inside job. Only someone with operational knowledge could eliminate two agents in a week without detection. Agents were highly trained assassins. They did not die easily. They were monitored constantly, their movements tracked by handlers.
Each implant recorded everything. GPS data. Vital signs. Every transmission streamed back to Agency servers. Spade reviewed the logs from the days preceding both deaths.
A disturbing anomaly emerged.
For twenty-four hours before each death, the database was blank. The implants stopped transmitting. The steady flow of data ceased entirely.
That should have been impossible.
The implants did not rely on batteries or require recharging. They drew power from the body’s bioelectric system. As long as the host lived, the implant functioned. The only permanent way to silence one was death.
Yet the implants went offline before the victims were killed.
Spade picked up the phone. A tired voice answered.
“Tell me you have something,” the Director said.
“Was there any personal connection between Agent 083 and Agent 429 before recruitment? We need overlap. There are one thousand agents in the field. These two cannot be random.”
“Who’s to say they are the only targets? Another body could surface tomorrow.”
“Do not hide anything from me. I know you archive their former identities. I need everything if we are going to solve this.”
The Director sighed. She could practically hear him thinking.
“Robert, if there is a security breach, I need to know now. Is someone inside the Agency killing our people?”
“I am a man of secrets,” he said. “You have reason to doubt me. But I swear I know nothing beyond what you know.”
“I was afraid you would say that.”
“Why?”
“Because I do not know what the hell is going on.”

A
gent 917 pressed the gas pedal. The Lincoln maneuvered through the steel and glass towers of Los Angeles. One hand rested on the steering wheel while he loosened his tie with the other. It pulled at his neck like a noose. He had been in the city for forty-eight hours and could not wait to leave. Thick smog blanketed everything. People wore grungy clothes. A hooker with runny makeup had thrown herself at him the previous night. Cigarette smoke seemed embedded in the molecular structure of the city. There was a common quality that all matter in this place appeared to share. The cheeseburgers, the cars, the people and the pavement all felt made from the same substance. Whatever it was, 917 wanted to get away from it.
He had no memory of where he was from, but he was willing to bet it was not Los Angeles. This place felt like an unwelcoming alien planet.
The hotel oozed luxury. Thirty-seven floors of polished rooms boasted two restaurants, a gym with a sauna and a massive swimming pool. 917 pulled in front of the lobby and tossed the keys to a valet. The day before, a suitcase full of weapons had been in the trunk. That suitcase now sat in his room, where a Do Not Disturb sign hung permanently from the door handle.
He stepped into the lobby and took the elevator to his corner suite on the twentieth floor. He flashed the keycard at the lock. Small mechanical clicks whirred as the gears turned. Once inside, he discarded his blazer and tossed it onto a chair near the window. The chair looked elegant but felt stiff. The room was designed for sheen, not comfort.
He had woken in that bed two days earlier after his memory wipe. When his eyes opened, dawn had barely broken. He had been wearing Nikes, jeans and a black T-shirt. His face felt freshly shaved. The room had been empty except for an encrypted cellphone on the nightstand. When he picked it up, retinal and voice recognition unlocked it. A fingerprint scan would have been pointless. Like all agents in the field, 917 had no prints. He belonged to the Agency. Whoever he had been before this life was gone.
The mission instructions waiting in an unread message were unsettling. His handler was named Spade. She would contact him periodically throughout the mission. She suspected a breach of Agency security. Someone was killing agents one by one. A body had been found at Venice Beach.
Two days later, another turned up in Arizona. The Agency had lost contact with their implants twenty-four hours before the agents died, a seasoned LAPD detective had been assigned to the case. 917 was to pose as FBI Agent Benjamin Horace, work alongside Detective Andrews and eliminate the killer once identified.
Spade warned him this mission was different. Every precaution was necessary. Most missions were engineered for success. A target was selected for punishment, and 917 was deployed to remove them. The Agency did not believe in turning the other cheek. Hollow-point rounds and sharpened steel proved more effective than mercy. Some souls were beyond redemption.
This time, there was no clear target. The plan would form only after the killer's identity surfaced. Even with the Agency's vast resources, Spade had uncovered no viable leads. Relying on Detective Andrews felt like desperation.
Spade had informed him that a Lincoln SUV waited in the hotel parking lot. The keys were at the front desk, along with an envelope containing credit cards and identification for Benjamin Horace. In the trunk was a suitcase with a pressed suit and an arsenal.
The inventory included a sniper rifle, grenades, two packs of C4, two Beretta M9s with extra magazines, night vision goggles, strangling wire, military-grade lock picks and a selection of poisons. Spade had no clear sense of what he might need, so she sent everything.
He secured the weapons in the closet, instructed the front desk to refuse housekeeping and kept the Do Not Disturb sign on the door.
He sat on the bed instead of the rigid chair and removed his clothes piece by piece. There was no trace of whoever had stolen The Lesser Key of Solomon. The only conclusion he could draw was that the thief and the killer were professionals. Executing crimes like this required technology, discipline and high-level training.
The thought made him smile.
It took someone like him to operate with that precision.
He removed his shoes and sat on the edge of the bed in his undershirt and slacks. The Beretta M9 rested in the shoulder holster beneath his right arm. Waking without memory could be disorienting. Familiar sensations grounded him. The weight of the Beretta steadied his nerves. Though he could never remember firing it, the way his fingers settled around the grip told him he had pulled the trigger many times.
There were no new messages on the phone. Spade's final instructions were to investigate the library and work with Detective Andrews. Neither of them had a lead. The evidence was nonexistent.
He sat on the edge fo the bed and turned on the television.
Instead of a channel menu, green code cascaded down a black screen while a low electronic hum filled the room
.
The implant at the base of his skull tingled. His body locked up, his muscled tightened and he froze in front of the TV. No matter how hard he tried, he could not move a muscle. His body was stuck.
Across the country, in a small apartment in New York, Spade panicked as the Agency network lost connection with 917.

etective Andrews tailed FBI Agent Ben Horace to the luxury hotel. He ensured the gap between them never dropped below four cars to remain unseen.
The hotel sat a few blocks off Hollywood Boulevard. The Los Angeles skyline loomed in the distance.
Andrews fed quarters into the parking meter while watching Ben hand his keys to a valet and step through the double doors into the lobby. Andrews had worked with the FBI before. They never stayed in hotels this extravagant. The Bureau owned several safe houses scattered throughout the city. Most appeared to be ordinary homes or condos, but inside they were outfitted with surveillance equipment.
For a heavyset man, Andrews was light on his feat. He crossed the street and slipped into the lobby. Across the marble floor, he saw Ben enter an elevator. Once the doors closed, Andrews hurried over and watched the small screen above the doors count upward. The elevator stopped at the twentieth floor, paused, and then descended.
Andrews pressed the call button. As he waited, he sorted through the case in his mind. Two victims were confirmed, assuming Ben was telling the truth about the body in Arizona. To solve murders, you find links. Connections revealed motive and victim selection patterns. The detail that unsettled him most was the absence of fingerprints on both victims. Ben also lacked fingerprints. The connection gnawed at Andrews, like a splinter he couldn't reach.
The elevator doors opened, and Andrews stepped inside and mashed the button for the twentieth floor. As it rose, he watched the numbers climb on the digital display. He checked his weapon. The magazine was full. A round sat in the chamber. The safety was off. The leather strap securing the holster was unclipped for a quick draw.
Maybe today I pop my cherry and fire a weapon on the job? Please God, help my aim be true and the other guy's be shit.
He reached into his pocket and pulled out the pack of nicotine gum his wife had given him. He popped two pieces into his mouth and chewed hard.
A bell chimed overhead. The doors opened to the twentieth floor
.
The hallway was empty. Empty hotel corridors always reminded him of The Shining. He half expected a strange creature or creepy twin girls to be waiting at the far end. Rooms stretched in both directions. Each door looked identical. Some had room service trays resting outside.
A door to his left opened suddenly. Andrews reached for his gun out of instinct, then relaxed when a young couple stumbled out in bathing suits, laughing and drunk. They walked past him and called the elevator.
He checked his watch. Three in the afternoon. There must be sixty rooms on this floor. He could flash his badge and demand a room number for Benjamin Horace. The hotel staff was not obligated to provide it without a warrant. It would draw attention. Ben might not even be registered under that name.
Andrews turned down the left hallway. He moved slowly, listening and studying each door as he passed. He stopped in front of room 2048. A Do Not Disturb sign hung from the handle. It was the only door in the wing with a Do Not Disturb sign.
In the late afternoon, only two kinds of people used those signs. Those seeking privacy, and those who never removed them because they preferred to be left alone.
Andrews pulled out his cell phone and dialed Ben's number. He pressed his ear to the door. He thought he heard the faint chirp of a phone inside the room. The sound stopped.
Ben did not answer.
Andrews stepped back from the door and weighed his next move.

rrogance was the downfall of the mighty.
Sofia Grace sat at her desk with her legs crossed and leaned back in the uncomfortable chair as she watched her laptop screen. Green lines of code streamed downward like running water. These cryptic symbols and binary numbers could unwind an agent's mind. The Agency believed its implants were secure. It assumed its closed-loop system was unhackable. It was wrong. The firewalls designed to protect the implants had never truly been tested because no one outside the Agency knew the agents existed.
But Sofia knew.
Not long ago, one of those implants had erased her memory. Before she broke free, she had no name, only a number, 048, stamped on her existence like a barcode. Waking to missions without memory had been soulless. She had become more machine than human, yet the human inside her had refused to die.
She had checked into the room adjacent to 917 the day before. Finding him had not been difficult. She only needed to leave breadcrumbs for the Agency to follow. Using pages torn from a rare book stolen from the Huntington Library added a touch of theater. She knew Spade would connect the paper to the book and dispatch an agent to investigate.
The moment 917 touched down in Los Angeles, he illuminated her screen. She tracked him to the hotel, noted his room number, and when he left to investigate the rare book room at the library, she secured the room next to his. The concierge seemed puzzled by her insistence on that specific room, but it was vacant, and he had no reason to refuse. After handing over the key card, he forgot her face. Guests moved through the hotel in constant waves. Strange requests were ordinary in Los Angeles.
A connecting door joined the two rooms. It remained locked unless opened by special request at check-in. Sofia could pick primitive locks effortlessly. It took her less than five minutes to breach 917's room through the adjoining door. Once inside, she felt no thrill, no fear. She knew he was miles away at the library grounds. Hacking into his GPS link had been simple.
She found his arsenal in the closet and allowed herself a quiet smile. The sheer volume of weapons told her there was no defined plan. The Agency
usually provided mission-specific equipment. This time they had sent him prepared for a war they could not define.
She removed a USB drive from her pocket and inserted it into the television. The device mirrored her system onto the screen. After confirming the signal was stable, she slipped back into her room and waited.
Now she sat and watched as her program engaged the implant.
917 was paralyzed.
The instant his eyes fixed on the cascading code, she seized control. The implant severed its connection to Agency servers and linked to hers. Once she gained access, it shifted frequencies and transmitted controlled electrical impulses through his body. The signals hijacked his nervous system and locked him in place. He sat frozen, a man suspended in time. The implant regulated everything from his breathing to his pulse.
Sofia opened the connecting door and entered his room.
917 remained seated on the edge of the bed, eyes locked on the screen.
She pulled up a chair and sat beside him. A single finger traced along his jawline. His physique was sculpted, sharp and deliberate, as if carved from stone.
She placed her hands gently on either side of his head. His blue eyes stared forward, alive yet unreachable.
"There is so much memory in there," she whispered. "So much life behind that forehead of yours. They stole your mind. But I am going to give it back. And when you remember who you are, the end will come. Then you will finally be able to sleep."
917's cell phone on the nightstand began to ring. Detective Andrews' name flashed across the screen. After several moments, the ringing stopped and the call rolled to voicemail.
Sofia leaned closer.
"Do not worry," she said softly. "No one is going to interrupt us."

The back door shut his entire body down and performed a forced reboot on the implant. 917 wasn't much different than a computer. If everything froze, pressing the power key for a few seconds and performing a hard reset often fixed the bug. It was hell on the operating system, though.
The back door was sealed shut. Without a link to the implant, her hands were tied.

Detective Andrews.

impossible.
The second was a syringe filled with a neurotoxin the Agency used for torture. A refined strain of pufferfish venom. It paralyzed the body while amplifying sensory input. The victim couldn't move but felt everything. Every second. Every violation. Sofia was returning their medicine to its source.
Her slender fingers worked the collar around the back of 917's neck. Once secured, she powered it on.
Ten minutes.
Infrared beams penetrated his skull, locating the implant embedded in his brain. It was the size of a dime. No moving parts. A single microchip running delicate functions. A GPS tracker so the Agency could follow. A tiny EMP unit emitting subtle electrical pulses. Nearly imperceptible. But when directed into the pituitary and down the central nervous system, those pulses could wipe memory. Paralyze muscle.
The collar locked onto the implant and began syncing. Within minutes, the static on the TV would be irrelevant. The Agency's firewall would start to collapse.
Sofia readied the syringe.
Once the implant was disabled, 917's memories would return. He deserved liberation.

N
icotine gum tastes like tar.
The texture reminded Detective Andrews of rubber tires. Standing in front of the door, he popped another piece into his mouth. A stale taste settled on the tip of his tongue. No one chewed nicotine gum for the flavor; it was supposed to dull the craving. But as any seasoned smoker knew, the stuff was nearly useless.
His mind swirled. A cigarette would calm his nerves, but he'd promised his wife he would quit. Retirement was around the corner. He'd been blessed with no serious health problems. Patricia would be damned if they spent their golden years battling lung cancer. Andrews had given up enough of his life running through the underbelly of this damned city and tracking criminals through its gutters. The golden years belonged to his wife. It was all he had left to give. So when she came to him in tears and begged him to quit because the habit had taken her father too soon, what could he say?
He could have told her the truth. That everyone was dying. That living was the illusion. Each second moved them closer to the inevitable moment when their eyes would close and never open again. Cigarettes might take him early, but the smoke blotted out the evil. He'd seen too much of it. The inhale, nicotine tendrils wrapping around his lungs, kept him present. Kept him steady. Kept him alive long enough not to get shot doing something stupid.
He thought he saw movement through the hotel room peephole, but it was impossible to tell. At best, a blurry dark shape shifted inside the lit room.
His phone buzzed. Ringer off. Too many movies had taught him that a cop's phone always rang at the worst possible moment, giving away his position before he got tragically positioned. Andrews had learned long ago to keep the damn thing on vibrate.
Private number.
Likely a scam. His policy was simple on private numbers was to ignore it. Private numbers usually meant credit card offers or timeshare pitches, neither of which interested him in the slightest
.
His retirement plans were humble. He'd drop the gun and badge on the Chief's desk, pack up his things, and he and Patricia would leave California behind. He was done with Los Angeles. Done with big cities. He wanted to spend the last quarter of his life in the country, sipping Long Island Iced Teas, reading good books, and making love to his wife until his body quit on him. He'd given enough to trying and failing to fix Los Angeles. The rewards of detective work were rare.
Years later, when Andrews looked back on the moment outside that hotel room, he could never explain why he answered the phone.
Who was he to argue with divine intervention?

That was about to change.

D
ozens of guests bottlenecked at the lobby doors, trying to get out. Police ushered them aside as men in SWAT gear carrying semi-automatic weapons pushed in. 917 blended into the crowd and squeezed forward. He stepped out of the hotel and into the LA sun.
Warmth kissed his face. The heat reminded him of the Pacific, and the faint giggle of a half-forgotten daughter echoed in his mind.
There would be time to think later. Reflection required space. Right now, he had to disappear.
He grabbed a valet and handed over his ticket. "Can you get my car?"
The pimple-faced valet blinked at him. "Are you crazy? I'm not pulling cars while there's an active shooter inside. We need to get the hell out of here."
"Then give me the keys and tell me where it is."
The valet opened his mouth to refuse, then froze when he met 917's stare. Cold. Measured. The look of a man who had ended lives before and would do it again without hesitation.
The valet took the ticket, unlocked the key box, and shoved the keys into 917's hand. "First floor. Row C."
917 found the black SUV and slid behind the wheel. Lucky it was on the first level. Any higher and he'd have been trapped in the chaos. He pulled out and found the nearest on-ramp to the 405.
He kept the speed between sixty-five and seventy. Soon enough, Detective Andrews would be with his people giving a description of him and the vehicle. He couldn't draw attention, but distance mattered.
He couldn't keep the SUV. The Agency might have lost the implant signal, but odds were good the car carried a GPS. The credit cards in his wallet were useless too. One swipe and they'd have him.
Ten miles later, he exited and parked near a bus line. He slipped Sofia's notebook into his breast pocket.
In the trunk sat a spare Beretta M9 with a loaded magazine and stacks of five-hundred-dollar bills totaling ten grand. He tucked the pistol into his waistband and stuffed the cash into his blazer pockets.
A man sold spiced fruit beneath an umbrella on the curb. 917 bought a cup and handed him a five-hundred-dollar bill.
"Twenties and tens," 917 said. "Keep a hundred. Be quick.
"
The vendor's eyes widened. He counted out the change with trembling hands.
917 moved to a bus kiosk and fed it cash. A pass printed out. Minutes later, he was riding through LA in the back of a crowded bus.
He glanced out the window at the black SUV he'd abandoned. Keys on the dash. Doors unlocked. It wouldn't be long before someone took it. He was counting on that. With luck, the thief would drag the Agency in the wrong direction.
The route map overhead stretched across the city.
He leaned his head against the window and closed his eyes.
Memory returned in fragments. The wall of amnesia softened, melting at the edges. Sensations dripped back into him. The vice around his brain loosened. It felt like steam clearing old grime from glass.
A daughter. A wife.
The three of them laughing on a living room sofa. A Christmas tree glowing behind them. A bright star at the top.
They were happy.
What happened to them?
"Audrey and…" he whispered.
His daughter's name had returned. His wife's had not.
What was her name?
OceanofPDF.com

16
T
echnology and violence don't mix. When computers glitch, the urge is to slam them, pound the monitor, force obedience through impact. All that does is break expensive equipment. It doesn't take a genius to know punching a keyboard is pointless.
Spade didn't care.
She hammered the keys like Zeus hurling lightning. Two popped loose and skittered across the floor. The desk rattled under the assault.
Panic ricocheted inside her skull. Heat flooded her vision. Red blotches bled at the edges. She tore off her glasses and buried her face in her hands. Her breathing fractured.
This was the closest she'd ever come to losing control. She always thought people who had anxiety attacks were pathetic. For the first time, she understood losing control of your nervous system as emotion took command.
Pressure never bothered her. In her world, the margin for error was razor thin. One misstep and good people died. Every operation was planned to suffocation before an agent ever stepped into the field.
In the last seventy-two hours, two agents had died. One in Los Angeles. One in the Arizona desert.
The Agency had operatives everywhere.
Whoever was killing them was a ghost.
And the ghost had reached 917.
The satellites lost contact with his implant twenty-nine minutes ago.
Spade leaned back in her wheelchair and forced herself to think.
No connections. No patterns. 917 left the Huntington Library. Detective Andrews followed. She'd watched it unfold like background entertainment, until the feed died.
One second, the readouts were stable.
The next: CONNECTION LOST in neon green.
She'd called Andrews immediately. He'd answered. He'd kicked in the door.
Then silence.
Without the implant signal, she was blind.
She inhaled slowly.
She was better than this
.
Her mind was forged in pressure.
Focus. Breathe. Think. Act.
You can still save him.
Her keyboard was destroyed, but a spare sat in the closet. Two minutes later it was plugged in and working.
Her thoughts moved so fast she could almost hear them snapping into place. Fingers flew, smashing keys. The apartment filled with the sharp clatter of keys.
Slipping through the back door of the hotel's security system was easy. Within minutes, lobby cameras streamed across her monitors.
Chaos.
Police swarmed the floor. Caution tape stretched across marble. Staff huddled in corners answering questions.
No 917.
No Andrews.
Then she saw it.
Medics wheeled out a gurney. A black body bag.
Her pulse stuttered.
She enhanced the image. Zoomed.
Female contours beneath the plastic.
Dead.
The elevator doors opened. Andrews stepped out, flanked by two uniformed officers.
She dialed.
On-screen, she watched him reach into his pocket. He studied the phone. Considered ignoring it and then answered.
"He's alive," Andrews said.
Relief hit hard enough to hurt.
"Where is he?"
Through the feed, she watched Andrews scan the room before slipping into a quiet corner.
"Hell if I know. He cuffed me and bolted."
"Was there someone else?"
"Yeah. A woman. She's dead. No fingerprints. Same as your other ghosts."
Spade closed her eyes as he described the room. The severed implant. The shooting.
The woman had disabled the blocker.
917 was free.
Free and unmonitored.
Where was he going? What state of mind was he in? He hadn't called. His phone still pinged inside the hotel.
"I need answers," Andrews said. "What the hell did I walk into?"
"Nothing," she said. "Whatever you stumbled into ends here. Go back to your life, Detective. Good luck quitting smoking."
She ended the call before he could respond
.
Her next call would be to the Director.
It would trigger a manhunt.
They couldn't allow a rogue agent to roam the country with a fractured mind.
OceanofPDF.com

17
9
17 switched bus lines three times before finally stepping off at a Greyhound station. Signs warned that ID was required to board the Greyhound, but the pimple-faced kid behind the ticket booth was too stoned to care.
The bus was half full. 917 took a seat in the back. He scanned the windows and marked the emergency exit levers. Finding exits was instinct.
Exhaustion crept in as the sun dipped low. He pulled the Beretta M9 from his waistband and slid it into his blazer pocket beside the cash. Sitting on it had grown uncomfortable. Drawing from the pocket would be slower, clumsier, but he was too tired to worry.
After a few hours of broken sleep, the bus rolled into Bakersfield. Nearly halfway to San Francisco.
Memories kept bleeding through the cracked wall of amnesia. Fragments of the man he'd been surfaced like scattered breadcrumbs. He pushed them aside. Survival first. Reflection later.
Bakersfield was quiet. Los Angeles was a roaring swirl of chaos. The Bakersfield countryside was a place of whispered. Passengers stepped off to stretch, smoke, grab food. The driver announced a thirty-minute stop before continuing north.
917 stepped off.
He needed to slow down. Think. The Agency was hunting him. He hadn't seen them, but he felt them. Satellites. Databases. Eyes sweeping the globe.
Four blocks from the station stood a Best Western boasting complimentary breakfast, Wi-Fi, and HBO. He walked into the lobby, ignoring the ache building in his lower back. He needed sleep and a place to to hide while he formulated a plan.
"You'll need ID for a room," the concierge said without looking up.
"How long is your shift?"
"Just started. Here till morning. They pay extra for nights."
917 reached into his pocket. His knuckles brushed the pistol before he pulled out a thick roll of bills. He peeled off five hundred dollars and placed it on the desk
.
"You give me a room. Forget the ID. If anyone asks, you didn't see me. If you get in trouble, tell them I threatened you. And if you don't take the money, I actually will. Let's keep it simple. No ID. A room. Privacy."
The concierge stared at the cash. Whatever loyalty his minimum wage bought evaporated. If he lost this job, there were a a dozen other hotels hiring.
He pocketed the money.
"I was never here," 917 said.
"Who are you?"
"A man who needs rest. I'll give you another five hundred in the morning if I'm left alone."
The concierge slid a keycard across the counter.
Inside the room, 917 locked the deadbolt and set the chain. He checked the windows. Secure.
He stripped to his underwear and sat on the edge of the bed. His clothes were damp and sour with sweat. Before leaving town, he'd need jeans, a shirt and a jacket.
He emptied his pockets onto the bed.
Beretta M9. One magazine loaded with nine hollow points.
Eight thousand nine hundred thirty-one dollars in mixed bills.
A worn black leather journal.
Sixty pages filled in blue ink. Edges yellowed. Coffee stains. A dark red blotch that looked like blood. Greasy fingerprints smudged the corners where anxious hands had turned pages.
917 opened the journal and began to read.
OceanofPDF.com

18
T
he Director stood before the floor-to-ceiling windows of his office overlooking the Yukon. Hands in his pockets, he stared through thick glass at the endless wilderness. Summer was coming, not that it mattered. In the Yukon, high sixties counted as warm. The chill never fully left.
His usual mug of extra-strong coffee sat untouched on the conference table behind him.
Beside it lay a Walther PPK.
Seven rounds.
James Bond had made the weapon famous. The Director preferred it for the same reasons as 007. It was small, concealable and reliable. He was an excellent marksman. Better than some of the agents he commanded. Accuracy made caliber irrelevant. A small bullet to the skull still carved a fatal tunnel through the brain.
He wasn't in immediate danger. The Agency fortress was isolated. Loyal men surrounded him. Fewer than twenty people outside the compound knew its location.
Still, Los Angeles had unsettled him.
A chill crept up his spine and settled at the base of his neck like ice pressed against skin.
Spade's voice came through the speaker phone.
"What do you suggest we do?" he asked.
"We wait. There's no way to track 917. The implant's fried. I lost all connection. He left his phone at the hotel. Ditched his car. He won't use the credit cards. He's got about ten thousand in cash and he'll stretch it."
"We can't have a rogue agent running loose. 917 is a killer. Without control, there's no telling what he'll do."
"He's a killer," Spade said evenly. "But he isn't evil. He kills because we make him."
The Director picked up the Walther and weighed it in his palm. For a moment he imagined flipping off the safety and emptying the magazine into the glass, letting Yukon wind rip through the office.
The fantasy passed.
He set the pistol down.
This required control
.
"You know we can't sit idle," he said. "Two agents are dead. 917 is running around with a broken implant. Do we know who attacked him?"
Spade hesitated. She'd been combing LAPD systems and forensic reports. The body was in the morgue. Identification had stalled.
But she knew.
"A detective shot her before she finished the job. Multiple gunshot wounds, abdomen and chest. No fingerprints."
A few seconds of silence passed. The Director said nothing. Spade continued.
"She was one of ours, Robert."
The words landed like a hammer.
His mouth went dry.
"I dug through our archives," Spade continued. "Her name was Sofia Vega. We took her memory and turned her into Agent 048. Six months ago she disappeared in Chicago chasing an arms dealer. We lost implant contact. Presumed dead. Implants only go offline when the heart stops. We assumed the body was disposed of."
"So she survived," he said quietly.
Agents didn't die often. Missions were calculated. Resources vast. If they followed orders, they lived.
"I don't know what she's been doing these past six months," Spade said. "But she's dead now."
"That removes one problem." His tone flattened. "917 is the last loose end."
Spade didn't like the way he said it. As if agents were malfunctioning equipment.
"We bring him in," she said. "There are assets nearby which I can deploy. I'll continue scanning public surveillance and reroute satellite sweeps. It's a needle-in-a-haystack search, but needles prick eventually."
"If you can't bring him in," the Director said, "kill him."
The cold in his voice unsettled her.
"Robert, 917 isn't our enemy."
"Sofia wasn't either. Until she buried two agents. If you can't bring him in, terminate him. Understood?"
Silence stretched.
Reluctantly, she nodded even though he couldn't see it.
"All right, Robert. One way or another, the loose ends will be tied."
OceanofPDF.com

Book Two - Recall
OceanofPDF.com

19
M
otel rooms and mute colors calmed 917's mind.
He was on the verge of collapse, and the familiarity grounded him. Synthetic carpet beneath his toes. Scratchy plastic duvet under his fingertips. Popcorn ceiling overhead.
How many times had he woken in rooms like this with no memory, no past, only an encrypted phone instructing him to kill?
He'd asked himself that before every mission.
The answer always slipped away. The implant made sure of that. Now the implant was broken. The answers were rising.
Images surfaced; guns, knives, syringes, fire and ghosts clawing through fog.
Red neon numbers on the clock blinked past midnight. His body was spent, but his mind hummed like a tuned engine. He moved from the bed to the stiff chair in the corner. Springs pressed through thin padding, but he barely felt it. The journal consumed him.
The words seemed to twist on the page, mocking him.
The dime-sized implant buried near his pituitary sat inert. The artificial walls of amnesia collapsed. That strange cascade of code on the hotel TV had shattered the device with science he didn't understand.
Sensation flooded back. Emotion. Fragments
.
A pressure pulsed half an inch behind his forehead, making focus nearly impossible.
He was changing.
Reverting.
Who was I before this?
Was I good?
Was I decent?
Metamorphosis.
Caterpillars entered cocoons shriveled and blind, emerged transformed. But what if what waited on the other side wasn't beautiful?
The amnesia had held something back. Now, that darkness seeped forward unchecked. Memories flickered like a strobe. Out of order. Violent. Disjointed. His mind was fractured. But the instincts remained.
Before locking the door, he'd wrapped a glass in a towel and crushed it with the heel of his boot. Then, he scattered the shards across the threshold. The door was thin. Hard soles would crunch. Crude alarm system, but enough.
The Beretta M9 rested on the bedside table beneath a dim lamp.
Part of him longed for the sound of breaking glass; for the recoil of firing a weapon. Bullets punching through wood and flesh before anyone could get the jump on him.
They erased his memory after every mission, but the certainty that he was built for violence was always present.
Why else did the weight of a handgun feel like reassurance?
Is that why they wiped my memory? Because deep down I'm no different from the men I hunt?
Reflection could wait. The Agency was hunting him.
In the morning, he'd move again. Stillness got you caught. Movement kept you alive.
The journal felt heavy in his hands.
He was ready.
"You will know the truth, and the truth will set you free." John 8:32.
The verse echoed inside him.
He saw himself younger, ten years maybe more, sitting in a blue wingback chair with a worn Bible resting in his hands. Steam rose from a mug of coffee on the side table. His father's Bible. Leather soft from years of use. He remembered running a thumb over the cover and thinking about the cow that had died to bind it.
His father hadn't left much behind. A dying car. A stack of old books.
The Bible was the only thing worth keeping.
Pages thin as tissue. He'd turned them with the same precision he used to swap a magazine in a firearm.
He wanted to keep digging through his memory, but if he fell too far into the past, he'd lose the present. And he needed the present to survive.
He looked down at the first line of the journal.
I am more than a number.
For years, I have been Agent 048. But I have a name. They took it from me. Now it's back.
My name is Sofia Vega.
And I will have my vengeance, in this life or the next.

20
S
pade cracked her knuckles and began typing.
Earlier, she'd smashed her sleek Bluetooth keyboard in frustration. The Agency system had been breached. 917's implant was disabled. Powerless was a foreign sensation, and when it hit her, she lashed out.
"Violence is the last refuge of the incompetent."
Isaac Asimov said that. She'd devoured his books about fantastic robots and artificial intelligence as a girl. Now she was living inside one of those stories.
Fiction had shaped her. High concepts bled into reality. Cell phones. AI. Driverless cars. Virtual worlds. Writers dreamed them first. People like her built them after. Without science fiction, the Agency might never have existed. Radical ideas had to be imagined before they could be engineered.
After breaking the keyboard, she'd pulled an old relic from the closet, an IBM plastic brick from the '90s. Boxy. Loud. The mechanical clatter beneath her fingers sounded like distant gunfire.
The Director wanted 917 dead. No loose ends. No rogue agents. End it now.
It was already messy. An LAPD detective was tangled in the fallout. Public eyes were watching. Three dead operatives in as many days, one on a beach in Los Angeles, one in the Arizona desert, and now a woman shot by Detective Andrews.
There was no trail leading back to them. The Agency erased identities with surgical precision. No fingerprints. No records. No databases. Not even the NSA could trace them.
Months ago, she'd slipped into the NSA's back door and tested there systems herself. The NSA didn't have the slightest idea the Agency existed. The implants self-destructed at death, synced to vital signs. When the heart stopped, an acid capsule dissolved the device within minutes. No coroner would ever recover one.
Still, attention was unacceptable. The Agency survived in shadow.
Spade pulled up a directory of every Agency asset on the West Coast. Seven in range. Four dormant. Three active, hunting rapists and killers. Best not to disrupt those missions. Four would be enough. One could reach Los Angeles within hours. The other three by tomorrow
.
This wasn't the first time the Director had ordered 917 terminated. She'd defied him before, brought 917 home, repaired him, reset him. But the implant had always been intact. The implant made him manageable. Now it was gone.
There was no reset switch for a free mind.
She issued directives. The four available agents were dispatched to 917's last known location. He'd abandoned the Agency SUV near a bus station. One agent would inspect it for clues. It was unlikely he'd left any, but procedure demanded the attempt.
As the assets converged, a memory surfaced.
A year ago, 917 had taken a round to the stomach. Motel room. Blood loss. Minutes from death. Nanobots injected into his bloodstream had rebuilt tissue from the inside out.
Her mind sparked.
The implant was gone. But the nanobots might still be active. They didn't carry GPS, but they synced wirelessly with Agency devices if they came within range. Crude, almost primitive. Encrypted Bluetooth.
Her vision blurred. She pressed her fingers to her temples. She hadn't slept in over twenty-four hours.
Cold tea sat on her desk. The motor of her wheelchair hummed as she rolled into the kitchen and started a fresh pot of coffee.
Fatigue bred mistakes. She couldn't afford mistakes. Caffeine would have to do.
